On the structural properties of Lie algebras via associated labeled directed graphs



Tim Heib ID



Email: t.heib@fz-juelich.de

Affiliation: Institute for Quantum Computing Analytics (PGI-12), Forschungszentrum Jülich, 52425 Jülich, Germany

Affiliation: Theoretical Physics, Universität des Saarlandes, 66123 Saarbrücken, Germany

  
David Edward Bruschi ID



Email: david.edward.bruschi@posteo.net

Email: d.e.bruschi@fz-juelich.de

October 20, 2026


Abstract
 

We present a method for associating labeled directed graphs to finite-dimensional Lie algebras that are graph-admissible. The proposed framework enables visual identification of key structural algebraic features. We first formalize this approach and establish several classes of Lie algebras that admit the categorization as graph-admissible Lie algebras, and then investigate suitable valid graphs for this approach by translating the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket and the Jacobi identity into rules that such graphs must satisfy. Furthermore, based on these foundations, we provide graph-theoretic criteria for solvability, nilpotency, presence of ideals, simplicity, semisimplicity, and reductiveness of an algebra. We also present practical algorithms for constructing such graphs and those associated with the lower central series and derived series via iterative pruning procedures. We further cover specific examples, such as the Schrödinger and Lorentz algebras, which illustrate the applicability of the developed tools to physically relevant cases. Moreover, we explore applications in physics, where the method facilitates computation of similarity transformations essential for determining quantum mechanical time evolution via the Lie-algebraic factorization method. We also discuss a possible generalization of the framework based on magma-gradings of Lie algebras and formulate related open questions. Our approach bridges algebraic and combinatorial perspectives, offering theoretical tools for this area of mathematical physics.
 




Introduction




Lie algebras are fundamental objects in mathematics and theoretical physics that arise naturally in the study of continuous symmetries [135, 94], differential equations [151], and quantum mechanical systems, where they are ubiquitous [187, 110, 173, 42, 99, 88, 101]. Their structural properties, such as simplicity, solvability, or nilpotency, play a crucial role in classification theory and representation theory [126], and find applications across diverse fields, including control theory [40], the theory of differential equations [179, 180], continuous variable quantum information [2], or particle physics [89]. Thus, understanding and visualizing these properties is not only essential for gaining theoretical insight and easing computational algorithms, but also helpful for conceptual purposes, since visual representations can facilitate understanding of the structures of interest. Here, we aim to construct a visual representation by associating appropriate labeled directed graphs to Lie algebras.




The use of graphs to represent mathematical structures is a well-established and powerful technique. Graphs have long served as tools for encoding algebraic and combinatorial relationships, with notable examples including Feynman diagrams in quantum field theory [81], Cayley graphs for groups [156], and weight space diagrams in representation theory [94]. More recently, graph-based methods have been employed to classify Pauli Lie algebras [3], describe quantum electrical circuits [55], or to determine the controllability of arrays of coupled qubits [87]. Graphs also serve as effective visualization tools for complex concepts, such as the depiction of unitary irreducible representations for certain Lie algebras related to the symmetries of the Schrödinger equation [26], or the Fano plane and its connection to the multiplication table of specific Lie algebras [60].




This work introduces a framework for associating certain finite-dimensional Lie algebras with labeled directed graphs. The goal is not to produce a unique or basis-independent representation, but rather to construct a graph that reflects aspects of the internal structure of a given Lie algebra in a way that renders its properties visually accessible. Notably, the construction presented here is neither unique nor basis-independent: the same graph may, in certain cases, correspond to two distinct Lie algebras and, conversely, a single Lie algebra may be associated with multiple distinct graphs. Nevertheless, the proposed method enables the identification of solvable and nilpotent Lie algebras, the detection of ideals, and the formulation of graph-theoretic criteria for simplicity, semisimplicity, and reductiveness, thus highlighting the fact that Lie algebras that share the same graph also share similar structural properties.




The connection between graphs and Lie algebras has been explored in various contexts and through diverse implementations. Complex semisimple Lie algebras are, for example, traditionally classified using graphical tools such as Dynkin diagrams, which correspond to specific root systems [109]. This approach has been generalized to real semisimple Lie algebras using Satake diagrams [103].




An association between Lie algebras and graphs, more closely related to the idea presented here, has been introduced before [62]. In this case, one can construct unlabeled and undirected graphs and associate them with two-step nilpotent Lie algebras. This approach has been adopted [155, 8, 24] and extended [52, 145] in further work to incorporate labeled and directed edges. In this extended framework, vertices of the graph are identified with basis elements of a Lie algebra, and edges between two vertices are labeled with the result of the corresponding Lie bracket; the direction of the edge encodes the sign of the bracket. One advantage of this construction over the method presented here is that the labels of the edges do not have to coincide with the vertex elements. This approach has also been generalized to certain three-step nilpotent Lie algebras using Schreier graphs [160], and extended to solvable Lie algebras [93]. It is, however, important to note that this is not the only effort to associate nilpotent Lie algebras with graphs; alternative constructions have been also pursued elsewhere [69, 175].




Another approach that explores the connection between graphs and Lie algebras begins with a simple graph and attempts to associate a Lie algebra to it [90, 120]. This is done in order to classify these Lie algebras, which are denoted with the ambiguous term “graph Lie algebras”. However, they should not be confused with “graph algebras” in the sense of [158], where one isn’t concerned with Lie algebras but with C^{*} -algebras, or graph algebras as discussed in [121]. Moreover, the term “graph Lie algebra” has been used in the literature in various other contexts [74, 33], further contributing to the ambiguity in terminology. In contrast, the present work starts with a graph-admissible Lie algebra given a priori and constructs a graph in a consistent manner that encodes its bracket structure. This approach is therefore more closely related to the visualization technique utilized in [34], where basis elements are represented as vertices, and the Lie bracket operations are visualized using lines that start at two distinct vertices and join at the element that results from performing the corresponding Lie bracket. However, that method is restricted to Engel-type algebras only, which are a subclass of nilpotent Lie algebras.




To address and overcome the limitations of the aforementioned approaches, we propose a construction based on labeled directed graphs. In this framework, the vertices of the graph represent a spanning set of the Lie algebra. The starting vertex of an edge corresponds to the element acting via the adjoint action, the edge-label indicates the input element of the adjoint action (also taken from the vertex set), and the target vertex represents the result of the bracket, up to a non-zero proportionality constant. This construction is conceptually similar to the one considered in [37], although that approach employs unlabeled edges, is restricted to Lie algebras over finite fields, and determines all actions via the same adjoint actions of a single external element not represented as a vertex. Our idea is also inspired by commutativity graphs, which connect elements of a Lie algebra whenever their bracket is non-zero [178], and the commutator graphs discussed in [186], which - similar to [37] - link elements based on the adjoint action of a specific element from the Lie algebra. It is important to note, however, that the term “commutator graph” is not used uniformly in the literature. In some contexts, it refers to graphs that connect elements which commute [153, 129], i.e. whose Lie bracket would vanish - effectively the opposite of the commutativity graph discussed in [178]. These constructions are among the closest relatives to the framework presented here. Our approach, therefore, can be seen as a direct adaptation of the idea of commutator graphs developed in [119], although generalized from strings of Pauli operators to arbitrary finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebras.




Other intriguing approaches exist that investigate the interplay between graphs and Lie algebras, such as the idea presented in [141], where one incorporates the concept of Graph Grammars, originally developed in the field of theoretical computer science, into the construction of Lie algebraic structures, or the one studied in [44], where the combinatorial structures are the main focus.




This work is organized as follows: In Section I, we recall the essential definitions and conventions pertaining to Lie algebra and graph theory. In Section II, we introduce the central concept of graph-admissibility, which serves to characterize whether a given Lie algebra can be faithfully represented by a finite labeled directed graph. Section III establishes criteria for determining the validity of such graph representations. In Section IV, we examine key structural properties of Lie algebras through their associated graphs, including the identification of central elements, the computation of the derived and lower central series, the detection of ideals, and the formulation of criteria for simplicity and semisimplicity. Section V presents applications of this approach to prominent examples, such as the Schrödinger algebra and the Lorentz algebra, and discusses applications to the classification of finite-dimensional Lie algebras of physical relevance. Section VI discusses limitations and possible extensions, and outlines a series of open questions and conjectures that emerge from the graph-theoretic framework. Finally, Section VII offers some concluding remarks.


Figure 1: Schematic overview of the workflow presented in this paper. We consider finite-dimensional Lie algebras and begin by introducing the notion of graph-admissibility. Based on this concept, we construct labeled directed graphs associated with these algebras. Subsequently, we develop graph-theoretical tools, such as the lower central series and the derived series for graphs, in order to analyze structural properties. These tools enable the formulation of classification criteria for Lie algebras using graph-theoretical methods. Finally, we consider some specific examples and discuss possible extensions of these methods.





I Background concepts




We begin by recalling the most essential definitions relevant to this work, as well as introducing the conventions used. This encompasses several definitions for classifying Lie algebras and graphs. In addition, we adopt the following conventions: We denote any field with the symbol \mathbb{F} , and the set \mathbb{F} without the zero element as \mathbb{F}^{*}:=\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\} . Unless explicitly stated otherwise, all fields considered in this work are assumed to have characteristic zero. Moreover, we assume that \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})\neq 2 holds for all fields discussed. We also use the symbol “ \propto ” to express a proportionality relation of two vectors. However, we emphasize that we write x\propto y if and only if there exists a constant \kappa\in\mathbb{F}^{*} such that x=\kappa y for two vectors x,y in a vector space V over the field \mathbb{F} . Consequently, the only vector proportional to the zero vector is the zero vector itself.




I.1 Fundamental concepts of Lie algebras




In this subsection we introduce the foundational definitions and algebraic structures that underpin the study of Lie algebras. These include the Lie bracket, the center, and key series used to classify Lie algebras based on their structural properties. These definitions will serve as the backbone for the associated graphs in the later sections.



Definition 1 (Lie algebra).



A Lie algebra is a vector space \mathfrak{g} over a field \mathbb{F} equipped with an antisymmetric bilinear binary operation [\;\cdot\;,\;\cdot\;]:\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g} that satisfies the Jacobi identity




 \displaystyle[x,[y,z]]+[y,[z,x]]+[z,[x,y]]=0 

(1)


for all x,y,z\in\mathfrak{g} . This operation is called the Lie bracket [126, 110].





Such algebras can also be regarded as inner product spaces when equipped with an appropriate scalar product. For instance, one can use the scalar product defined on the basis elements by \langle x_{j},x_{k}\rangle=\delta_{jk} , in case of a finite basis, where \delta_{jk} denotes the Kronecker delta.




Remark: The commutator as Lie bracket


In physics, the term Lie bracket is often used interchangeably with the commutator [14]. For example, for square matrices, the commutator is defined as




 [\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]:\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\times\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}\to\mathbb{C}^{n\times n},\,(\boldsymbol{M}_{1},\boldsymbol{M}_{2})\mapsto[\boldsymbol{M}_{1},\boldsymbol{M}_{2}]:=\boldsymbol{M}_{1}\boldsymbol{M}_{2}-\boldsymbol{M}_{2}\boldsymbol{M}_{1}. 


However, the Lie bracket is not restricted to this operation. Other bilinear operations also satisfy the axioms of a Lie bracket. A notable example is the vector cross product [143], which, according to [13], is defined by




 \times:\mathbb{C}^{3}\times\mathbb{C}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}^{3},(v=v^{j}e_{j},w=w^{j}e_{j})\mapsto v\times w={\varepsilon_{jk}}^{\ell}v^{j}w^{k}e_{\ell}, 


where we employ Einstein’s summation convention [78]. Here, e_{j} denotes the canonical basis vectors of \mathbb{C}^{3} and \varepsilon_{jk\ell} is the completely antisymmetric Levi-Civita symbol [13]. This example illustrates that the Lie bracket concept extends beyond commutators to other algebraic structures.





We are interested in exploring the structural properties of a given Lie algebra. A basic example of such a structural property is the center of the Lie algebra.



Definition 2 (Center).



Let \mathfrak{g} be a Lie algebra. The center of \mathfrak{g} is defined as the set \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) of all elements in \mathfrak{g} whose Lie bracket with all other elements in the algebra vanishes identically. That is




 \displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}):=C_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{g}):=\{x\in\mathfrak{g}\,\mid\,[x,y]=0\text{ for all }y\in\mathfrak{g}\}, 

(2)


where C_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathfrak{g}) is the centralizer of \mathfrak{g} in \mathfrak{g} [126]. If \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{g} , we say that \mathfrak{g} is abelian [126].





Other structural properties of Lie algebras can be studied by considering, for example, the derived series or the lower central series. These are generally defined by the operation [\mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathfrak{h}_{2}]:=\spn\{[h_{1},h_{2}]|\;h_{1}\in\mathfrak{h}_{1},\,h_{2}\in\mathfrak{h}_{2}\} , where \mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathfrak{h}_{2}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} are two subalgebras of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} [126]. It is clear that [\mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathfrak{h}_{2}] is itself a vector space but not necessarily a Lie algebra.



Definition 3 (Derived series).



The derived series (\mathcal{D}^{k}\mathfrak{g})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}} of a Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , also known as the commutator series [126], consists of the algebras constructed by the following recursive procedure




 \displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{k+1}\mathfrak{g}:=[\mathcal{D}^{k}\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{D}^{k}\mathfrak{g}]\quad\text{for all }k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{D}^{0}\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{g}. 

(3)


The algebras \mathcal{D}^{k}\mathfrak{g} are furthermore denoted as the k -th derived algebra [170].





In addition to the derived series, we are also interested in the lower central series, which is defined through a similar recursive construction.



Definition 4 (Lower central series).



The lower central series (\mathcal{C}^{k}\mathfrak{g})_{k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}} of a Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} [109], also known as the descending central series [170], is recursively defined by:




 \displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{k+1}\mathfrak{g}:=[\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{C}^{k}\mathfrak{g}]\quad\text{for all }k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{C}^{0}\mathfrak{g}:=\mathfrak{g}. 

(4)






These series — the derived and lower central series — are fundamental tools to classify Lie algebras according to their structural properties, namely solvability and nilpotency of a Lie algebra.



Definition 5 (Solvable and Nilpotent algebra).



A Lie algebra is solvable if its derived series terminates after a finite number of steps; i.e., there exists an integer k_{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} such that \mathcal{D}^{k_{*}}\mathfrak{g}=\{0\} , where \{0\} denotes the trivial Lie algebra.
Similarly, a Lie algebra is nilpotent if the lower central series terminates after a finite number of steps; i.e., there exists an integer k_{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , such that \mathcal{C}^{k_{*}}\mathfrak{g}=\{0\} . In the case of nilpotent algebras, the smallest such integer is referred to as the index of the Lie algebra.





Note that the notion of nilpotency is analogous to the nilpotency of linear maps, as formalized by Engel’s theorem [109]. It is evident that \mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} and \mathcal{D}^{k}\mathfrak{g}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{k}\mathfrak{g} for all k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , which shows that every nilpotent Lie algebra is also solvable, although the converse does not hold: there exist solvable Lie algebras that are not nilpotent such as the two-dimensional affine Lie algebra \mathfrak{aff}(\mathbb{F}) [11].




Another important concept in the study and classification of Lie algebras is that of an ideal. The ideal plays a central role in defining and understanding structural notions such as “simplicity”, “semisimplicity”, and the “radical” of a Lie algebra.



Definition 6 (Ideal).



Let \mathfrak{g} be a Lie algebra. An ideal \mathfrak{h} in \mathfrak{g} is a subspace \mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} satisfying [\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}]\subseteq\mathfrak{h} [126].





Typical examples of ideals include the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) of a Lie algebra, the first derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} , or the kernel of a Lie-algebra homomorphism [126].




A particularly important ideal in the structure theory of Lie algebras is the radical, which captures the largest solvable part of a Lie algebra.



Definition 7 (Radical).



Let \mathfrak{g} be a Lie algebra. The radical of \mathfrak{g} , denoted \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{g}) , is defined as the largest solvable ideal of \mathfrak{g} [126].





The radical of a given Lie algebra exists and is unique, as guaranteed by Proposition 1.12 from [126]. We now define the notions of simple and semisimple Lie algebras, which play a pivotal role in the structural classification of finite-dimensional Lie algebras. A complete classification of these Lie algebras over the fields of the real and complex numbers has been established [50, 18, 19, 20, 21, 139]. Moreover, it is known that any finite-dimensional Lie algebra over a field of characteristic zero can be expressed as a semidirect sum of a semisimple Lie algebra and its radical. This is known as the Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition [130]. These results form the foundation for understanding the decomposition and internal structure of general Lie algebras.



Definition 8 (Simple algebra, Semisimple algebra).



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra. This algebra is simple if it is non-abelian and contains no proper non-zero ideals. If \mathfrak{g} has no non-zero solvable ideals, i.e., \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{g})=\{0\} , we say that the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is semisimple.





It follows immediately that every simple Lie algebra is also semisimple. The final structural classification of Lie algebras we want to discuss is that of reductive versus non-reductive Lie algebras. This distinction generalizes the concept of semisimplicity by allowing for a non-trivial center, while still retaining a semisimple component.



Definition 9 (Reductive algebra).



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra. The algebra \mathfrak{g} is a reductive algebra if to each ideal \mathfrak{i}_{1} in \mathfrak{g} there exists a corresponding ideal \mathfrak{i}_{2} in \mathfrak{g} such that \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{i}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{i}_{2} .





Note that if \mathfrak{g} is reductive, then \mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) , where \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} is semisimple [126].





I.2 Graphs




A labeled directed graph G(V,E) is defined by an ordered tuple (V,E) , where V is a set of vertices and E is a collection of labeled directed edges. Each edge is a three-tuple e=(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}}) , where the first entry v_{\mathrm{s}} denotes the starting vertex (i.e., the vertex from which the edge originates), the second entry v_{\mathrm{l}} is the vertex that labels the edge, and the third entry v_{\mathrm{e}} is the vertex at which the edge terminates (i.e., the vertex to which the edge points to). Accordingly, we associate with every graph G(V,E) the three functions \varpi_{\mathrm{s}},\varpi_{\mathrm{l}},\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}:E\to V , which map each edge e\in E to a vertex from V , corresponding to its starting vertex, labeling vertex, and ending vertex respectively. Concretely, we have




 \displaystyle\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}:e\equiv(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}}) 
 \displaystyle\mapsto\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e):=v_{\mathrm{s}},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}:e\equiv(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}}) 
 \displaystyle\mapsto\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e):=v_{\mathrm{l}},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}:e\equiv(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}}) 
 \displaystyle\mapsto\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e):=v_{\mathrm{e}}. 

(5)


It is worth noting that, in the literature, a labeled directed graph G(V,E) is also defined as the triple G(V,E,\varphi) , where V denotes the set of vertices, E the set of edges (typically consisting of tuples containing only the starting and ending vertices), and the label of each edge is determined by a corresponding labeling function \varphi [145]. In some contexts, the function \varphi is also used to describe the direction of a given edge [27]. Our approach differs from the ones just mentioned.




We now introduce the notions of “walk”, “path”, “trail”, “circuit”, and “cycle”. Since our focus is exclusively on directed graphs, we restrict ourselves to their directed counterparts.



Definition 10.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph. Let S:=(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},e_{2},\ldots,e_{s},v_{s+1}) be a finite, non-trivial sequence of vertices \{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s+1}\subseteq V and edges \{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s}\subseteq E . In line with the literature [27], we define the following:

(i) 



If \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j})=v_{j} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{j})=v_{j+1} for all j\in\{1,\ldots,s\} , we call S a directed walk.

(ii) 



If S is a directed walk and |\{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s+1}|=s+1 , then S is a directed path.

(iii) 



If S is a directed walk and |\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s}|=s , then S is a directed trail.

(iv) 



If S is a directed walk and v_{1}=v_{s+1} , then S is a closed directed walk.

(v) 



If S is a directed trail that is also a closed directed walk, then S is a directed circuit.

(vi) 



If S is a directed circuit and S^{\prime}=(v_{1},e_{1},\ldots,e_{s-1},v_{s}) is a directed path, then S is a cycle.



The length of S is defined as \operatorname{len}(S):=s .





In certain contexts, it is more convenient to consider the subgraphs induced by sequences of vertices and edges, rather than the sequences themselves.



Definition 11 (Induced graphs).



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph, and let S:=(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},\ldots,e_{s},v_{s+1}) be a non-trivial sequence of vertices \{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s+1}\subseteq V and edges \{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s}\subseteq E that satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j}),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{j})\in\{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s+1} . The graph induced by S is defined as G(\{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s+1},\{e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s})=:G_{S} .





Since we are exclusively concerned with labeled directed graphs, it is furthermore useful to introduce the concept of “self-contained subgraphs”, which are subgraphs, where the labels of the edges are only given by the vertices of the subgraph itself.



Definition 12.



[Self-contained subgraph]
Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph, and let G_{S}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E})\subseteq G(V,E) be a subgraph induced by a non-trivial sequence S of vertices and edges. The subgraph G_{S} is self-contained if and only if \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\tilde{V} for all edges e\in\tilde{E} .





Note that a self-contained subgraph induced by a directed walk W does not necessarily imply that W is a closed directed walk. For instance, consider the directed walk W=(v_{1},e_{1}=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{2}),v_{2}) : it induces a self-contained subgraph but is not closed.




Finally, we introduce the concept of unconnected subgraphs of a directed graph, which, as shown in Proposition 30 and Lemma 48, is closely related to the direct sums of the associated Lie algebras.



Definition 13.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph, and let G(V_{1},E_{1}),G(V_{2},E_{2}) be two subgraphs of G(V,E) . These subgraphs are unconnected if V_{1}\cap V_{2}=\emptyset and, for all v_{1}\in V_{1} and v_{2}\in V_{2} there exists no edge e\in E such that either \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v_{1} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v_{2} , or vice versa.





Note that unconnected subgraphs are closely related to the concept of disconnected components. These are unconnected subgraphs of a given graph G(V,E) that do not contain further unconnected subgraphs themselves and are not subgraphs of any larger unconnected subgraph [35, 96].



Definition 14 (Equivalent graphs).



Let G(V_{1},E_{1}) and G(V_{2},E_{2}) be two labeled directed graphs. These two graphs are isomorphic if and only if there exists a bijection \varphi:V_{1}\to V_{2} , such that E_{2}=\{(\varphi(\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)),\varphi(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)),\varphi(\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)))\;\mid\;e\in E_{1}\} . We call the mapping \varphi an isomorphism and write, in the case of isomorphic graphs, G(V_{1},E_{1})\cong G(V_{2},E_{2}) [115]. We say two graphs are equivalent if they are isomorphic.







II Formulation of the Central idea




This section lays the groundwork for the graph-theoretic framework developed in this paper. It introduces the core concept of graph-admissibility, which classifies finite-dimensional Lie algebras according to whether they can be associated with a finite labeled directed graph. The first part of this section formalizes this notion, while the second examines the concrete construction of graphs that encode the structural properties of such Lie algebras.




II.1 Graph-admissible Lie algebras




Let us begin by introducing a specific classification of n -dimensional Lie algebras. This classification is central to the graph-based approach developed in this work, as it determines whether a Lie algebra can be associated with a finite labeled directed graph in a way that preserves its structural properties.



Definition 15 (Graph-admissible Lie algebra).



Let \mathfrak{g} be an n -dimensional Lie algebra. We say that \mathfrak{g} is minimal-graph-admissible if it admits a basis \{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n} such that the Lie bracket satisfies:




 \displaystyle[x_{j},x_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=\alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)}\quad\quad\text{ for all }j,k\in\mathcal{N}:=\{1,\ldots,n\}, 

(6)


where \boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n} is an antisymmetric matrix and \delta:\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N}\to\mathcal{N} is a symmetric function with concrete action \delta:(j,k)\mapsto\delta(j,k)=j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N} that maps pairs of indices to another index.




If \mathfrak{g} does not necessarily admit such a basis satisfying the relations (6), but instead an overcomplete basis \{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m} of non-zero elements with m\geq n satisfying




 \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)}\quad\quad\text{ for all }j,k\in\mathcal{M}:=\{1,\ldots,m\},\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\tilde{x}_{j}\propto\tilde{x}_{k}\quad\quad\text{if and only if }j=k, 

(7)


where \tilde{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times m} is an antisymmetric matrix and \delta:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M} is a symmetric function on the space of indices as defined above, then we call \mathfrak{g} redundant-graph-admissible.




Any finite-dimensional Lie algebra that is either minimal-graph-admissible or redundant-graph-admissible is called graph-admissible. Finally, we say that an algebra is non-graph-admissible if it is not graph-admissible.




To ensure consistency and for later convenience, we define \delta(j,k):=0 whenever \alpha_{jk}=0 (or \tilde{\alpha}_{jk}=0 respectively), and set x_{0}:=0=:\tilde{x}_{0} . We also set \alpha_{j0}:=0=:\alpha_{0j} for j\in\mathcal{N}\cup\{0\} , and analogously \tilde{\alpha}_{j0}:=0=:\tilde{\alpha}_{0j} for j\in\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\} . Moreover, we set \delta(j,0)=0=\delta(0,j) for all j\in\mathcal{N} or j\in\mathcal{M} , respectively. This allows us to extend the domain of \delta to include zero: \delta:\mathcal{N}\cup\{0\}\times\mathcal{N}\cup\{0\}\to\mathcal{N}\cup\{0\} or \delta:\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\}\times\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\}\to\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\} if \mathfrak{g} is minimal-graph-admissible or respectively redundant-graph-admissible.





Note that in the more general setting of (anti)commutative algebras, a basis that satisfies the conditions of Definition 15 for minimal-graph-admissibility is called multiplicative [48, 46]. Multiplicative bases are a well-established concept occurring for example in Lie algebra set gradings [47] and in the study of Lie algebras with nice bases [68].



Remark.



Minimal-graph-admissibility, redundant-graph-admissibility, graph-admissibility, and non-graph-admissibility are intrinsic properties of a Lie algebra. Indeed, these properties are defined by the existence, or non-existence, of a suitable basis or finite overcomplete spanning set satisfying the defining relation (6) or (7) of the definition above. Their validity therefore does not depend on an initially chosen basis. This should not be confused with basis independence of the associated graph: not every basis or overcomplete spanning set is suitable, and different suitable choices for the same Lie algebra may, in fact, produce non-isomorphic labeled directed graphs. In this respect, graph-admissibility is analogous to diagonalizability: the existence or non-existence of a suitable basis is intrinsic to a given vector-space endomorphism, whereas the concrete representation obtained after choosing such a basis need not be unique [138].





The condition of graph-admissibility is restrictive; however, it has a powerful consequence: it ensures that the Lie bracket of any two basis elements is proportional to a third basis element, thus allowing us to encode the algebra as a graph where the vertices represent basis elements and the edges represent the bracket relations of these basis elements. The classification of graph-admissible and non-graph-admissible Lie algebras is therefore crucial because it determines whether a Lie algebra can be faithfully represented as a graph. Naturally, the first question that arises is:


Q: Do there exist Lie algebras that can be uniquely classified as minimal-graph-admissible, redundant-graph-admissible but not minimal-graph-admissible, or non-graph-admissible?





As we will demonstrate later, the answer to this question is “yes”. To begin addressing it, we first show that there exist both minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras and Lie algebras that are not minimal-graph-admissible. This assertion is formalized in the following theorem, which is proven by providing concrete examples.



Theorem 16.



There exist finite-dimensional Lie algebras that are minimal-graph-admissible and ones that are not minimal-graph-admissible.





A detailed proof of this result is provided in Appendix B. The one-dimensional abelian Lie algebra provides a trivial example of a minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra. To obtain Lie algebras that are not minimal-graph-admissible, we consider the family of real Lie algebras L_{\alpha}^{3}=\spn\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\} determined by the Lie brackets




 \displaystyle[x_{1},x_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{1},x_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=-x_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{2},x_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=-\alpha x_{1}-x_{2}, 

(8)


which occurs in the classification of low-dimensional Lie algebras [63]. As shown in Appendix B, the real Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} is not minimal-graph-admissible whenever \alpha<-1/4 . The obstruction is specific to the real base field and ultimately arises from the negative discriminant of the relevant quadratic equation and the fact that \mathbb{R} is not algebraically closed.




After establishing that not every finite-dimensional Lie algebra is minimal-graph-admissible, it is natural to ask whether there are Lie algebras that are graph-admissible in a more general sense, i.e., if there are redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebras that are not minimal-graph-admissible, which would justify the distinction of graph-admissible Lie algebras into these two classes. The following corollary addresses this question directly. It is, furthermore, noteworthy that by Definition 15 every Lie algebra that is minimal-graph-admissible is also redundant-graph-admissible.



Corollary 17.



There exist Lie algebras that are graph-admissible but not minimal-graph-admissible.





This corollary can be shown by a direct demonstration; a simple example is the real Lie algebra L_{-1}^{3} , a member of the family of Lie algebras considered in the proof of Theorem 16. A detailed derivation can be found in Appendix B. Nonetheless, it is worth recalling that the proof of Theorem 16 relies on the fact that the field of real numbers is not algebraically closed and therefore highlights that whether a Lie algebra is minimal-graph-admissible can depend on the field over which the Lie algebra is defined. This subtlety underscores the importance of distinguishing between minimal- and redundant-graph-admissibility.




After having seen that not all finite-dimensional Lie algebras are minimal-graph-admissible, it is important to recognize that this property is not exceptional. In fact, there exist important classes of Lie algebras that are minimal-graph-admissible, such as abelian Lie algebras or finite-dimensional subalgebras of the complex single-mode Weyl algebra.



Proposition 18.



Every finite-dimensional abelian Lie algebra is minimal-graph-admissible. Moreover, every basis of such a Lie algebra is suitable in the sense of Definition 15, and all corresponding minimal graphs are isomorphic.




Proof.



This follows immediately from Definition 15, since in an abelian Lie algebra, the Lie bracket of any two elements vanishes. Indeed, this argument applies to every basis. Hence, every corresponding minimal graph consists of n isolated vertices, and any two such graphs are isomorphic.
∎




Proposition 19.



Every finite-dimensional Lie subalgebra of the complex one-mode Weyl algebra A_{1} is minimal-graph-admissible.




Proof.



The classification of every non-abelian finite-dimensional Lie subalgebra of the one-mode complex Weyl algebra A_{1} is known [159], and any such Lie algebra is minimal-graph-admissible, which can be verified by delving into the examples given in [159]. The previous observation that any finite-dimensional abelian Lie algebra is minimal-graph-admissible concludes the proof.
∎





The same conclusion holds for every finite-dimensional complex Lie subalgebra of the universal enveloping algebra U(\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})) , as established by Corollary 34 in the cited work [102].




The previous proposition raises the question of whether every finite-dimensional Lie subalgebra of the complex n -mode Weyl algebra A_{n} is minimal-graph-admissible. This is a natural question, given the central role of the Weyl algebra in mathematical physics and the fact that its structure becomes increasingly rich as the number of modes increases [42]. That having been said, the case at hand here is subtle. While the one-mode Weyl algebra A_{1} is highly restrictive, allowing only certain types of Lie subalgebras [159], higher-mode Weyl algebras with n>1 admit a much broader class of finite-dimensional Lie subalgebras. This leads us to the following conjecture:



Conjecture 20.



There exist non-graph-admissible Lie algebras in the complex n -mode Weyl algebra A_{n} for all n\geq 2 .





This conjecture is motivated by several structural results from the literature: Theorem 4.2 in [113] implies that only rank-one semisimple Lie algebras can be faithfully realized in the complex Weyl algebra A_{1} of a single mode. It is furthermore known that the only semisimple Lie algebra that can be faithfully realized in A_{1} is the three-dimensional special linear Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}) [125]. This occurs because \mathbb{C} is an algebraically closed field with characteristic zero, and also because the centralizer C_{A_{1}}(x) of every non-central element x\in A_{1}\setminus\mathcal{Z}(A_{1}) is an abelian subalgebra of A_{1} [72], a property known as the abelian centralizer condition [99]. Note that \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}) is already the only simple Lie algebra of rank one over \mathbb{C} [28].




For n>1 , the situation changes dramatically. The abelian centralizer condition mentioned above no longer holds for n\geq 2 , and the structure of the Weyl algebra becomes much richer. In particular, the n -mode Weyl algebra may admit semisimple Lie algebras of rank greater than one as finite-dimensional subalgebras. This opens the possibility for more intricate finite-dimensional subalgebras, including those that are not minimal-graph-admissible or even graph-admissible.




Beyond the special cases already discussed, there are entire classes of Lie algebras for which graph-admissibility is always guaranteed. In particular, nilpotent Lie algebras, which play an important role in the structure theory of Lie algebras, control theory, representation theory, and geometric analysis [126, 145], always admit an appropriate basis or spanning set, as demanded by Definition 15, although they are not necessarily minimal-graph-admissible. The following proposition makes this precise:



Proposition 21.



Every finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is graph-admissible.




Proof.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra with basis \mathcal{B}=\{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n} . We construct now an (overcomplete) basis of \mathfrak{g} that satisfies the requirement of graph-admissibility, as follows: Start with the original basis \mathcal{B} . Then add for every pair (x_{j},x_{k})\in\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{B} the Lie bracket [x_{j},x_{k}] to \mathcal{B} if it is non-zero and not proportional to an element that is already in \mathcal{B} . These new elements necessarily belong to the first term of the lower central series \mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{g}=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}] . One iterates this now with higher-order Lie brackets. That is, for all x_{j} in the original basis, one considers the brackets of the form [x_{j},[x_{k},x_{\ell}]] and [[x_{j},x_{k}],[x_{p},x_{q}]] . If any such bracket is non-zero and not proportional to an element already in \mathcal{B} , one adds it to \mathcal{B} . One continuously repeats this process and adds at each step new non-zero brackets that are not already proportional to existing elements in \mathcal{B} . The crucial observation is that, at each stage, the newly added elements belong to the k -th algebra \mathcal{C}^{k}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series of \mathfrak{g} . Since \mathfrak{g} is nilpotent, there exists a positive integer k_{*} such that \mathcal{C}^{k}\mathfrak{g}=\{0\} for all k\geq k_{*} . This means that after finitely many steps, no non-zero elements can be generated by further Lie brackets, and the process terminates. The resulting (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B} is finite and satisfies the bracket relations (7) required for graph-admissibility (i.e., every non-zero Lie bracket of elements from the new \mathcal{B} is proportional to a basis element of \mathcal{B} now that it has been appropriately extended). Therefore, every finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra is graph-admissible.
∎





The property of graph-admissibility is not limited to Lie algebras over infinite fields or those with particular algebraic structures. In fact, it holds in a very general setting, including all finite-dimensional Lie algebras defined over a finite field \mathbb{F} . This is summarized in the following straightforward proposition:



Proposition 22.



Every finite-dimensional Lie algebra over a finite field \mathbb{F} is graph-admissible.




Proof.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional Lie algebra over a finite field \mathbb{F} . Since \dim(\mathfrak{g}):=n<\infty and |\mathbb{F}|<\infty , the algebra consists of finitely many elements \{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}} , where |\mathcal{J}|=|\mathbb{F}|^{n} , as can be shown by a simple induction on the dimension n . Now, consider an overcomplete spanning set formed by taking one representative from each non-zero element modulo scalar multiples. Now let us construct a subset \mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} using the following iterative procedure. Consider the empty set \mathcal{B}=\emptyset . Then, fix some ordering of the elements in \mathcal{J} . We can assume, without loss of generality that \mathcal{J}=\{1,\ldots,|\mathbb{F}|^{n}\} . Now we iterate over the elements in \mathcal{J} . That is, for each j\in\mathcal{J} in order we add x_{j} to \mathcal{B} if x_{j}\neq 0 and there is no x_{k}\in\mathcal{B} such that x_{j}\propto x_{k} . The resulting set \mathcal{B} is finite and spans \mathfrak{g} , since every non-zero element of \mathfrak{g} is proportional to an element of \mathcal{B} . Moreover, and crucially, for any two elements in this set, their Lie bracket is either zero or proportional to another element in \mathcal{B} . Therefore, the proportionality condition from Definition 15 is satisfied, making \mathfrak{g} at least redundant-graph-admissible and hence graph-admissible.
∎




Remark.



The spanning set utilized in the proof above contains (|\mathbb{F}|^{n}-1)/(|\mathbb{F}|-1) elements, one for each one-dimensional subspace of \mathfrak{g} . For fixed \mathbb{F} , its size therefore grows exponentially with n , making this construction generally impractical for visualization or computation. Nevertheless, it shows that any such Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is graph-admissible, and, in practice, a suitable spanning set with fewer terms should be constructed, if possible.





While the preceding results establish that several distinct classes of Lie algebras are graph-admissible, thereby demonstrating the broad applicability of the graph-admissibility framework, it is crucial to recognize that this property does not universally extend to all Lie algebras and its limitations have to be acknowledged: Not every finite-dimensional Lie algebra admits a basis or overcomplete generating set that satisfies the structural conditions required for a graph representation to be viable as defined in this work. Demonstrating the existence of such exceptions is essential as it delineates the boundaries of the graph-admissibility approach. The following theorem formalizes this observation and conclusively answers, along with Theorem 16 and Corollary 17, the introductory question of whether the classification into minimal-graph-admissible, redundant-graph-admissible, and non-graph-admissible Lie algebras is justified.



Theorem 23.



There exist finite-dimensional Lie algebras that are non-graph-admissible.





The theorem follows directly from Lemma 93, delegated to Appendix B. That lemma establishes that the real Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} , already used to establish Theorem 16 and Corollary 17, is graph-admissible if and only if




 \displaystyle\alpha>-\frac{1}{4}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\frac{1}{\pi}\arctan\left(\frac{\sqrt{|1+4\alpha|}}{1+2\sqrt{|\alpha|}}\right)\in\mathbb{Q}^{*}. 



For \alpha=-1/4 , neither condition is satisfied, because the displayed quotient equals zero and 0\notin\mathbb{Q}^{*} . Consequently, L_{-1/4}^{3} is a concrete finite-dimensional non-graph-admissible Lie algebra. The graph-admissibility obstruction at \alpha=-1/4 is a non-trivial Jordan block, which persists over every field of characteristic zero, even if it is closed, as the argument in Appendix B shows.




Despite the implications of Theorem 23, it is crucial to emphasize that the graph-admissibility framework remains applicable to a broad and structurally significant group of Lie algebras. In particular, the following result demonstrates that complex semisimple Lie algebras, which play a central role in the classification theory of Lie algebras, fall within the scope of graph-admissibility.



Theorem 24.



Every finite-dimensional semisimple Lie algebra over an algebraically closed field \mathbb{F} is graph-admissible.




Proof.



This is an immediate consequence of the classification of semisimple Lie algebras over algebraically closed fields [94, 84, 109]. Any such semisimple Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , associated to a set of r simple roots, is uniquely determined by 3r elements \mathcal{S}:=\{E_{\pm}^{(j)},H^{(j)}\}_{j=1}^{r} which satisfy the Chevalley-Serre relations:




 \displaystyle[H^{(j)},H^{(k)}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[H^{(j)},E_{\pm}^{(k)}] 
 \displaystyle=\pm A_{kj}E_{\pm}^{(k)},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[E_{+}^{(j)},E_{-}^{(k)}] 
 \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}H^{(j)}, 



as well as the Jacobi identity and the Serre conditions




 \displaystyle\left(\operatorname{ad}_{E_{\pm}^{(j)}}\right)^{1-A_{kj}}E_{\pm}^{(k)}=0\qquad\text{for }j,k\in\{1,\ldots,r\}\text{ with }j\neq k. 



Here \operatorname{ad}_{x}(y):=[x,y] denotes the adjoint action, \operatorname{ad}_{x}^{n}(y) is a short-hand-notation for n -chained brackets, i.e., \operatorname{ad}_{x}^{n}(y)=[x,[x,\ldots,[x,y]]] , and \boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{Z}^{r\times r} is the corresponding Cartan matrix [84]. The set \mathcal{S} generates the Lie algebra, i.e., \langle\mathcal{S}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}=\mathfrak{g} . Furthermore, the set \mathcal{S} can be extended to the set \mathcal{S}^{\prime} which includes all associated root vectors E_{\alpha} , not only those associated with simple roots. The additional root vectors can be obtained as non-zero iterated Lie brackets of the Chevalley generators, while the Serre conditions ensure that the number of these elements remain finite. Moreover, for every non-simple positive root \alpha\in\Phi^{+} , we add the Cartan element H_{\alpha}:=[E_{\alpha},E_{-\alpha}] , which need not be proportional to any of the simple Cartan generators H^{(j)} . Setting H_{\alpha_{j}}:=H^{(j)} for every simple root \alpha_{j} , the resulting set can be written as \mathcal{S}^{\prime}:=\{E_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Phi\}\cup\{H_{\alpha}\mid\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\} for the associated root system \Phi with positive roots belonging to \Phi^{+} . Because \Phi is finite [94], the extended set \mathcal{S}^{\prime} remains finite. It also spans \mathfrak{g} , since it contains a non-zero vector from every root space and the elements corresponding to the simple roots contain a basis of the Cartan subalgebra [109]. Furthermore, no two distinct elements of \mathcal{S}^{\prime} are proportional: root vectors associated with distinct roots belong to distinct root spaces, no root vector is proportional to an element of the Cartan subalgebra, and no two distinct positive coroots are proportional because the root system is reduced.




It remains to verify relation (7). For all \alpha,\beta\in\Phi^{+} and \gamma\in\Phi , the standard root-space relations imply




 \displaystyle[H_{\alpha},H_{\beta}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[H_{\alpha},E_{\gamma}] 
 \displaystyle=\gamma(H_{\alpha})E_{\gamma}. 



Moreover, for all \gamma,\delta\in\Phi ,




 \displaystyle[E_{\gamma},E_{\delta}]\in\left\{\begin{matrix}\spn\{E_{\gamma+\delta}\},&\gamma+\delta\in\Phi,\\
\spn\{H_{\gamma}\},&\delta=-\gamma\text{ and }\gamma\in\Phi^{+},\\
\spn\{H_{-\gamma}\},&\delta=-\gamma\text{ and }-\gamma\in\Phi^{+},\\
\{0\},&\gamma+\delta\notin\Phi\cup\{0\}.\end{matrix}\right. 



Hence, the Lie bracket of any two elements of \mathcal{S}^{\prime} either vanishes or is proportional to an element of \mathcal{S}^{\prime} . Therefore, the finite overcomplete spanning set \mathcal{S}^{\prime} satisfies relation (7), making \mathfrak{g} graph-admissible according to Definition 15.
∎




Remark.



Note that the preceding theorem is solely intended to establish the graph-admissibility of finite-dimensional semisimple Lie algebras. However, a more interesting question is whether every such Lie algebra is minimal-graph-admissible and, if not, which ones are and what distinguishes those from the ones that are not. This question has been partially addressed in the grading literature, where substantially stronger results have been presented. In particular, \mathfrak{sl}({n},{\mathbb{C}}) , \mathfrak{so}(n,\mathbb{C}) , and \mathfrak{sp}(2^{k},\mathbb{C}) for k\geq 1 admit gradings with one-dimensional homogeneous components [98, 95], and hence possess multiplicative bases, making them minimal-graph-admissible. The existence of such bases for all finite-dimensional semisimple Lie algebras is also stated in [48]; we do not address this stronger assertion here.





This result generalizes naturally to reductive Lie algebras:



Corollary 25.



Every finite-dimensional reductive Lie algebra over an algebraically closed field \mathbb{F} is graph-admissible.




Proof.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a reductive Lie algebra. Then \mathfrak{g}=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]\oplus\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) , where [\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}] is semisimple [126]. Due to Theorem 24, the semisimple part [\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}] admits a (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B} that satisfies (7). This (possibly overcomplete) basis can be extended to a (possibly overcomplete) basis of \mathfrak{g} , by adding elements from the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) , which are abelian. This implies that the extended basis satisfies (7), making \mathfrak{g} graph-admissible.
∎






II.2 Graphs associated with finite-dimensional Lie algebras




Having established that a wide range of finite-dimensional Lie algebras are graph-admissible, whilst also highlighting the restrictions to the definition given, it is natural to ask how one can visualize such algebras in a way that makes their structure transparent. The method described here provides a systematic procedure for associating a labeled directed graph to any graph-admissible Lie algebra, making it possible to “see” the Lie algebra’s properties at a glance.




Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra.
We provide the following method for visualization: First, choose a (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} (or \mathcal{B}=\{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} ) that satisfies relations (6) (respectively, (7)). Then, draw a vertex v_{j} for every basis element x_{j}\in\mathcal{B} in a two-dimensional plane and label it using the corresponding element, i.e., v_{j}=x_{j} 11
 1
 
 
 
 To improve the precision of the notation, it can be helpful to introduce the function \varrho_{\mathrm{v}}:V\to\mathfrak{g} that maps a vertex v\in V to the corresponding labeling basis element x_{j}\in\mathfrak{g} . Similarly, for K\in\{\mathrm{s},\mathrm{l},\mathrm{e}\} define the functions \varrho_{K}:E\to\mathfrak{g} , e\mapsto\varrho_{\mathrm{v}}(\varpi_{K}(e)) that map the starting vertex, labeling vertex, or end vertex of an edge e to the corresponding element in \mathfrak{g} . This definition would remove ambiguity in the interpretation of graph elements. However, for convenience, we choose to identify a vertex with its labeling element directly.. In the next step, one considers every pair of basis elements x_{j},x_{k}\in\mathcal{B} . If there exists an element x_{\ell}\in\mathcal{B} such that [x_{j},x_{k}]\propto x_{\ell} , one draws a directed edge from v_{j} to v_{\ell} , labeled by v_{k} . In other words, whenever [x,y]=\kappa z with \kappa\in\mathbb{F}^{*} , draw an edge from x to z labeled by y :
 x z y \hat{=} [x,y]=\kappa z with \kappa\in\mathbb{F}^{*} .


Thus, for any finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , we can associate the graph G(V,E) with \mathfrak{g} by identifying V=\mathcal{B} and E:=\{e=(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}})\,\mid\,e\in V^{\times 3}\,\wedge\,[v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}}]\propto v_{\mathrm{e}}\} . This construction can be concisely formalized as an algorithm for any graph-admissible Lie algebra, as shown in Algorithm 1. However, we want to note that here and in the subsequent pruning algorithms (Algorithms 2 and 3) an admissible (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B} and its bracket data are supplied. Finding such a set or deciding graph-admissibility for a general input Lie algebra is beyond the scope of this work and left for future work.


Algorithm 1 Algorithm for generating a labeled directed graph associated with a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} 



Data: A (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} of the finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , satisfying the Lie bracket relations (7).


Result: A labeled directed graph associated with the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} constructed with respect to the given basis \mathcal{B} .



 1
 V \leftarrow \mathcal{B} 
/* Initialize the vertex set with the basis \mathcal{B} */



 2
 E \leftarrow \emptyset 
/* Initialize an empty edge set */



 3
 foreach (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} with j<k do /* Add all relevant edges to edge set E */


   
 4
 if [\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)} with \tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0 then


      
 5
 E \leftarrow E\cup\{(\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\} 


      
 6
 E \leftarrow E\cup\{(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\} 
/* Add edge to edge set E ; each edge is an ordered triple: (start vertex, edge-label, end vertex) */



 7
 return G(V,E) /* Return labeled directed graph G(V,E) associated with \mathfrak{g} */









Remark.



As required by the input data of Algorithm 1, it is essential to consider only (possibly overcomplete) bases \mathcal{B} that satisfy the relations (6) (resp. relations (7)). Thus, for every pair of x,y\in\mathcal{B} , one must have either [x,y]=0 or [x,y]\propto z for some z\in\mathcal{B} . Otherwise the algorithm does not produce an associated graph in the sense of Definitions 15 and 26, because the Lie bracket of two vertex elements is not proportional to at most one other vertex. Consequently, the structural criteria, for example for solvability or nilpotency, developed later will generally not apply. Allowing finite overcomplete bases is important in this context, since additional bracket-output directions can be represented by separate vertices even when no suitable basis exists.





It is essential to distinguish between the associated labeled directed graph G(V,E) together with its vertex and edge labels and its underlying combinatorial structure. Regarding the vertex labels as elements of \mathfrak{g} , one recovers \mathfrak{g} as the vector space spanned by V , i.e., \mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{span}\{V\}{}=\langle V\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} . However, regarding the vertex labels as arbitrary elements, the graph G(V,E) does generally not determine the original Lie algebra, because the non-zero proportionality constants appearing in the Lie brackets are not encoded.




The following definition formalizes the correspondence between Lie algebras and their associated graphs, ensuring that the graph encodes the Lie bracket structure to the desired abstraction.



Definition 26.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra. A labeled directed graph G(V,E) is said to be associated with \mathfrak{g} if and only if it can be obtained by applying Algorithm 1 to a—possibly overcomplete—basis \mathcal{B} of \mathfrak{g} satisfying the conditions from Definition 15. We say a graph G(V,E) , associated with a Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , is a minimal graph if |V|=\dim(\mathfrak{g}) , otherwise we say that G(V,E) is a redundant graph.





Example: Graphs associated with graph-admissible Lie algebras


We here consider how the construction given above works in practice:


Example 27.

Consider the simple real Lie algebra \mathfrak{su}(2) , defined by the following Lie bracket relations [152]:




 \displaystyle[e_{1},e_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=e_{3},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[e_{2},e_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=e_{1},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[e_{3},e_{1}] 
 \displaystyle=e_{2}. 

(9)

Applying Algorithm 1, we construct the associated directed labeled graph, which is depicted in Figure 2 (a).



It is important to emphasize that the graph associated with a given Lie algebra is not unique, as illustrated by Example 28.


Example 28.

Consider the real Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) , which is not isomorphic to \mathfrak{su}(2) [103]. A standard basis of \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) is given by the elements h , x , y with the Lie bracket relations [103]:




 \displaystyle[h,x] 
 \displaystyle=2x,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[h,y] 
 \displaystyle=-2y,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x,y] 
 \displaystyle=h. 

(10)

This basis yields the graph depicted in Figure 2 (b). However, we can also choose an alternative basis, defined by the elements e_{1}:=h/2 , e_{2}:=(x-y)/2 , and e_{3}:=(x+y)/2 . It is straightforward to verify that this new basis satisfies the Lie bracket relations:




 \displaystyle[e_{1},e_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=e_{3},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[e_{2},e_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=e_{1},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[e_{3},e_{1}] 
 \displaystyle=-e_{2}. 

(11)

These relations closely resemble those of \mathfrak{su}(2) from Example 27, differing only by a sign in the final bracket. Consequently, the graph associated with this basis is identical to the graph for \mathfrak{su}(2) depicted in Figure 2 (a), despite the fact that \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) and \mathfrak{su}(2) are not isomorphic.



Figure 2: Depiction of the graphs associated with the Lie algebras \mathfrak{su}(2) and \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) from Examples 27 and 28 respectively.




Remark.



Examples 27 and 28 demonstrate two important limitations of the construction discussed here. First, the graph associated with a given Lie algebra is not unique and generally depends on the chosen (possibly overcomplete) basis. In particular, the two bases of \mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R}) considered in Example 28 produce different associated graphs. Second, non-isomorphic Lie algebras may produce isomorphic labeled directed graphs: the graph obtained from the alternative basis of \mathfrak{sl}(2,\mathbb{R}) from above coincides with the graph obtained for \mathfrak{su}(2) in Example 27. Consequently, the associated graph does not generally determine the Lie algebra up to isomorphism and need not distinguish compact Lie algebras from non-compact ones, as demonstrated with the examples [94]. This loss of information results from suppressing the nonzero proportionality constants appearing in the Lie brackets. To address such limitations, one may introduce additional edge weights or colors encoding the sign and magnitude of these structure constants. For instance, edges corresponding to brackets with positive \alpha_{jk} could be colored red, while those with negative coefficients blue. Such a refinement would allow the graph to distinguish between otherwise indistinguishable bracket structures and potentially retain more of the algebra’s intrinsic structure. We refrain from this approach, as the focus of the present framework is not on encoding all numerical data of the structure constants, but rather on utilizing graph-theoretic features to highlight structural properties, such as solvability, nilpotency, and the presence of ideals. In this context, coloring is primarily employed to identify and emphasize subgraphs that correspond to these properties.





The family of real Lie algebras L_{\alpha}^{3} was essential to prove Theorem 16, Corollary 17, and Theorem 23, as it serves as an example for demonstrating the existence of Lie algebras that are only redundant-graph-admissible but not minimal-graph-admissible, and Lie algebras that are non-graph-admissible altogether. This motivates Example 94, discussed in Appendix B, which provides explicit graphs for L_{\alpha}^{3} at specific values of \alpha .



Lemma 29.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra, and let \varphi:\mathfrak{g}\to\varphi(\mathfrak{g}) be a Lie-algebra isomorphism. Let \mathcal{B} be a (possibly overcomplete) basis satisfying (6) (respectively (7)). Then, \varphi induces an isomorphism between the labeled directed graph generated by \mathcal{B} and the labeled directed graph generated by \varphi(\mathcal{B}) .




Proof.



Let \mathcal{B}=\{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m} be a (possibly overcomplete) basis of \mathfrak{g} that satisfies the relations (6) (respectively (7)) and \varphi:\mathfrak{g}\to\varphi(\mathfrak{g}) a Lie-algebra isomorphism. Define, y_{j}:=\varphi(x_{j})\in\varphi(\mathfrak{g}) . Then, since \varphi is an invertible linear map that preserves the Lie bracket, \varphi(\mathcal{B})=\{y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m} is again a (possibly overcomplete) basis, and one has for all y_{j},y_{k}\in\varphi(\mathcal{B}) :




 \displaystyle[y_{j},y_{k}]_{\varphi(\mathfrak{g})}=[\varphi(x_{j}),\varphi(x_{k})]_{\varphi(\mathfrak{g})}=\varphi([x_{j},x_{k}]_{\mathfrak{g}})=\varphi(\alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)})=\alpha_{jk}\varphi(x_{\delta(j,k)})=\alpha_{jk}y_{\delta(j,k)}. 



Hence, by application of Algorithm 1, the sets \mathcal{B} and \varphi(\mathcal{B}) generate isomorphic graphs.
∎




Remark.



The preceding Lemma 29 demonstrates two important properties. First, graph-admissibility, including the distinction between minimal- and redundant-graph-admissibility, is preserved under Lie-algebra isomorphisms, an essential sanity check. We can again compare this with the property of diagonalizability for vector-space endomorphisms which is preserved under conjugation by invertible matrices [138]. Second, transporting a fixed suitable (possibly overcomplete) basis along a Lie-algebra isomorphism preserves the corresponding graph structure. Different graphs associated with the same Lie algebra therefore arise from choosing different suitable bases, rather than from replacing the Lie algebra and the chosen spanning set by an isomorphic copy.





The observations in Theorem 24 and Corollary 25 are crucial, as they show that the graph-based approach developed here is widely applicable and not restricted to a narrow subclass of Lie algebras. In particular, it applies to all semisimple and reductive Lie algebras over algebraically closed fields. Recall that a Lie algebra is semisimple if and only if it admits a unique decomposition of the form \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{s} , where each \mathfrak{g}_{j} is a simple ideal of \mathfrak{g} [126]. This algebraic decomposition has a direct analog in the graph-theoretic framework, as shown in the next proposition.



Proposition 30.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional semisimple Lie algebra over an algebraically closed field \mathbb{F} . Then \mathfrak{g} can be associated with a labeled directed graph G(V,E) that splits into multiple mutually unconnected subgraphs G(V_{j},E_{j}) , where each subgraph corresponds to a simple ideal \mathfrak{g}_{j}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} . In particular, if \mathfrak{g} is simple, then the associated graph consists of a single connected component.




Proof.



By the classification of semisimple Lie algebras over algebraically closed fields, any such Lie algebra admits a decomposition \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{k} , where each \mathfrak{g}_{j}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} is a simple ideal of \mathfrak{g} , and k\geq 1 . These ideals are mutually commutative, i.e., [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}]=\{0\} if j\neq\ell . By Theorem 24, each \mathfrak{g}_{j} is furthermore graph-admissible. Thus, for every \mathfrak{g}_{j} , there exists a labeled directed graph G_{j}\equiv G(V_{j},E_{j}) . These graphs must be mutually unconnected, otherwise there would exist elements x_{j}\in\mathfrak{g}_{j} and x_{k}\in\mathfrak{g}_{k} that do not vanish under the Lie bracket, even if j\neq k . Each graph G_{j} can moreover be chosen to be connected. Indeed, if an associated graph of the simple ideal \mathfrak{g}_{j} had two distinct connected components, the spans of their respective vertex sets would be non-zero ideals of \mathfrak{g}_{j} and would commute with one another. Simplicity would force both spans to equal \mathfrak{g}_{j} , implying [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{j}]=\{0\} , which contradicts the fact that \mathfrak{g}_{j} is non-abelian and simple. If \mathfrak{g} is simple, then k=1 , and the graph G(V,E) consists of a single connected component.
∎







III Criteria for valid graphs




In this section, we establish formal criteria for determining whether a labeled directed graph can be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra. These criteria are essential for distinguishing proper and improper graphs and for understanding the structural constraints imposed by the Lie bracket. We focus primarily on minimal graphs associated with minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras, although some results generalize to redundant graphs as well. Note that we are here tackling the “reverse problem” to that tackled above.



Definition 31.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph. We call G(V,E) proper if it can be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra; we call it furthermore proper-minimal if, in addition, it can be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra as a minimal graph. We call G(V,E) improper if it is not a proper graph.




A proper graph G(V,E) is said to be choice-independent if every edge e\in E can be translated into a valid Lie bracket [\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)]=\kappa\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) , for every \kappa\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . Otherwise, the graph is called choice-dependent, meaning that the validity of the bracket depends on specific values for \kappa .





Simple criteria stemming from the antisymmetric property of the Lie bracket include the following:



Proposition 32.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph. Then G(V,E) is a proper graph only if all the following conditions are satisfied:

(a) 



All edges e\in E must satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) .

(b) 



The edge e=(\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) belongs to E if and only if the edge e^{\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) also belongs to E .

(c) 



Each edge e\in E must be uniquely defined by \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e) and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) . That is, if two edges e,e^{\prime}\in E satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime}) and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime}) , then it must follow that e=e^{\prime} .






Proof.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph and suppose it can be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra  \mathfrak{g} . Consider an edge e\in E . Then:

• 



Condition (a): If \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) , then the corresponding Lie bracket is [\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)]=[\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)]\propto\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . This is not possible, since [x,x] must vanish for all x\in\mathfrak{g} , due to the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket. Therefore, such an edge cannot exist in the proper graph G(V,E) .

• 



Condition (b): Suppose e=(\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e))\in E and e^{\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e))\notin E , then, by construction of an associated graph, [\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)]\propto\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) and [\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)]=0 . This violates the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket, since 0=[\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)]=-[\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)]\not\propto\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) is impossible.

• 



Condition (c): Suppose there exist two distinct edges e,e^{\prime}\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e) and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) but e\neq e^{\prime} , i.e., \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime})\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . This implies that the Lie bracket [\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)] would be simultaneously proportional to two distinct elements, namely \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) . Since \propto is an equivalence relation, it follows that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\propto\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) , which is prohibited by Definition 26, as it would imply that the two distinct elements \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) lie in V and are proportional to each other. Hence, such ambiguity is not allowed in a proper graph.



∎





We can also attribute some properties to the entire associated graph:



Proposition 33.



Let \mathfrak{g} be an n -dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra. Then any minimal graph G(V,E) associated with \mathfrak{g} must satisfy the following properties: (a) The number of vertices equals the dimension of the Lie algebra: |V|=n ; (b) The number of edges satisfies the bound: 0\leq|E|\leq n(n-1) ; (c) For any vertex v_{\mathrm{s}}\in V , the number of outgoing edges from v_{\mathrm{s}} is bounded by: 0\leq|\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{s}}\}|\leq n-1 ; (d) For any vertex v_{\mathrm{e}}\in V , the number of incoming edges to v_{\mathrm{e}} is bounded by: 0\leq|\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{e}}\}|\leq n(n-1) .




Proof.



This claim follows directly from Definitions 15 and 26; in particular claim (d) follows immediately from claim (b).
∎




Proposition 34.



The bounds stated in Proposition 33 are tight. That is, there exist minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras that can be associated with minimal graphs that saturate these bounds.




Proof.



We start by constructing an explicit example of a minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} whose associated graph achieves the upper bounds from Proposition 33. Let \{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\} be a set of linearly independent elements and set x_{0}:=0 by convention. Define the symmetric function:




 \displaystyle\delta:\{0,1,\ldots,n\}\times\{0,1\ldots,n\}\to\{0,1,\ldots,n\},\,(j,k)\mapsto\delta(j,k):=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&,\text{ if }j=k\text{ or }j=0\text{ or }k=0\\
1&,\text{otherwise}\end{matrix}\right., 



and the antisymmetric matrix




 \displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{F}^{n\times n}\quad\text{with}\quad\alpha_{jk}:=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&\text{, if }j=k\\
1&\text{, if }j=1\text{ and }k>1\\
-1&\text{, if }k=1\text{ and }j>1\\
j-k&\text{ otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.. 



We now prove that these define the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g}=\langle\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} via the relations: [x_{j},x_{k}]:=\alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)} . Thus, we need to confirm that the Lie bracket is antisymmetric and satisfies the Jacobi identity. One has [x_{j},x_{k}]=\alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)}=-\alpha_{kj}x_{\delta(k,j)}=-[x_{k},x_{j}] for all j,k\in\{1,\ldots,n\} , since \boldsymbol{\alpha} is by construction antisymmetric and \delta symmetric. Now consider three indices j,k,\ell , all unequal to one. We compute:




 \displaystyle[x_{j},[x_{k},x_{\ell}]]+[x_{k},[x_{\ell},x_{j}]]+[x_{\ell},[x_{j},x_{k}]] 
 \displaystyle=-\alpha_{k\ell}\alpha_{1j}x_{1}-\alpha_{\ell j}\alpha_{1k}x_{1}-\alpha_{jk}\alpha_{1\ell}x_{1}=-(k-\ell+\ell-j+j-k)x_{1}=0. 



Now, let at least one of these indices be one. Without loss of generality, let j=1 . Then, we have:




 \displaystyle[x_{1},[x_{k},x_{\ell}]]+[x_{k},[x_{\ell},x_{1}]]+[x_{\ell},[x_{1},x_{k}]] 
 \displaystyle=\alpha_{1\ell}\alpha_{1k}x_{1}-\alpha_{1k}\alpha_{1\ell}x_{1}=0. 



Hence, by linear combinations, this constitutes a valid Lie algebra. We can now apply Algorithm 1 to obtain the graph G(V,E) which is associated with \mathfrak{g} . This graph has by construction n vertices. Next, we observe that the graph has n(n-1) distinct edges, as every distinct pair of indices (j,k)\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N} for which \alpha_{jk}\neq 0 induces a distinct edge. By construction, \alpha_{jk}=0 if and only if j=k . Thus |E|=n(n-1) . We also see that exactly n-1 edges start at x_{1} , and n(n-1) edges end at x_{1} .




To show that the lower bounds are also tight, consider the abelian Lie algebra of dimension n . It is immediate to notice that any basis of this Lie algebra satisfies (6), and the graphs constructed by Algorithm 1 do not possess any edges.
∎





We can draw, as an example, the graph associated with the non-abelian Lie algebra constructed in the proof of Proposition 34 for the case n=4 , as shown in Figure 3 (a). In this visualization, every edge in the set E is explicitly drawn, resulting in a visually cluttered representation, showing that this approach is not practical and loses its visual simplicity. This is especially true for large algebras, as the number of edges grows quadratically with the number of vertices. To address this issue, we introduce the convention: For any pair of vertices v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{e}}\in V , if there exists at least one edge e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{s}} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{e}} we draw only a single edge from v_{\mathrm{s}} to v_{\mathrm{e}} , regardless of how many such edges exist. We then annotate this edge with all corresponding labeling vertices v_{\mathrm{l}} that appear in the original edge set. This convention is illustrated in Figure 3 (b).


Figure 3: Depiction of the graph G(V,E) associated with the non-abelian Lie algebra from the proof of Proposition 34, for the case n=4 . In (a), the graph is visualized by drawing every edge in E explicitly; in (b) the graph is drawn using the convention of collapsing multiple edges with the same starting and end vertices into a single edge and annotating them with all relevant labeling vertices.




We now proceed with our analysis. For the sake of simplicity we assume that \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0 for the remainder of this section.



Theorem 35.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph with three or more distinct vertices. Moreover let \mathcal{V}:=\{\tilde{V}\subseteq V\,\mid\,|\tilde{V}|=3\} be the set containing all subsets \tilde{V}\subseteq V of three distinct vertices and define, for a given set \tilde{V}\in\mathcal{V} , the edge set \tilde{E}(\tilde{V})\equiv\tilde{E}:=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\} containing all edges in E , where starting vertex, labeling vertex, and end vertex belong to \tilde{V} . Then G(V,E) is a proper-minimal graph only if for every \tilde{V}\in\mathcal{V} , the subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E})\subseteq G(V,E) is equivalent to one of the graphs depicted in Figure 3.




Remark.



Before proceeding with the proof of this claim, we observe that Theorem 35 provides a necessary condition for verifying whether a given labeled directed graph G(V,E) with at least three vertices can be proper-minimal. The corresponding test requires considering every subset \tilde{V}\subseteq V with exactly three distinct vertices, and the corresponding subgraph must contain all edges from E whose starting, labeling, and end vertices belong to \tilde{V} . Hence, for a graph with m vertices, one must examine




 \displaystyle\binom{m}{3}=\frac{m(m-1)(m-2)}{6}\in\mathcal{O}(m^{3}) 



three-vertex subgraphs. Since each such subgraph contains only three vertices and is compared with the fixed list of eleven graphs depicted in Figure 3, the number of required checks belongs to \mathcal{O}(m^{3}) . However, passing all these checks is not sufficient, as illustrated by Examples 36 and 37 found below.





Remark: Theorem 35 does not provide sufficient conditions


Example 36.

Graphs of Type VIII (see Figure 3) satisfy all conditions stated in Theorem 35. However, as demonstrated in the upcoming Corollary 38, such graphs are improper graphs, meaning they cannot be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra as a minimal graph.



Example 37.

The graph depicted below clearly satisfies all conditions from Theorem 35, as the subgraphs G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) containing three distinct vertices and all edges e in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V} are all equivalent to graphs of Type I or II. Nevertheless, the Lie bracket relations yield: [a,[b,c]]=0 , [b,[c,a]]\propto e , and [c,[a,b]]=0 , which implies [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]\neq 0 , constituting a violation of the Jacobi identity.


Figure 4: Illustration of an improper labeled directed graph that satisfies all conditions from Theorem 35.




Figure 5: Visualization of all possible candidate three-vertex subgraph configurations G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) of a proper graph G(V,E) , where the edges e\in\tilde{E}\subseteq E are those that satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V} , considered up to topological equivalence. The eleven subgraphs are furthermore classified according to the following criteria: (a) proper versus improper graphs; (b) choice-independent versus choice-dependent, in case the subgraphs are proper; and (c) an algebraic classification of whether these graphs can be associated with abelian, nilpotent, solvable and non-nilpotent, and simple Lie algebras. A legend is provided to indicate these classifications using colored classification markers where applicable33
 3
