 Note that the convention for drawing graphs has changed compared to the previous figures. This adjustment was made to distinguish general graphs from those graphs associated with specific Lie algebras, where the vertices represent particular elements. In the second convention, the label of a vertex is placed beside the vertex rather than inside..



Proof.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph with three or more distinct vertices that is proper-minimal.
Since any proper-minimal graph is, by definition, proper, one can invoke Proposition 32, which implies that only two types of edges can occur:

• 



Wedge-type edges: These are edges e\in E for which all the three vertices \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) are pair-wise distinct. Such edges are called wedge-type edges because, as shown in Proposition 32, an edge e belongs to E if and only if the edge e^{\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) also belongs to E . The graph G(\{\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\},\{e,e^{\prime}\}) , depicted in Figure 6 (a), resembles a wedge when the three vertices are placed at the corners of an isosceles triangle.

• 



Loop-type edges. These are edges e\in E , where either \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) or \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e) , with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) . These are called loop-type edges because: (i) an edge e belongs to E if and only if the edge e^{\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) also belongs to E , and (ii) one of those edges is of the form (a,b,a) . In other words, one of those edges is a loop, connecting a vertex to itself. The graph G(\{\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\},\{e,e^{\prime}\}) is depicted in Figure 6 (b).



It is immediately evident that wedge-type and loop-type edges are distinct, and no graph isomorphism exists that maps one to the other.


Figure 6: Depiction of wedge-type edges in (a) and loop-type edges in (b). Note that we use a different convention for visualizing vertices, if the figure is intended to convey a general feature and not a particular Lie algebra, as before.




We proceed here with the proof of the theorem. Consider every subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E})\subseteq G(V,E) , where \tilde{V}=\{a,b,c\}\subseteq V is a set of three distinct vertices, and \tilde{E}=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\} is the set of edges whose source, label, and target all lie in \tilde{V} . Due to the edge-rule discussed in Propositions 32 and 33 , the number of edges is even and bounded from above, such that one has |\tilde{E}|\in\{0,2,4,6\} . We now analyze these cases separately, and use upper-case roman indices to refer to the corresponding graphs in Figure 3:




(i) 



 \boldsymbol{|\tilde{E}|=0} . Here, there is only one possible graph: G_{\mathrm{I}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{I}}) , where \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{I}}=\emptyset .

(ii) 



 \boldsymbol{|\tilde{E}|=2} . Here, there are nine possible graphs, divided into two groups, one group of three graphs with only wedge-type edges, and another group of six graphs with only loop-type edges. The three graphs with only wedge-type edges are the ones with \tilde{E}=\{(a,b,c),(b,a,c)\} , \tilde{E}=\{(b,c,a),(c,b,a)\} , and \tilde{E}=\{(c,a,b),(a,c,b)\} . These are clearly all equivalent, as any one of those can be transformed into the other two by a simple relabeling of the vertices. The six graphs with only loop-type edges are those with \tilde{E}=\{(a,b,a),(b,a,a)\} , \tilde{E}=\{(a,b,b),(b,a,b)\} , \tilde{E}=\{(a,c,a),(c,a,a)\} , \tilde{E}=\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c)\} , \tilde{E}=\{(b,c,b),(c,b,b)\} , and \tilde{E}=\{(b,c,c),(c,b,c)\} . These are also all clearly equivalent among themselves, but not equivalent to the previous wedge-type graphs. Thus, we identify two inequivalent graph types: G_{\mathrm{II}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{II}}) and G_{\mathrm{III}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{III}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{II}}\equiv\{(a,b,c),(b,a,c)\} and \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{III}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c)\} respectively.

(iii) 



 \boldsymbol{|\tilde{E}|=4} . This case is subdivided into three distinct subcases, depending on the types of edges involved.

(iii.i) 



All four edges are wedge-type. Here, there is only one class of inequivalent subgraphs in this category, namely subgraphs of the form G_{\mathrm{IV}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{IV}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{IV}}\equiv\{(c,b,a),(b,c,a),(c,a,b),(a,c,b)\} .

(iii.ii) 



All four edges are loop-type. Here, one finds three inequivalent subgraphs:

• 



 G_{\mathrm{XII}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XII}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XII}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(c,b,b),(b,c,b)\} ,

• 



 G_{\mathrm{V}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{V}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{V}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(b,c,c),(c,b,c)\} ,

• 



 G_{\mathrm{VI}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VI}}) with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VI}}\equiv\{(c,a,a),(a,c,a),(c,b,b),(b,c,b)\} .



Notably, subgraphs of the type G_{\mathrm{XII}} are incompatible with the Jacobi identity. Specifically: [b,[c,a]]\propto b , while [a,[b,c]]\propto[a,b]\in\spn\{V\setminus\tilde{V}\} . If [a,b]=0 , then [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]\propto b , which violates the Jacobi identity. If instead [a,b]\propto x , where x\in V\setminus\tilde{V} , then [c,x] must be proportional to an appropriate linear combination of x and b , which is prohibited for a minimal graph since all non-zero bracket results must be proportional to elements of V , which must be a basis. Hence, graphs containing subgraphs of type G_{\mathrm{XII}} cannot be associated with finite-dimensional Lie algebras as minimal graphs.

(iii.iii) 



Two wedge-type and two are loop-type edges. Here, one finds the following two inequivalent subgraphs:

• 



 G_{\mathrm{VII}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VII}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VII}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(a,b,c),(b,a,c)\} ,

• 



 G_{\mathrm{VIII}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VIII}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{VIII}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(b,c,a),(c,b,a)\} .





(iv) 



 \boldsymbol{|\tilde{E}|=6} . This last case is divided into four subcases based on the composition of wedge-type and loop-type edges.

(iv.i) 



All six edges are wedge-type. There is only one inequivalent subgraph in this category, namely G_{\mathrm{IX}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{IX}}) with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{IX}}\equiv\{(a,b,c),(b,a,c),(b,c,a),(c,b,a),(c,a,b),(a,c,b)\} .

(iv.ii) 



All six edges are loop-type. Here, there exist two inequivalent subgraphs:

• 



 G_{\mathrm{XIII}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XIII}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XIII}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(c,b,b),(b,c,b),(b,a,a),(a,b,a)\} ,

• 



 G_{\mathrm{XIV}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XIV}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XIV}}\equiv\{(c,a,a),(a,c,a),(c,b,b),(b,c,b),(a,b,b),(b,a,b)\} .



We will now show that in both cases the graph G(V,E) cannot be associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra as a minimal subgraph. For G_{\mathrm{XIII}} , one would have that [a,[b,c]]\propto a , [b,[c,a]]\propto b , and [c,[a,b]]\propto c . Thus, the Jacobi identity cannot be satisfied as one assumes that the three elements a,b,c are linearly independent and can therefore not be non-trivially linearly combined such that their weighted sum vanishes. Also, graphs with subgraphs of type G_{\mathrm{XIV}} are prohibited, since this would imply [a,b]=\kappa_{1}b , [b,c]=\kappa_{2}b , and [c,a]=\kappa_{3}a , where \kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . The Jacobi identity in this case would read: [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]=\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}b-\kappa_{1}\kappa_{3}b-\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}b=-\kappa_{1}\kappa_{3}b\neq 0 , which contradicts the very definition of the Jacobi identity.

(iv.iii) 



Four wedge-type edges and two are loop-type edges. Here, there is only one inequivalent subgraph, namely G_{\mathrm{XV}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XV}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XV}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(a,b,c),(b,a,c),(b,c,a),(c,b,a)\} . This configuration implies: [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]\propto a , which is again a violation of the Jacobi identity.

(iv.iv) 



Two wedge-type edges and four are loop-type edges. Here, there are three inequivalent subgraphs:

• 



 G_{\mathrm{XVI}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XVI}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XVI}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(a,b,c),(b,a,c),(c,b,b),(b,c,b)\} ,

• 



 G_{\mathrm{X}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{X}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{X}}\equiv\{(c,a,a),(a,c,a),(c,b,b),(b,c,b),(a,b,c),(b,a,c)\} ,

• 



 G_{\mathrm{XI}}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XI}}) , with \tilde{E}_{\mathrm{XI}}\equiv\{(a,c,c),(c,a,c),(b,c,c),(c,b,c),(a,b,c),(b,a,c)\} .



Subgraphs of the type G_{\mathrm{XVI}} would imply [a,[b,c]]\propto c , [b,[c,a]]\propto b , and [c,[a,b]]=0 . Thus, a,b,c cannot satisfy the Jacobi identity under the assumption that they are linearly independent.





This concludes the case-by-case analysis of all possible three-vertex subgraphs. Each valid configuration corresponds to one of the eleven graphs depicted in Figure 3, and any deviation from these possible configurations leads to a violation of either the Jacobi identity, bilinearity, antisymmetry of the Lie bracket, or the minimality condition.
∎




Corollary 38.



All graphs depicted in Figure 3 are proper subgraphs, except those of Type VIII. Furthermore, all proper graphs depicted in Figure  3 are choice-independent, except those of Type X.




Proof.



The edges of the graphs depicted in Figure 3 define the Lie bracket relations among the three vertex elements, up to irrelevant nonzero proportionality constants. In this context, and without loss of generality, we here treat the graphs in Figure 3 as standalone graphs themselves, not as subgraphs of a larger graph. Consequently, missing edges imply that the corresponding Lie bracket vanishes since the bracket cannot connect to a vertex outside the graph.




To determine whether a graph is proper, it suffices to verify whether the Jacobi identity is satisfied, since the vertex set V spans a vector space and the Lie bracket is antisymmetric by construction. To determine whether a given graph is choice-independent, one must similarly verify if the Jacobi identity holds irrespective of the choice for proportionality constants associated with the Lie bracket defined by the edges. We now analyze each graph type individually.




• 



Type I: The Lie bracket of all basis elements and therefore of all elements vanishes. Thus, Type I graphs can be associated with the three-dimensional abelian Lie algebra, making them proper and choice-independent.

• 



Type II: The only non-trivial Lie bracket is [a,b]\propto c . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type II proper and choice-independent.

• 



Type III: The only non-trivial Lie bracket is [a,c]\propto c . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type III proper and choice-independent.

• 



Type IV: The only non-trivial Lie brackets are [b,c]\propto a and [a,c]\propto b . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type IV proper and choice-independent.

• 



Type V: The only non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,c]=\kappa_{1}c and [b,c]=\kappa_{2}c , where \kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . Thus [a,[b,c]]=\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}c , [b,[c,a]]=-\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}c , and [c,[a,b]]=0 . Hence [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]=0 , making graphs of Type V proper and choice-independent.

• 



Type VI: The only non-trivial Lie brackets are [c,a]\propto a and [b,c]\propto b . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type VI proper and choice-independent.

• 



Type VII: The only non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,c]\propto c and [a,b]\propto c . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type VII proper and choice-independent.

• 



Type VIII: The only non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,c]=\kappa_{2}c and [b,c]=\kappa_{3}a with \kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0 , [b,[c,a]]=-\kappa_{2}\kappa_{3}a , and [c,[a,b]]=0 . Hence [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]\propto a , making graphs of Type VIII improper.

• 



Type IX: The non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,b]\propto c , [b,c]\propto a , and [a,c]\propto b . Thus [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] , making graphs of Type IX proper and choice-independent.

• 



Type X: The non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,b]=\kappa_{1}c , [a,c]=\kappa_{2}a , and [b,c]=\kappa_{3}b , where \kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . Thus [a,[b,c]]=\kappa_{1}\kappa_{3}c , [b,[c,a]]=\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}c , and [c,[a,b]]=0 . Hence [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]=\kappa_{1}(\kappa_{2}+\kappa_{3})c . To satisfy the Jacobi identity, one needs, therefore, to require \kappa_{2}=-\kappa_{3} , making graphs of Type X proper and choice-dependent.

• 



Type XI: The non-trivial Lie brackets are [a,b]=\kappa_{1}c , [a,c]=\kappa_{2}c , and [b,c]=\kappa_{3}c , where \kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} . Thus [a,[b,c]]=\kappa_{2}\kappa_{3}c , [b,[c,a]]=-\kappa_{2}\kappa_{3}c , and [c,[a,b]]=0 . Hence [a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]=0 . Hence, the Jacobi identity is satisfied for all \kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} , making graphs of Type XI proper and choice-independent.



∎




Corollary 39.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with a labeled directed graph G(V,E) , where the vertices satisfy [v_{j},[v_{k},v_{\ell}]]=0 for all j,k,\ell with j\neq k\neq\ell\neq j . Then, for any choice of three distinct vertices \{v_{1},v_{2},v_{3}\}=\tilde{V}\subseteq V , and considering only edges e\in\tilde{E}=\{e=(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}})\in E\,\mid\,v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}}\in\tilde{V}\} , the resulting subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) must be of Type I, II, III, IV, VI, VII, or IX, depicted in Figure 3.




Proof.



This statement follows from the same computations performed in the proof of Corollary 38.
∎




Corollary 40.



The proper graphs in Figure 3 can each be associated with at least one real three-dimensional Lie algebra. The naming conventions used below for these Lie algebras follow the classification given in the literature [146, 154]. We have:

• 



Type I: The abelian Lie algebra 3\mathfrak{l}_{1}=\mathfrak{l}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , also known as Bianchi I.

• 



Type II: The Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,1}\cong\mathfrak{h}_{1} [118, 91], also known as Bianchi II, determined by the nontrivial Lie bracket: [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1} .

• 



Type III: The Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , also known as Bianchi III, determined by the nontrivial Lie bracket: [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} .

• 



Type IV: The solvable Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,5}^{\beta=0} , also known as Bianchi VII, determined by the nontrivial Lie brackets [e_{1},e_{3}]=\beta e_{1}-e_{2} and [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1}+\beta e_{2} with \beta=0 .

• 



Type V: The Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , also known as Bianchi III, determined by the nontrivial Lie bracket: [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} .

• 



Type VI: The solvable Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,3} , also known as Bianchi V, determined by the nontrivial Lie brackets [e_{1},e_{3}]=e_{1} and [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{2} .

• 



Type VII: The Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , also known as Bianchi III, determined by the nontrivial Lie bracket: [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} .

• 



Type IX: The simple Lie algebra \mathfrak{gl}_{3,7} , also known as Bianchi IX, or \mathfrak{so}_{\mathbb{R}}(3)\cong\mathfrak{su}(2) [83], determined by the nontrivial Lie brackets [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{1} , [e_{3},e_{1}]=e_{2} , and [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{3} .

• 



Type X: The simple Lie algebra \mathfrak{gl}_{3,6} , also known as Bianchi VIII, or \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) [99], determined by the non-trivial Lie brackets [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{1} , [e_{2},e_{3}]=e_{3} , and [e_{1},e_{3}]=2e_{2} .

• 



Type XI: The Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , also known as Bianchi III, determined by the nontrivial Lie bracket: [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} .







This statement does not claim completeness of identification or classification. For instance, the Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{3,4} , also known as Bianchi VI, determined by the nontrivial Lie brackets [e_{1},e_{3}]=e_{1} and [e_{2},e_{3}]=\alpha e_{2} , with -1\leq\alpha<1 and \alpha\neq 0 , can be associated with a Type VI graph.



Proof.



First note that by Corollary 38, the graphs listed above are all proper graphs depicted in Figure 3. Most of these graphs can be furthermore directly obtained by applying Algorithm 1 to the respective bases of the Lie algebras stated above. However, this is not the case for the graphs of Type V, VII, and XI. To demonstrate that these graphs can be associated with the Lie algebra \mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1} , which is defined by the nontrivial Lie bracket [e_{1},e_{2}]=e_{2} , while e_{3}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{l}_{2,1}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{1}) , we consider the following alternative bases: For Type V, one uses the basis \{e_{1},e_{1}+e_{3},e_{2}\} . Applying Algorithm 1 to this basis yields a graph of Type V. For Type VII, one uses the basis \{e_{1},e_{2}+e_{3},e_{2}\} , and for Type XI, the basis \{e_{2},e_{1}-e_{2},e_{1}+e_{2}+e_{3}\} , which both yield the respective graphs.
∎




Lemma 41.



Let G(V,E) be a proper graph associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . Let G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) be a subgraph of G(V,E) with |\tilde{V}|\geq 3 . Then G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) is a proper subgraph associated with a Lie subalgebra \mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} if \tilde{E}:=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\}=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\rm s}(e),\varpi_{\rm l}(e)\in\widetilde{V}\} .




Proof.



This result is evident from the construction of the associated graphs and requires no further argument.
∎




Corollary 42.



Let G(V,E) be a proper-minimal graph associated with a real finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . If G(V,E) contains a subgraph of Type IX or Type X, then \mathfrak{g} contains a simple three-dimensional Lie subalgebra, i.e., a Lie subalgebra isomorphic to \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) or \mathfrak{su}(2) , rendering \mathfrak{g} not solvable.




Proof.



By Lemma 41, the Lie algebra associated with the graph G(V,E) contains a subalgebra \mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} that is associated with a subgraph of Type IX or X. Let this subgraph be denoted by G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) . Since G(V,E) is proper-minimal, the three vertices in \tilde{V} are linearly independent. Furthermore, one observes that \tilde{V}\subseteq[\tilde{V},\tilde{V}] , which implies that the subalgebra \mathfrak{h}:=\spn\{\tilde{V}\} is not solvable. According to the Bianchi-classification, the only non-solvable real Lie algebras of dimension three are the simple Lie algebras \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) , and \mathfrak{su}(2) [30]. Therefore, \mathfrak{g} contains a simple three-dimensional Lie subalgebra, which makes it not solvable.
∎




Proposition 43.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph. For G(V,E) to be a proper-minimal graph, it must satisfy the following condition: For every subset \tilde{V}=\{a,b,c\}\subseteq V of three distinct vertices, one of the following must hold:

(a) 



There exist three directed trails C_{1}=(a,e_{11},x_{1},e_{12},y) , C_{2}=(b,e_{21},x_{2},e_{22},y) , and C_{3}=(c,e_{31},x_{3},e_{32},y) , where x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},y\in V and the labeling conditions are \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{22})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{31})=a , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{11})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{32})=b , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{12})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{21})=c .

(b) 



After a possible relabeling of a,b,c , there exist two directed trails C_{1}=(a,e_{11},x_{1},e_{12},y) and C_{2}=(c,e_{21},x_{2},e_{22},y) with x_{1},x_{2},y\in V and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{22})=a , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{12})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{21})=b , and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{11})=c , but no directed trail C_{3}=(b,e_{31},x_{3},e_{32},y) with x_{3}\in V , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{31})=a , and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{32})=c .

(c) 



For every v\in\tilde{V} there exist no edges e,e^{\prime}\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\setminus\{v\} , \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})\in\tilde{V}\setminus\{v,\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\} .





Figure 7: Visualization of the three structural conditions described in Proposition 43. Note that this figure presents an abstraction and does not necessarily depict the actual graphs, since the Lie brackets involving the vertices \tilde{V}=\{a,b,c\} may be proportional to the vertices themselves, e.g., [c,a]\propto c .



Proof.



Every set \{a,b,c\}\subseteq V of distinct vertices must satisfy the Jacobi identity:




 \displaystyle[a,[b,c]]+[b,[c,a]]+[c,[a,b]]=0. 



Since all vertices satisfy relations (6) and are linearly independent elements, there are three distinct possibilities for how the Jacobi identity can be satisfied:

(i) 



All three nested brackets are proportional to the same nonzero element: [a,[b,c]]\propto[b,[c,a]]\propto[c,[a,b]] .

(ii) 



Two nested brackets are proportional to the same element, while the third one vanishes. After a possible relabeling of a,b,c : [a,[b,c]]\propto[b,[c,a]] , while [c,[a,b]]=0 .

(iii) 



All nested brackets vanish: [a,[b,c]]=0=[b,[c,a]]=[c,[a,b]] .



These cases are the only algebraic configurations that satisfy the Jacobi identity under the given assumption. It is immediate to observe that




 \displaystyle\text{Case }(i) 
 \displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\text{Condition }(a),\; 
 \displaystyle\;\text{Case }(ii) 
 \displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\text{Condition }(b),\; 
 \displaystyle\;\text{Case }(iii) 
 \displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\text{Condition }(c), 



as verified by the application of Algorithm 1.
∎





We can also simplify the procedure to check every triple of vertices by the following simple observation:



Proposition 44.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph with vertices V=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} and edges E=\{e_{p}\}_{p\in\mathcal{P}} that satisfies the properties (a) - (c) from Proposition 32.
Define the map \delta:(\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\})\times(\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\})\to\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\} with concrete action




 \displaystyle\delta:(j,k)\mapsto\delta(j,k):=\left\{\begin{matrix}\ell&\text{ if }\exists\text{ edge $e\in E$ with }e=(v_{j},v_{k},v_{\ell})\\
0&\text{ otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.. 

(12)


Then: (i) G(V,E) is a proper choice-independent graph if \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))=0 for all j,k,\ell\in\mathcal{M} ; (ii) G(V,E) is a proper-minimal graph only if \left|\left\{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell)),\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j)),\delta(\ell,\delta(j,k))\right\}\setminus\{0\}\right|\leq 1 for every fixed triple j,k,\ell\in\mathcal{M} with j\neq k\neq\ell\neq j .




Proof.



Suppose G(V,E) is a labeled directed graph satisfying the conditions stated above, i.e., properties (a) - (c) from Proposition 32. We begin by observing the following: The map \delta is well-defined by property (c); it is symmetric by property (b); and satisfies \delta(j,j)=0 by property (a) from Proposition 32.
Now consider the vector space \spn\{V\} equipped with the bilinear operation [\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,] defined on the basis elements via [v_{j},v_{k}]=\alpha_{jk}v_{\delta(j,k)} , where v_{0}:=0 and \boldsymbol{\alpha} is chosen to be antisymmetric. One can for example define \alpha_{jk}:=(j-k)(1-\delta_{\delta(j,k),0}) , ensuring that \boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\mathrm{Tp}}=-\boldsymbol{\alpha} . By construction, the bracket [\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,] is antisymmetric. Thus, one is left verifying that [\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,] satisfies the Jacobi identity. Observe that if \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))=0 for all j,k,\ell\in\mathcal{M} , then




 \displaystyle[v_{j},[v_{k},v_{\ell}]]+[v_{k},[v_{\ell},v_{j}]]+[v_{\ell},[v_{j},v_{k}]] 
 \displaystyle=\alpha_{j,\delta(k,\ell)}\alpha_{k\ell}v_{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))}+\alpha_{k,\delta(\ell,j)}\alpha_{\ell j}v_{\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j))}+\alpha_{\ell,\delta(j,k)}\alpha_{jk}v_{\delta(\ell,\delta(j,k))}=0, 



since each term is proportional to a vertex of the form v_{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))}\equiv v_{0}=0 . This proves claim (i). Note this conclusion does not require the assumption that V is a linearly independent set.




Let us proceed to prove claim (ii). Suppose the contrapositive, i.e., that there exist fixed indices j,k,\ell\in\mathcal{M} with j\neq k\neq\ell\neq j such that




 \displaystyle\left|\left\{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell)),\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j)),\delta(\ell,\delta(j,k))\right\}\setminus\{0\}\right|\geq 2. 



Without loss of generality, assume that \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))\neq\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j)) , where \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell)),\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j))\in\mathcal{M} , and j\neq k\neq\ell\neq j . Then, the terms \alpha_{j,\delta(k,\ell)}\alpha_{k\ell}v_{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))} and \alpha_{k,\delta(\ell,j)}\alpha_{\ell j}v_{\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j))} are linearly independent elements in \spn\{V\} , since \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0 . If V is taken to be a basis, then the third term \alpha_{\ell,\delta(j,k)}\alpha_{jk}v_{\delta(\ell,\delta(j,k))} cannot cancel both of the previous terms. This shows that violating the condition \left|\left\{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell)),\delta(k,\delta(\ell,j)),\delta(\ell,\delta(j,k))\right\}\setminus\{0\}\right|\leq 1 implies that G(V,E) cannot be associated with a minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra as a minimal graph.
∎





Here, one needs to mention that Proposition 43 and part (ii) of 44 only provide weak criteria for checking the validity of graphs that are based on the Jacobi identity, since they are only applicable for minimal graphs and only provide necessary conditions. However, they are not sufficient to distinguish between choice-dependent and choice-independent graphs, which is essential in some cases, especially when this classification is also affected by the characteristic of the field over which the algebra is defined. For further remarks on this topic see Appendix C. More fundamentally, the principal obstruction is that the prescribed graph construction suppresses the non-zero proportionality constants appearing in the Lie brackets. Consequently, particularly for choice-dependent graphs, the Jacobi identity cannot generally be verified from the graph alone. Indeed, the nested commutators entering the Jacobi identity may be proportional to the same vertex element without their coefficients cancelling. Although the graph records their common output direction, it does not retain the proportionality constants required to determine whether their sum vanishes.
The situation is further complicated for redundant graphs because their vertex elements may satisfy non-trivial linear relations. Consequently, nested commutators terminating at distinct, and even pairwise non-proportional, vertex elements may nevertheless cancel in a suitable linear combination. These linear relations are likewise not encoded by the graph itself. Strong purely graph-theoretic sufficient criteria for properness may therefore be expected primarily in the minimal choice-independent setting. In the general choice-dependent or redundant setting, one must either solve additional algebraic equations for the relevant proportionality constants and linear relations or modify the graph construction by introducing suitable weights, colors, or additional labeling rules as discussed earlier.




To summarize the current status of the obtained conditions, Appendix C.1 contains two complementary tables. Table 2 summarizes the available criteria, indicating whether they are necessary or sufficient, the settings in which they apply, and their remaining limitations. Table 3 organizes the results according to the Lie-algebra axioms and distinguishes between minimal and redundant graphs, and identifies aspects that remain unresolved.





IV Structural properties of finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebras via associated graphs




We are now equipped with all the necessary tools to identify structural properties of a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} using only the graphs G(V,E) associated with \mathfrak{g} . Among the most common structural classifications of Lie algebras are those based on the notions of abelian, nilpotent, solvable, simple, semisimple, or reductive Lie algebras. These classifications rely typically on algebraic constructions such as the lower central series, the derived series, or the identification of ideals or central elements. In what follows, we explore how these structural features can be inferred directly from the graph representation, without requiring explicit computation of the Lie bracket beyond its graphical encoding.




For simplicity, we proceed by assuming that the characteristic of the field \mathbb{F} is \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0 , and continue thereby with the same assumption introduced in the previous section.




IV.1 Identifying central elements



Lemma 45.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Let W\subseteq V be the set of all vertices with no outgoing edges. Then \spn\{W\} is a subalgebra of the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) of \mathfrak{g} .




Proof.



Let w\in W\subseteq V be a vertex with no outgoing edges. Then, by the construction of Algorithm 1, there exists no element v\in V such that [v,w]\neq 0 . Since the set V spans the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , any element g\in\mathfrak{g} can be written as a linear combination: g=\sum_{v\in V}c_{v}v with some coefficients c_{v}\in\mathbb{F} . Therefore, for any such g , one has [w,g]=0 for all g\in\mathfrak{g} , implying that w\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . This argument holds for every vertex w\in W that has no outgoing edges, and all their linear combinations. Thus \spn\{W\}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) .
∎





It is important to note that Lemma 45 does not guarantee that the set of all vertices with no outgoing edges spans the whole center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . That is, while such vertices always lie in the center, the center may contain additional elements not represented directly by single vertices without outgoing edges. To illustrate this, consider the real Lie algebra visualized in Figure 8.


Figure 8: Graph associated to the real Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , spanned by the basis elements x_{1} , x_{2} , x_{3} satisfying [x_{1},x_{2}]=0 , [x_{2},x_{3}]=x_{3} , and [x_{3},x_{1}]=x_{3} , which is known as the Lie algebra \mathfrak{aff}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathbb{R} [154, 11]. In panel (a), the graph G(V,E) constructed from this basis has no vertices without outgoing edges, i.e., the set W from Lemma 45 is empty. Nonetheless, the center of the Lie algebra is clearly nontrivial, as \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g})=\langle\{x_{1}+x_{2}\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} . In panel (b), we constructed the graph using the modified basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{1}:=x_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}:=x_{3},\tilde{x}_{2}:=x_{1}+x_{2}\} . Here, one has clearly \spn\{W\}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) .




We can, however, remedy this problem with the following result:



Lemma 46.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra with non-trivial center. Then \mathfrak{g} admits a labeled directed graph G(V,E) such that the set of all vertices with no outgoing edges spans the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) .




Proof.



Let G(V,E) be a graph associated with a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , and suppose \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g})\neq\{0\} . Let V=\{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m} denote the set of vertices spanning \mathfrak{g} . Let C=\{c_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k} be a basis for the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . If any c_{j}\in C is not proportional to an existing vertex v\in V , one can simply add c_{j} to V . Since each c_{j}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) , it follows that [v,c_{j}]=0 for all v\in V , and hence the modified set V is a valid set of vertices, according to Definition 15, and hence the subset of vertices in V with no outgoing edges spans the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) .
∎




Lemma 47.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with a labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then \mathfrak{g} is abelian if and only if the set of edges E is empty.




Proof.



Let G(V,E) be a graph associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . Suppose E is empty. Then there exists no edge e\in E , and hence no triple (v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}})\in V^{\times 3} such that [v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}}]\propto v_{\mathrm{e}} . Thus [v_{1},v_{2}]=0 for all v_{1},v_{2}\in V . Since \spn\{V\}=\mathfrak{g} , it follows by linearity [\spn\{V\},\spn\{V\}]=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]\subseteq\{0\} , implying that \mathfrak{g} is abelian. Conversely, suppose \mathfrak{g} is abelian. Then any (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} satisfies [\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=0 for all j,k\in\mathcal{M} . By the construction of Algorithm 1, the graph associated with \mathcal{B} contains no edges, i.e., E=\emptyset .
∎




Lemma 48.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with a minimal labeled directed graph G(V,E) . If G(V,E) splits into two non-trivial unconnected subgraphs, then \mathfrak{g} is the direct sum of two mutually commuting ideals.




Proof.



Suppose G(V,E) splits into two non-trivial unconnected subgraphs G(V_{1},E_{1}) and G(V_{2},E_{2}) . Then E=E_{1}\cup E_{2} and V=V_{1}\cup V_{2} , where V_{1}\cap V_{2}=\emptyset=E_{1}\cap E_{2} . An edge e\in E_{j} cannot have its labeling vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) outside of V_{j} , since by Proposition 32 its antisymmetric counterpart would belong to E and connect vertices from V_{1} and V_{2} , contradicting the assumption that the two graphs are unconnected. Thus, by construction, \spn\{V_{j}\} are two commuting ideals. Moreover, because V is a basis partitioned by V_{1} and V_{2} , \mathfrak{g}=\spn\{V_{1}\}\oplus\spn\{V_{2}\} .
∎





We conclude with the following observation. In Lemma 46 we established that for any finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra, one can construct a graph that faithfully represents the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) . That is the set of vertices with no outgoing edges spans the center. However, the provided construction does generally not yield a minimal graph since it involves adding new vertices to represent central elements not already present in the original basis. This raises a natural question: Is it possible to construct a minimal graph that faithfully represents the center of the Lie algebra if it is minimal-graph-admissible? The following conjecture addresses this question.



Conjecture 49.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra with non-trivial center. Then \mathfrak{g} admits a minimal graph G(V,E) , where the set of all vertices with no outgoing edges spans the center \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) .






IV.2 Computing the derived series




Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra with a basis \mathcal{B}=\{x_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n} that satisfies the Lie bracket relations (6). Let G(V,E) denote the labeled directed graph associated with \mathfrak{g} , constructed via Algorithm 1 using the basis \mathcal{B} . The first derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g}=[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}] is spanned by the set \{\alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)}\}_{j,k=1}^{n} , which is clearly linearly dependent, since it contains the zero element 0=\alpha_{jj}x_{\delta(j,j)} for all j\in\mathcal{N} . To refine this set, we proceed as follows: We remove all elements \alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)} for which \alpha_{jk}=0 or respectively \delta(j,k)=0 . We remove, furthermore, duplicate elements. That is, we remove all but one element for which \delta(j,k)=\delta(p,q) and \alpha_{jk}\neq 0\neq\alpha_{pq} . In particular, due to the antisymmetry of \boldsymbol{\alpha} and the symmetry of \delta , the elements \alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)} and \alpha_{kj}x_{\delta(k,j)} are proportional to each other. To formalize the recursive computation of the derived series, we define the following sequence of index sets:




 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)}:=\delta\left(\left\{(j,k)\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\mid\;\alpha_{jk}\neq 0\right\}\right)\quad\text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(0)}:=\mathcal{N}. 

(13)


This recursive definition can be analogously applied not only to minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras but also for redundant-graph-admissible ones and bases of redundant graphs. The key observation is that the following result implies that \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{x_{j}\mid\,j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\} , which allows one to compute the entire derived series by this recursive procedure via \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{x_{j}\mid\,j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\} .



Lemma 50.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra. Then, the derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} is also graph-admissible for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Furthermore, if \mathfrak{g} is minimal-graph-admissible, then \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} is also minimal-graph-admissible for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} .




Proof.



Let \{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} be an overcomplete basis of the finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} that satisfies the Lie bracket relations (7). We define recursively the sets:




 \displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)}:=\delta\left(\left\{(j,k)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\mid\;\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0\right\}\right)\quad\text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(0)}:=\mathcal{M}. 

(14)


By virtue of Definition 15 of \delta it is immediate that \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Furthermore, one notes the following:

• 



Suppose z\in\mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} . Then there exist pairs of elements x_{j},y_{j}\in\mathfrak{g} , indexed by a finite non-empty index set \mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} , such that z=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}[x_{j},y_{j}] holds44
 4
 
 
 
 To realize that, recall that z\in[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}] if and only if z\in\spn\{[x,y]\,\mid\,x,y\in\mathfrak{g}\} . Thus, any z\in\mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g} can be written as z=\sum_{j,k}c_{jk}[x_{j},x_{k}] , where j,k denote indices from suitable index sets and c_{jk} are appropriate coefficients. However, due to the bilinearity of the Lie bracket, one can write z=\sum_{j}[x_{j},y_{j}] with y_{j}=\sum_{k}c_{jk}x_{k} , establishing the validity of the initial claim.. Since \{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} spans \mathfrak{g} , there exist coefficients \mu_{jp}\in\mathbb{F} and \nu_{jq}\in\mathbb{F} , with p,q\in\mathcal{M} , such that x_{j}=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{M}}\mu_{jp}\tilde{x}_{p} and y_{j}=\sum_{q\in\mathcal{M}}\nu_{jq}\tilde{x}_{q} . Then:




 \displaystyle z=\sum_{p,q\in\mathcal{M}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mu_{jp}\nu_{jq}[\tilde{x}_{p},\tilde{x}_{q}]=\sum_{p,q\in\mathcal{M}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mu_{jp}\nu_{jq}\tilde{\alpha}_{pq}\tilde{x}_{\delta(p,q)} 



Thus z\in\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\} , by the definition of \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)} , and the convention that \delta(p,q)=0 whenever \tilde{\alpha}_{pq}=0 .

• 



Suppose z\in\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\} . Then there exist coefficients \lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{F} with j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)} such that z=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}}\lambda_{j}\tilde{x}_{j} . By the definition of \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)} , there exist indices p_{j},q_{j}\in\mathcal{M} such that [\tilde{x}_{p_{j}},\tilde{x}_{q_{j}}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{p_{j}q_{j}}\tilde{x}_{j} with \tilde{\alpha}_{p_{j}q_{j}}\neq 0 . We now choose a particular pair of (\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j})\in\mathcal{M}^{2} that satisfies [\tilde{x}_{\hat{p}_{j}},\tilde{x}_{\hat{q}_{j}}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{\hat{p}_{j}\hat{q}_{j}}\tilde{x}_{j} with \tilde{\alpha}_{\hat{p}_{j}\hat{q}_{j}}\neq 0 . Then:




 \displaystyle z=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}}\lambda_{j}\frac{1}{\tilde{\alpha}_{\hat{p}_{j}\hat{q}_{j}}}[\tilde{x}_{\hat{p}_{j}},\tilde{x}_{\hat{q}_{j}}]=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}}\left[\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{\hat{p}_{j}\hat{q}_{j}}}\tilde{x}_{\hat{p}_{j}},\tilde{x}_{\hat{q}_{j}}\right], 



which implies that z\in\mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} .



Hence, we conclude that \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)}\} . Given the recursive definition \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}\mathfrak{g}=[\mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}] of the elements of the derived series, it follows immediately by means of induction that \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\} . Consequently, one observes that \{\delta(j,k)\,\mid\,j,k\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\}\setminus\{0\}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)} , since \mathfrak{g} is assumed to be graph-admissible, and \{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} is an appropriate overcomplete basis satisfying the bracket relations:




 \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)}\quad\text{ for all }j,k\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}, 



where \delta(j,k)=0 if \tilde{\alpha}_{jk}=0 and otherwise \delta(j,k)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} for all j,k\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} . This confirms that every derived algebra is graph-admissible.




The second claim follows analogously by replacing \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} with \mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} and noticing that the sets \{x_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\} are linearly independent as they are subsets of the basis \{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} .
∎





Let us now denote the graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} by \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)\equiv G(\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V,\mathcal{D}^{\ell}E) , and investigate the relationship between \mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}G(V,E) and \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) . Constructing a graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra is achieved by applying Algorithm 1 to the set \{x_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\} , where \{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} is a basis of \mathfrak{g} satisfying the bracket relations (6). A detailed implementation of this procedure is provided in Appendix E, specifically in Algorithm 5.




In analyzing the graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} , it becomes necessary to understand the conditions for an index j to belong to \mathcal{N}^{(\ell-1)}_{\mathrm{D}} but not to \mathcal{N}^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{D}} . Consider therefore the definition of the sets \mathcal{N}^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{D}} , provided in (13). If an index j belongs to \mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell-1)} but not to \mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} , this implies that there exists no pair (p,q)\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell-1)}\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell-1)} such that [x_{p},x_{q}]=\kappa x_{j} with \kappa\in\mathbb{F}^{*} and j\in\mathcal{N} . In other words, the element x_{j} does not appear as a nontrivial Lie bracket of any two basis elements from the previous derived set. Translated into graph-theoretic terms, x_{j} corresponds to a vertex that is not the endpoint of any edge in \mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}G(V,E) . Consequently all such vertices with no incoming edges must be removed when constructing the graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) from \mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}G(V,E) . Due to their importance, and to differentiate them from abelian elements, we introduce the following notation:



Definition 51.



A vertex v with no incoming edges but at least one outgoing edge is called a loose end.





To maintain the validity of the graph structure, it is mandatory to remove every edge in \mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}G(V,E) whose starting, labeling, or end vertex belongs to the set of vertices eliminated in the current pruning round. All vertices and edges satisfying these deletion conditions are first marked and removed simultaneously only after all vertices have been inspected. All other vertices and edges persist since their corresponding elements are evidently part of \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . This “pruning” procedure is formalized into Algorithm 2.


Algorithm 2 Algorithm for generating a labeled directed graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} 



Data: A (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} of the finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , satisfying the Lie bracket relations (7).


Result: A sequence of labeled directed graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} for all \ell\geq 0 , constructed with respect to the given basis \mathcal{B} .



 1
 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \emptyset 
/* Initialize the set containing all graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with each derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} */



 2
 V \leftarrow \mathcal{B} 
/* Initialize the vertex set for G(V,E) with the basis \mathcal{B} */



 3
 E \leftarrow \emptyset 
/* Initialize the edge set for G(V,E) */



 4
 foreach (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} with j<k do /* Add all relevant edges to edge set E */


   
 5
 if [\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)} with \tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0 then


      
 6
 E \leftarrow E\cup\{(\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\}\cup\{(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\} 
/* Add edge to edge set E ; each edge is an ordered triple: (start vertex, edge-label, end vertex) */



 7
 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \{G(V,E)\} /* Add initial graph associated with \mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{D}^{0}\mathfrak{g} to \mathcal{D} */



 8
 foreach \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} do


   
 9
 R_{V} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize set of vertices that must be deleted in each pruning step */


   
 10
 R_{E} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize set of edges that must be deleted in each pruning step */


   
 11
 foreach vertex v\in V do


      
 12
 if there exists no edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v then


         
 13
 R_{V} \leftarrow R_{V}\cup\{v\} /* Mark v for deletion from V */


         
 14
 foreach edge e\in E do


            
 15
 if v\in e then /* i.e., edge is of the form (\cdot,\cdot,v) or (\cdot,v,\cdot) or (v,\cdot,\cdot) */


               
 16
 R_{E} \leftarrow R_{E}\cup\{e\} /* Mark e for deletion from E */


   
 17
 V \leftarrow V\setminus R_{V} /* Simultaneously remove untargeted vertices from V */


   
 18
 E \leftarrow E\setminus R_{E} /* Simultaneously remove marked edges from E */


   
 19
 V^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V\leftarrow V 


   
 20
 E^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathcal{D}^{\ell}E\leftarrow E 


   
 21
 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \mathcal{D}\cup\{\mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(V^{(\ell)},E^{(\ell)})\} /* Add updated graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) */



 22
 return \mathcal{D} /* Return set of all graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for all \ell\geq 0 */









Lemma 52.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then, the sequence of graphs obtained via Algorithm 2 can be associated with the Lie algebras of the derived series of \mathfrak{g} .




Proof.



This claim follows directly from the preceding discussion, since the logic does not change when replacing the basis \{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} —satisfying the bracket relations (6)—with an overcomplete basis \{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} —satisfying the bracket relations (7). However, note that in each pruning round, the sets R_{V} and R_{E} are determined with respect to the unchanged graph from the preceding round. Hence, the membership of a vertex in R_{V} is not affected by the processing order. After all vertices have been inspected, the simultaneous removal of the marked vertices and edges produces precisely the graph associated with the next Lie algebra in the derived series.
∎





In order to highlight the working mechanisms of Algorithm 2, it is instructive to apply it to a specific example of a solvable minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra, as demonstrated in Example 53.



Remark.



It is important to note that this method is not restricted to minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras and minimal graphs. It also applies to redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebras and redundant graphs. In the case of redundant graphs, however, the set of vertices V is a set of linearly dependent elements spanning the associated Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . Consequently, when performing Algorithm 2 to compute \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}G(V,E) starting from \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) , it is possible that certain vertices v_{\ell} with indices \ell\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} are removed, even though the corresponding elements \tilde{x}_{\ell} belong to \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}\mathfrak{g} . This occurs whenever \ell\notin\delta(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)},\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}) but \tilde{x}_{\ell} is linearly dependent on other elements \tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)}\in\mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}V . If it is desirable to retain such elements in the graph, one can modify the Algorithm 2 to reintroduce these vertices back into the set \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}V , along with all corresponding edges into \mathcal{D}^{\ell+1}E . This modification does not affect the correctness of the procedure, as the algorithm remains valid regardless of whether the initial basis is overcomplete or not. The modified version is presented in Appendix E as Algorithm 6.





Example: Application of Algorithm 2


Example 53.

To illustrate Algorithm 2 we apply it to the real Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , defined on the seven-dimensional vector space spanned by the basis \mathcal{B}=\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{6}\} whose elements satisfy the following Lie brackets:




 \displaystyle[x_{0},x_{1}] 
 \displaystyle=x_{1},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{0},x_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=-(7-j)x_{j}\quad\text{for }j\in\{2,3,4,5,6\},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{1},x_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=x_{j+1}\quad\text{for }j\in\{2,3,4,5\}, 


which are the only non-vanishing ones.
This Lie algebra is part of a family of solvable Lie algebras that can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{1} , where the first derived algebra is non-abelian [99].


Applying Algorithm 2 to this algebra yields a sequence of directed graphs, each corresponding to a derived algebra in the series \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . The graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) are constructed with respect to the basis \mathcal{B} and according to the pruning procedure of Algorithm 2. The respective graphs are depicted in Figure 9.


Figure 9: Graphs associated with the derived series of the Lie algebra from Example 53. In panel (a): The initial graph \mathcal{D}^{0}G(V,E)\equiv G(V,E) associated with the full Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . In panel (b): The graph after removing all loose ends and isolated vertices, along with the corresponding edges, which can be associated with the first derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} . In panel (c): The graph \mathcal{D}^{2}G(V,E) , and in panel (d): The graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) for \ell\geq 3 .






We are now able to state the following fundamental results:



Theorem 54.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra, and let G(V,E) be any labeled directed graph associated with \mathfrak{g} . Then, \mathfrak{g} is solvable if and only if the series of derived graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) constructed by Algorithm 2 terminates after a finite number of steps.





Before proceeding with the proof, we need to mention that the series of derived graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) is said to terminate, if for some \ell_{*} , the corresponding derived graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell_{*}}G(V,E) is trivial, i.e., both the vertex and edge sets are empty: \mathcal{D}^{\ell_{*}}V=\emptyset=\mathcal{D}^{\ell_{*}}E . We adopt furthermore the convention that the trivial graph induces the trivial Lie algebra: \langle\emptyset\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}=\spn\{0\} .



Proof.



This follows immediately from Lemma 52 and the observation that each derived algebra satisfies \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}=\langle\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , where \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V,\mathcal{D}^{\ell}E) is the graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra. The termination of the derived graph sequence corresponds precisely to the vanishing of the derived series, which characterizes solvability.
∎





We can further establish some graph-theoretic criteria for solvability that solely rely on the structure of the graph associated with the original Lie algebra, without needing to explicitly compute the derived series.



Proposition 55.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then, \mathfrak{g} is non-solvable if G(V,E) contains a non-empty self-contained subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a cycle C 55
 5
 
 
 
 Note that every cycle is, by Definition 10, a directed walk. Thus a graph induced by a cycle is analogously defined as a graph induced by a directed walk (cf. Definition 11)..




Proof.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph associated with the finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , and suppose it contains a non-empty self-contained subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) induced by a cycle C . By definition, this means that G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) is a labeled directed graph, where every edge e\in\tilde{E} is labeled by a vertex v\in\tilde{V} . The set of vertices V spanning \mathfrak{g} is a possibly overcomplete, albeit finite, basis of \mathfrak{g} . We can therefore define V=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} . Without loss of generality, we may relabel the vertices such that \tilde{V}=\{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{s} and C=(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{s-1},e_{s-1},v_{s},e_{s},v_{1}) , where s\leq m=|\mathcal{M}| . Consequently, the Lie brackets of the subgraph elements satisfy:




 \displaystyle[v_{j},v_{k_{j}}] 
 \displaystyle=\alpha_{jk_{j}}v_{j+1}\quad\text{for all }j\in\{1,\ldots,s-1\}\quad\text{and}\quad[v_{s},v_{k_{s}}]=\alpha_{sk_{s}}v_{1}, 



where k_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,s\} and \alpha_{jk_{j}}\in\mathbb{F}^{*} for all j\in\{1,\ldots,s\} . It is therefore clear that v_{j},v_{k_{j}}\in[\spn\{\tilde{V}\},\spn\{\tilde{V}\}] for all j\in\{1,\ldots,s\} , and hence \spn\{\tilde{V}\}\subseteq[\spn\{\tilde{V}\},\spn\{\tilde{V}\}] . It follows consequently that \spn\{\tilde{V}\} is contained in every derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{k}\mathfrak{g} for all k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Since \tilde{V}\neq\emptyset by assumption, the derived series of \mathfrak{g} does not terminate, making \mathfrak{g} non-solvable.


Figure 10: Visualization of the Lie bracket relation [v_{j},v_{k_{j}}]\propto v_{j+1} , where v_{j},v_{j+1} are two consecutive vertices of a cycle C connected by the directed edge e_{j} . The vertex v_{k_{j}} is the labeling vertex of the edge e_{j} , i.e., \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{j})=v_{k_{j}} .




∎





We can further generalize Proposition 55 by extending the criterion from subgraphs induced by a directed cycle to those induced by closed directed walks.




We begin with an observation concerning graphs that do not contain self-contained subgraphs induced by closed directed walks, under the assumption that they are associated with a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra:



Lemma 56.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then, G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraphs G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) induced by non-trivial closed directed walks W if and only if, in every subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a non-trivial closed directed walk W , there exists a vertex w\in\tilde{V} such that for all edges e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=w , either the source \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} or the label \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} .





While this lemma might appear self-evident at first glance, it is not trivial, as demonstrated by the example below.




Remark: Discussion of Lemma 56


Example 57.

Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph associated with a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} that does not contain any subgraphs that are self-contained and induced by a closed directed walk. Envision a situation in which the subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) , depicted in Figure 11, is a subgraph of G(V,E) that is induced by a closed directed walk W . In this case, at least one edge must be labeled by a vertex from V\setminus\tilde{V} . For illustration purposes, we assume here that two such edges exist and have colored them orange in the figure. Additionally, we can now suppose that for every vertex v in \tilde{V} , there exists an edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v and both \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in\tilde{V} and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\tilde{V} . These edges, which do not belong to the walk W , are colored blue in Figure 11.


Figure 11: Depiction of a subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by the closed directed walk W . The edges of W are colored black if the labels are vertices from \tilde{V} and orange if this is not the case. Edges that do not belong to the walk W , but to the larger graph G(V,E) , are colored blue.


At first glance, it may appear that G(V,E) contains a different closed directed walk W^{\prime} that induces a self-contained subgraph G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) , as illustrated in Figure 12. However, upon closer inspection, some edges in this walk may be labeled by vertices that do not belong to \tilde{V}^{\prime} . This suggests that, even if no subgraph of G(V,E) is induced by a closed directed walk that is self-contained, in principle it might still be possible to encounter subgraphs where, for every vertex, there exists an edge e\in E such that the starting, labeling, and ending vertex of e belong to the subgraph. In the proof that follows, we show that such a configuration is not possible.


Figure 12: Overlay of the subgraph G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) , induced by the closed directed walk W^{\prime} , on top of the subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E})\subseteq G({V},{E}) from Figure 11. The edges of G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) are colored black if the labels are vertices from \tilde{V}^{\prime} , and orange if this is not the case. Vertices and edges that do not belong to the walk W^{\prime} but to the walk W are colored gray.





Proof.



Suppose that in every subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) induced by a non-trivial closed directed walk W there exists a vertex w\in\tilde{V} such that all edges e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=w satisfy either \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} or \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} . Then, by Definition 12, no such subgraph G_{W} can be self-contained, since at least one edge of the induced subgraph G_{W} violates the condition that all components of the edge belong to \tilde{V} .




For the converse direction, we argue by contraposition. That is, we want to show that if G(V,E) possesses a subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) , induced by a non-trivial closed directed walk W in G(V,E) , such that for all vertices v\in\tilde{V} there exists an edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\tilde{V} , then there exists a closed directed walk W^{\prime} that induces a self-contained subgraph G_{W^{\prime}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) . Thus, let now G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) be such a subgraph induced by W . Then, by assumption, for each v\in\tilde{V} there exists at least one edge e_{v}\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{v})=v and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{v}),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{v})\in\tilde{V} . For each v\in\tilde{V} choose exactly one such edge e_{v}\in E and keep these choices fixed. For later convenience, we say that W satisfies the incoming-edge condition.




All closed directed walks considered here and below have, without loss of generality, finite length. For a directed walk W^{\prime\prime} with induced graph G_{W^{\prime\prime}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}^{\prime\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime\prime}) , we introduce the set




 \displaystyle\mathcal{M}(W^{\prime\prime}):=\left\{e\in\tilde{E}^{\prime\prime}\,\middle\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\notin\tilde{V}^{\prime\prime}\right\} 



of all edges of the walk W^{\prime\prime} whose labeling vertices are not visited by W^{\prime\prime} itself and set m:=|\mathcal{M}(W)| . For each later walk, \mathcal{M} is defined analogously using that walk’s own visited vertices and traversed edges.




We will now show by complete induction that, for every m\geq 0 , every non-trivial closed directed walk W^{\prime\prime} that satisfies the incoming-edge condition and |\mathcal{M}(W^{\prime\prime})|=m implies the existence of a closed directed walk W^{\prime} that induces a self-contained subgraph G_{W^{\prime}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) .




The base case m=0 is immediate, since in this case W^{\prime\prime} visits every labeling vertex of its edges, implying that G_{W^{\prime\prime}} is self-contained.




Let us continue with the induction step. Thus, let m>0 and assume that the claim has been established for all non-trivial closed directed walks satisfying the incoming-edge condition and having fewer than m distinct edges labeled by vertices not visited by the walk itself. We may now choose some e\in\mathcal{M}(W) and set v:=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . For each t\in\tilde{V} with t\neq v , we can find a non-trivial directed segment S_{vt} of W that starts at v , ends at t , and does not traverse e . Indeed, start immediately after an occurrence of e in the walk W , i.e., at v , and follow the walk, cyclically if necessary, until t is reached. Whenever v is visited again before reaching t , discard the part of the segment preceding this visit. The remaining segment therefore starts at v and does not return to v before reaching t . It therefore does not traverse e , as traversing any occurrence of e would return to v , and the part through that occurrence would have been discarded. For t=v set S_{vv} to be the empty segment. By assumption, the previously chosen edge e_{v}\in E satisfies \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{v})=v and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{v}),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{v})\in\tilde{V} . Set u:=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{v}) . We may now construct the closed directed walk W_{1}=(v,\ldots,u,e_{v},v) by following the previously constructed segment S_{vu} from v to u and then traversing e_{v} back to v . By construction, W_{1} is a non-trivial finite closed directed walk that does not traverse e . Indeed, S_{vu} does not traverse e by construction and e_{v}\neq e , since \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{v})\in\tilde{V} , whereas \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} . Hence W_{1} avoids every occurrence of e and only visits vertices from \tilde{V} .




Observe that W_{1} need not satisfy the incoming-edge condition or induce a self-contained subgraph G_{W_{1}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}_{1},\tilde{E}_{1}) , as, for example, it may omit vertices that label some edges \tilde{e}\in\tilde{E}_{1} , including the label of e_{v} . In order to apply the induction hypothesis, we must restore the incoming-edge condition and obtain a walk with fewer than m edges whose labeling vertices are not visited.




With the construction presented below, we will include the missing vertices by an iterative procedure. Write G_{W_{j}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}_{j},\tilde{E}_{j}) for the graph induced by the current closed directed walk W_{j} , where we start with the walk W_{1} . This walk has the following properties: (i) W_{1} is a non-trivial finite closed directed walk; (ii) v\in\tilde{V}_{1}\subseteq\tilde{V} ; (iii) e\notin\tilde{E}_{1} ; and (iv) each edge \tilde{e}\in\tilde{E}_{1} satisfies either \tilde{e}\in\tilde{E}\setminus\{e\} or \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(\tilde{e}),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(\tilde{e}),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(\tilde{e})\in\tilde{V} . We will preserve these properties with index j at each stage in the following procedure. The index j labels the successively constructed walks. The outer induction parameter m=|\mathcal{M}(W)| remains fixed during this construction. Suppose W_{j} has the same properties. We now describe how to obtain W_{j+1} if (a) |\mathcal{M}(W_{j})|\geq m or (b) there exists a vertex t\in\tilde{V}_{j} whose chosen incoming edge e_{t} satisfies either \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{t})\notin\tilde{V}_{j} or \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{t})\notin\tilde{V}_{j} . If either condition holds, proceed as follows.

• 



Case (a). Here, there must exist an edge \tilde{e}\in\tilde{E}_{j} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(\tilde{e})\in\tilde{V}\setminus\tilde{V}_{j} , since |\mathcal{M}(W)\setminus\{e\}|=m-1 and, by property (iv), every edge \tilde{e}\in\tilde{E}_{j}\setminus\mathcal{M}(W) satisfies \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(\tilde{e})\in\tilde{V} . Choose such an edge \tilde{e} and set a:=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(\tilde{e}) , t:=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(\tilde{e}) , and u:=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(\tilde{e}) . By construction, t,u\in\tilde{V}_{j} as they are both visited by W_{j} . According to Proposition 32, the antisymmetric counterpart \tilde{e}^{\prime}=(a,u,t) belongs to E .




Now start at an occurrence of t in W_{j} and follow W_{j} , cyclically if necessary, until reaching v . Then follow the segment S_{va} of W , constructed as before so that it avoids traversing e . Finally, traverse \tilde{e}^{\prime} from a back to t . This produces a closed directed walk that can be inserted into W_{j} at t to obtain W_{j+1} . This procedure adds the previously missing vertex a to the set of vertices visited by W_{j+1} . Its closing edge \tilde{e}^{\prime} is labeled by u , which was already visited by W_{j} , and its starting and ending vertices are visited by W_{j+1} .

• 



Case (b). Let t\in\tilde{V}_{j} be a vertex whose chosen incoming edge e_{t} satisfies either \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{t})\notin\tilde{V}_{j} or \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{t})\notin\tilde{V}_{j} . Denote one such missing vertex by a . By assumption, a\in\tilde{V} . If \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{t})=a , set \tilde{e}^{\prime}:=e_{t} . Otherwise, \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{t})=a , and we set \tilde{e}^{\prime}:=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{t}),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{t}),t) , i.e., the antisymmetric counterpart of e_{t} , which is contained in E by Proposition 32. In either case, \tilde{e}^{\prime} starts at a and ends at t .




Now, similarly to case (a), start at an occurrence of t in W_{j} and follow W_{j} , cyclically if necessary, until reaching v . Then follow the segment S_{va} of W that avoids e , and finally traverse \tilde{e}^{\prime} from a back to t . Insert this closed directed walk at t in W_{j} to obtain W_{j+1} . This newly constructed walk visits the previously missing vertex a . If the label of its closing edge \tilde{e}^{\prime} is still missing, it is the other starting or labeling vertex of the chosen edge e_{t} . Case (b) can then be applied again at the same vertex t in the next iteration of this procedure, and the next insertion includes this remaining missing input.






In both cases, the inserted walk avoids e : its initial segment along W_{j} avoids e by property (iii), the segment S_{va} avoids it by construction, and the closing edge has its labeling vertex in \tilde{V} , whereas \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} . Also, every edge of W_{j+1} is either an edge of W_{j} , an edge of the original walk W other than e , or a new closing edge whose three vertices all belong to \tilde{V} . Thus, W_{j+1} satisfies conditions (i)-(iv) from before. The inserted excursion visits the previously missing vertex a and retains all vertices and edges of W_{j} . Hence, \tilde{V}_{j}\subsetneq\tilde{V}_{j+1}\subseteq\tilde{V} and \tilde{E}_{j}\subseteq\tilde{E}_{j+1} .




One now repeats the construction in cases (a) and (b) as long as either condition applies. This procedure must terminate after at most |\tilde{V}|-|\tilde{V}_{1}| insertions, since every insertion strictly enlarges the visited set \tilde{V}_{j} , which is always contained in the finite set \tilde{V} . If the visited set reaches \tilde{V} , case (b) cannot apply because, for each v\in\tilde{V} , the starting and labeling vertices of every chosen edge e_{v} lie in \tilde{V} . Case (a) cannot apply either: by property (iv), the edges labeled outside of \tilde{V} must belong to \mathcal{M}(W)\setminus\{e\} , of which there are m-1 . Thus, after finitely many steps we obtain a walk W^{\prime\prime} with induced graph G_{W^{\prime\prime}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}^{\prime\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime\prime}) for which neither case applies. The absence of case (a) implies that |\mathcal{M}(W^{\prime\prime})|<m , while the absence of (b) shows that W^{\prime\prime} satisfies the incoming-edge condition. The walk W^{\prime\prime} is thus a non-trivial finite closed directed walk that satisfies the incoming-edge condition, as well as |\mathcal{M}(W^{\prime\prime})|<m . By the complete induction hypothesis, there exists therefore a closed directed walk W^{\prime} that induces a self-contained subgraph G_{W^{\prime}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) . This proves the converse by contraposition. That is, if G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraphs G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) induced by a non-trivial closed directed walk W , then, in every subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a non-trivial closed directed walk W , there exists a vertex w\in\tilde{V} such that for all edges e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=w , either \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} or \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\notin\tilde{V} .
∎




Theorem 58.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra and let G(V,E) be any labeled directed graph associated with \mathfrak{g} . Then \mathfrak{g} is non-solvable if and only if G(V,E) contains a self-contained subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a non-trivial closed directed walk W .




Remark.



Note that the classification of a Lie algebra as solvable or non-solvable is intrinsic to the Lie algebra under consideration. Thus, the result above holds for every proper labeled directed graph that can be associated with the graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} .




Proof.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . For each \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , we write G_{\ell}:=G(V_{\ell},E_{\ell}):=\mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) , where \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) is the derived graph of the initial graph G(V,E)=G(V_{0},E_{0})=\mathcal{D}^{0}G(V,E)=G_{0} obtained by \ell applications of Algorithm 2. Since the graphs G_{\ell} are obtained by Algorithm 2, we may further observe that




 \displaystyle V_{\ell+1}:=\left\{v\in V_{\ell}\,\middle\mid\,\exists\,e\in E_{\ell}\text{ such that }v=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\right\}=\{\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\,\mid\,e\in E_{\ell}\},\qquad E_{\ell+1}:=\left\{e\in E_{\ell}\,\middle\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in V_{\ell+1}\right\}. 



This implies in particular that V_{\ell+1}\subseteq V_{\ell} and E_{\ell+1}\subseteq E_{\ell} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} .




First, suppose that G(V,E) contains a self-contained subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) induced by a non-trivial closed directed walk W=(w_{0},e_{0},w_{1},\ldots,w_{s},e_{s},w_{0}) . We now show by induction over \ell that \tilde{V}\subseteq V_{\ell} and \tilde{E}\subseteq E_{\ell} for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . The base case \ell=0 is trivially true. Thus, we may assume that the induction hypothesis, i.e., the two inclusions \tilde{V}\subseteq V_{\ell} and \tilde{E}\subseteq E_{\ell} , holds for some \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Let now w\in\tilde{V} be a vertex visited by W . Then, there exists an edge e_{w}\in\tilde{E}\subseteq E_{\ell} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{w})=w . This implies that w\in V_{\ell+1} for each w\in\tilde{V} , and therefore \tilde{V}\subseteq V_{\ell+1} . Moreover, since G_{W} is self-contained, it holds that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\subseteq V_{\ell+1} for every e\in\tilde{E} . Thus, by construction of the sets E_{\ell} , it holds that \tilde{E}\subseteq E_{\ell+1} , which completes the induction. Since W is non-trivial, this shows that V_{\ell}\neq\emptyset\neq E_{\ell} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Thus, by Theorem 54, \mathfrak{g} is non-solvable, since the series \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) never terminates at the trivial graph.




Let us now show the converse direction. For this, we argue by contraposition. That is, we assume that G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) that is induced by a non-trivial closed directed walk W and aim to show that \mathfrak{g} is in that case solvable. For this, according to Theorem 54, we have to show that there exists a non-negative integer \ell_{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} such that G_{\ell_{*}}=G(\emptyset,\emptyset) . To show that there exists such a non-negative integer \ell_{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} such that V_{\ell_{*}}=\emptyset , it suffices to show that V_{\ell+1}\subsetneq V_{\ell} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} such that V_{\ell}\neq\emptyset , since V is finite. This would then immediately also imply that E_{\ell_{*}}=\emptyset , and therefore G_{\ell_{*}}=G(\emptyset,\emptyset) .




Suppose, for a proof by contradiction, that there exists some non-negative integer f\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} such that V_{f+1}=V_{f}\neq\emptyset . This would then, by construction, immediately imply that E_{f+1}=E_{f}\neq\emptyset . Indeed, each edge e\in E_{f} has all three of its vertices in V_{f}=V_{f+1} and is therefore also retained, which implies that E_{f}\subseteq E_{f+1} . The converse inclusion follows from the earlier observation that E_{f+1}\subseteq E_{f} . Non-emptiness follows from V_{f+1}\neq\emptyset . Next, let us introduce, for each vertex v\in V_{f} , the set




 \displaystyle R_{v}:=\left\{w\in V_{f}\,\middle\mid\,\exists\,\text{closed directed walk $W$ in $G_{f}$ such that $W$ visits $w$ and $v$}\right\}\cup\{v\}. 



Here, we observe that, for u,v\in V_{f} , it clearly holds that R_{v}=R_{u} exactly when u\in R_{v} . More generally, for two vertices u,v\in V_{f} , it holds that either R_{v}=R_{u} or R_{v}\cap R_{u}=\emptyset . Furthermore, for at least one v\in V_{f} , the set R_{v} is not trivial, i.e., there exists a second vertex w\in R_{v}\setminus\{v\} . To show this, we first observe that, for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , if (v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}})\in E_{\ell} , then the anti-symmetric counterpart (v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{e}}) also belongs to E_{\ell} . The base case \ell=0 follows immediately from Proposition 32. Hence, assume the claim holds for some \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , and let e=(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}})\in E_{\ell+1} . Since E_{\ell+1}\subseteq E_{\ell} , one has that e\in E_{\ell} . By the induction hypothesis, one has e^{\prime}:=(v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{e}})\in E_{\ell} . However, since e\in E_{\ell+1} , it also holds that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})=v_{\mathrm{s}} , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})=v_{\mathrm{l}} , and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime})=v_{\mathrm{e}} all belong to V_{\ell+1} . Thus, e^{\prime}\in E_{\ell+1} , by construction of the sets E_{\ell} , which completes the proof by induction.




Now, pick some v\in V_{f}=V_{f+1} . Then, there exists an edge e_{v}\in E_{f} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{v})=v and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{v}),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{v})\in V_{f} . By the observation above, the antisymmetric counterpart \tilde{e}_{v}:=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{v}),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{v}),v) also belongs to E_{f} . According to Proposition 32, the starting and labeling vertices of e_{v} and therefore also of \tilde{e}_{v} are distinct, so at least one of these two edges has distinct starting and ending vertices. Pick this edge and move backwards to its starting vertex. Then repeat the same process for the vertex reached by this process. Since V and therefore V_{f} are finite, after at most |V_{f}| iterations a vertex must be visited twice. Retain the part of the walk constructed by this process that starts at the second occurrence of the first repeated vertex and ends at its first occurrence, where the walk shall be followed in the direction of the edges, i.e., the reverse direction compared to its construction. The resulting walk is a non-trivial closed directed walk that visits at least two distinct vertices. Hence, at least one set R_{v} contains at least two distinct vertices.




We may now decompose V_{f} as follows: pick some v_{1}\in V_{f} and compute R_{v_{1}} . Then choose each successive v_{k}\in V_{f} from V_{f}\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^{k-1}R_{v_{j}} and continue until every vertex v\in V_{f} is included in exactly one of these sets R_{v_{k}} . Since V_{f} is finite and non-empty, one has




 \displaystyle V_{f}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{d}R_{v_{k}}\quad\text{for some }d\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}. 



Note that by an earlier observation, the sets R_{v_{k}} are pairwise disjoint.




In a next step, we want to show that for at least one k\in\{1,\ldots,d\} there exists no edge e\in E_{f} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in R_{v_{k}} while \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\notin R_{v_{k}} . To establish this, we assume the contrary. In this case, we may pick some k_{1}\in\{1,\ldots,d\} such that there exists an edge e_{1}\in E_{f} with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{1})\in R_{v_{k_{1}}} and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{1})\notin R_{v_{k_{1}}} , where \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{1})\in R_{v_{k_{2}}} for some k_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\setminus\{k_{1}\} . We may now repeat this process iteratively. That is, we choose an edge e_{j}\in E_{f} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{j})\in R_{v_{k_{j}}} while \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j})\in R_{v_{k_{j+1}}} , where k_{j+1}\neq k_{j} .
