Since d is finite, there must exist a smallest integer j_{*}\geq 2 such that k_{j_{*}+1}\in\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{j_{*}}\}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,d\} . Since k_{j_{*}+1}\neq k_{j_{*}} , we may write k_{j_{*}+1}=k_{q} for some q\in\{1,\ldots,j_{*}-1\} . By the choice of j_{*} , the indices k_{q},k_{q+1},\ldots,k_{j_{*}} are pairwise distinct. For each p\in\{q+1,\ldots,j_{*}\} , the two vertices \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{p}) and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{p-1}) both belong to R_{v_{k_{p}}} . Moreover, \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{q}) and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j_{*}}) both belong to R_{v_{k_{q}}} , since k_{j_{*}+1}=k_{q} . By construction of the sets R_{v} , for each p\in\{q+1,\ldots,j_{*}\} there exists a directed walk W_{p} from \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{p}) to \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{p-1}) only visiting vertices in R_{v_{k_{p}}} . Moreover, there exists a directed walk W_{q} from \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{q}) to \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j_{*}}) only visiting vertices in R_{v_{k_{q}}} . If the two vertices are identical take the corresponding walk to be the empty walk. Now follow the edges e_{j_{*}},e_{j_{*}-1},\ldots,e_{q} in this order and join the ending and starting vertices \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{p}) and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{p-1}) of two consecutive edges with the corresponding walks W_{p} for p\in\{q+1,\ldots,j_{*}\} . Finally follow W_{q} from \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{q}) to \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j_{*}}) to close the resulting walk. This construction yields a non-trivial closed directed walk that visits the starting and ending vertices of every e_{p} with p\in\{q,q+1,\ldots,j_{*}\} . By the definition of the sets R_{v} and the equality of the overlapping sets, all these vertices belong to the same set. In particular, this would imply that




 \displaystyle\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j_{*}})\in\bigcap_{p=q}^{j_{*}}R_{v_{k_{p}}}=\emptyset, 



which is impossible. Thus, for at least one k\in\{1,\ldots,d\} there exists no edge e\in E_{f} with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in R_{v_{k}} and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\notin R_{v_{k}} .




Let now R_{v_{\hat{k}}} be such that every edge e\in E_{f} with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in R_{v_{\hat{k}}} satisfies \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in R_{v_{\hat{k}}} . We will now construct a closed directed walk W in R_{v_{\hat{k}}} where the labeling vertex of each traversed edge is also visited. Set \{w_{1},\ldots,w_{s}\}=R_{v_{\hat{k}}} and start with w_{1} . By construction, there exists an edge e_{w_{1}}\in E_{f} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{w_{1}})=w_{1} and a:=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{w_{1}}) and b:=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{w_{1}}) both belong to V_{f} . Moreover, by an earlier observation, the antisymmetric counterpart \tilde{e}_{w_{1}}=(b,a,w_{1}) also belongs to E_{f} . Since both edges end in R_{v_{\hat{k}}} , they must also both start in R_{v_{\hat{k}}} . Thus, both a and b belong to R_{v_{\hat{k}}} . The same argument applies to every edge of E_{f} ending in R_{v_{\hat{k}}} . The set R_{v_{\hat{k}}} contains at least two vertices, since Proposition 32 does not allow edges of the form (w_{1},w_{1},w_{1}) . By construction, for each w_{\ell}\in R_{v_{\hat{k}}}\setminus\{w_{1}\} there exists a closed directed walk W_{\ell} that visits both w_{1} and w_{\ell} . For each \ell\in\{2,\ldots,s\} , choose W_{\ell} such that it begins at w_{1} and follow these walks one after another. Each such walk stays within R_{v_{\hat{k}}} , because every vertex visited lies on a closed directed walk with w_{1} . The resulting walk therefore visits precisely all vertices from R_{v_{\hat{k}}} and is hence non-trivial. Let now G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) be the subgraph induced by this walk, where now \tilde{V}=R_{v_{\hat{k}}} . By construction and the argument before, for each vertex v\in\tilde{V} , there exists an edge e_{v}\in E_{f} with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{v})=v and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{v}),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{v})\in\tilde{V} . Lemma 56 now implies that G(V,E) contains a self-contained subgraph induced by a closed directed walk. This contradicts the initial assumption.




Consequently V_{\ell+1}\subsetneq V_{\ell} , whenever V_{\ell}\neq\emptyset . Since V is finite, after at most |V| steps we obtain V_{\ell_{*}}=\emptyset for some \ell_{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . By the discussion at the beginning of the proof of this direction, it follows that \mathfrak{g} is solvable. Hence, the contraposition holds: if \mathfrak{g} is non-solvable, then G(V,E) contains a non-trivial self-contained subgraph induced by a non-trivial closed directed walk. This proves the converse direction and therefore the complete claim.
∎




Corollary 59.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the redundant graph G(V,E) . Suppose G(V,E) contains a closed directed walk W that induces the subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) , where every edge in \tilde{E} is labeled either by a vertex v\in\tilde{V} or a vertex w\in(V\cap\spn\{\tilde{V}\})\setminus\tilde{V} —that is, a linear combination of the vertices from \tilde{V} . Then, G(V,E) contains a closed directed walk W^{\prime} that induces a self-contained subgraph G_{W^{\prime}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) .




Proof.



Let W=(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},e_{2},\ldots,e_{s},v_{1}) be a closed directed walk of length s that satisfies the conditions stated in the claim above. This walk induces the subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) , where \tilde{V}=\{v_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}\} and \tilde{E}=\{e_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}\} . This implies the following sequence of Lie bracket relations:




 \displaystyle[v_{1},\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{1})] 
 \displaystyle\propto v_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[v_{2},\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{2})] 
 \displaystyle\propto v_{3},\; 

 \displaystyle\;\ldots\; 
 \displaystyle\;[v_{s},\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{s})] 
 \displaystyle\propto v_{1}. 



That is, each bracket operation involving a vertex in the walk and the labeling vertex of the consecutive edge yields the next vertex in the walk, up to a scalar multiple. Recall that for two subspaces \mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{w} of a Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , one defines [\mathfrak{v},\mathfrak{w}]:=\spn\{[v,w]\,\mid\,v\in\mathfrak{v},\,w\in\mathfrak{w}\} [126]. We have, therefore, \spn\{\tilde{V}\}\subseteq[\spn\{\tilde{V}\},\spn\{\tilde{V}\}] , since \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\spn\{\tilde{V}\} for all e\in\tilde{E} . Hence \spn\{\tilde{V}\}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} , because \tilde{V}\subseteq V . Consequently: \spn\{\tilde{V}\}\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . It follows that the derived series of \mathfrak{g} does not terminate, making \mathfrak{g} non-solvable. By Theorem 58, this implies that G(V,E) must contain a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph of G(V,E) .
∎






IV.3 Computing the lower central series




The computation of the lower central series using a labeled directed graph associated with a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} follows a procedure similar to that used for the derived series. In fact, the first step must yield the same result as the one obtained from the first step for the procedure utilized in case of the derived series, since \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{g} holds for all Lie algebras \mathfrak{g} . The key difference lies in the recursive construction of the consecutive algebras: while the derived series is formed by taking Lie brackets among elements from the preceding algebra, the lower central series is obtained by taking Lie brackets between elements of the original algebra and those of the previous algebra in the series. Let \mathcal{B}=\{{x}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n} be a basis of the finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} satisfying the relations (6). We now define the index sets




 \displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}:=\delta\left(\left\{(j,k)\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\mid\;\alpha_{jk}\neq 0\right\}\right)\quad\text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(0)}:=\mathcal{N}. 

(15)


An analogous construction applies to finite-dimensional redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebras and their associated redundant graphs.
The essential observation is now that the elements of the lower central series are generally given by \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{x_{j}\,\mid j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\} , as will be formalized in the following lemma:



Lemma 60.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra. Then, the algebras \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} in the lower central series are also graph-admissible. Moreover, if \mathfrak{g} is minimal-graph-admissible, then each algebra \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} is also minimal-graph-admissible for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} .




Proof.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra. Then, there exists a (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} that satisfies the relations (7) and spans \mathfrak{g} . We now introduce the recursively constructed sets




 \displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}:=\delta\left(\left\{(j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\mid\;\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0\right\}\right)\quad\text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(0)}:=\mathcal{M}. 

(16)


It is straightforward to observe that \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , which can be shown by induction: Clearly \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(1)}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(0)}=\mathcal{M} , since \delta(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M})\subseteq\mathcal{M}\cup\{0\} and \tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0 if and only if \delta(j,k)\neq 0 . Suppose now j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)} . Then, there exists an index p\in\mathcal{M} and k\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)} such that \delta(p,k)=j . By the induction hypothesis, \mathcal{M}^{(\ell)}_{\mathrm{C}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell-1)} , it follows that k\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell-1)} , and consequently j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)} , proving \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} .




Furthermore, \spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(1)}\}=\mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{g} , which follows analogously to the proof of Lemma 50, since \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(1)}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(1)} and \mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} . We now proceed with the induction step:

• 



Suppose z\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}\mathfrak{g} . Then, there exist elements x_{j}\in\mathfrak{g} and y_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} , for j\in\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathbb{N} , a non-empty finite index set, such that z=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}[x_{j},y_{j}] . Since \mathcal{B} spans \mathfrak{g} , and, by induction hypothesis, \spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\}=\mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} , there exist coefficients \mu_{jp} and \nu_{jq} with p\in\mathcal{M} and q\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)} , such that x_{j}=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{M}}\mu_{jp}\tilde{x}_{p} and y_{j}=\sum_{q\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}}\nu_{jq}\tilde{x}_{q} . Thus:




 \displaystyle z=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{M}}\sum_{q\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mu_{jp}\nu_{jq}[\tilde{x}_{p},\tilde{x}_{q}]=\sum_{p\in\mathcal{M}}\sum_{q\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mu_{jp}\nu_{jq}\tilde{\alpha}_{pq}\tilde{x}_{\delta(p,q)}. 



Hence, z\in\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\} , by the definition of \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)} and the convention that \delta(p,q)=0 if \tilde{\alpha}_{pq} vanishes.

• 



Conversely, suppose z\in\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\} . Then, there exist coefficients \lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{F} for j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)} , such that z=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}}\lambda_{j}\tilde{x}_{j} . An index j belonging to \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)} implies the existence of indices p_{j}\in\mathcal{M} and q_{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)} such that \delta(p_{j},q_{j})=j\neq 0 , or equivalently [\tilde{x}_{p_{j}},\tilde{x}_{q_{j}}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{p_{j}q_{j}}\tilde{x}_{j} with \tilde{\alpha}_{p_{j}q_{j}}\neq 0 . Thus, for every j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)} , we can select exactly one pair (\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j})\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)} such that \delta(\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j})=j , and write:




 \displaystyle z=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}}\lambda_{j}\frac{1}{\tilde{\alpha}_{\hat{p}_{j}\hat{q}_{j}}}[\tilde{x}_{\hat{p}_{j}},\tilde{x}_{\hat{q}_{j}}]=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}}\left[\tilde{x}_{\hat{p}_{j}},\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{\hat{p}_{j}\hat{q}_{j}}}\tilde{x}_{\hat{q}_{j}}\right]. 



Hence, by the induction hypothesis \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\} , it follows that z\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}\mathfrak{g} .



Thus, one has inductively proven that \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}=\spn\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . The second claim can be shown analogously, by recognizing that \{{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\} forms a linearly independent set, as it is a subset of the basis \mathcal{B} .
∎




Corollary 61.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then, for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , there exists a subgraph G(\tilde{V}^{(\ell)},\tilde{E}^{(\ell)})\subseteq G(V,E) that can be associated with the \ell -th term of the lower central series \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} , and \tilde{V}^{(\ell)}=\{v_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\} .




Proof.



This follows directly by adapting the argument presented in the proof of Lemma 60.
∎





Lemma 60 implies that one can now associate a graph \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E) with the \ell -th element of the lower central series by applying Algorithm 1 to the (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V:=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\} of \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . However, to facilitate this, we propose an alternative approach via Algorithm 3. There, we extend the graph G(V,E) to a tripartite structure G(V,E,E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(0)}) , where E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(0)} is an initially empty set of labeled directed edges of a distinct other type66
 6
 
 
 
 These edges will be illustrated using dashed lines throughout this work. However, to visualize the semidirect structure of a Lie algebra, we will also represent the corresponding edges with dashed lines. The convention being used in each case will be clear from the context.. We now denote by \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E,E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(0)})\equiv G(\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V,\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E,E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)}) the subgraphs of G(V,E,\emptyset) that can be employed to construct graphs that can be in turn associated with \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} and whose vertices are \{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\} . The edge set E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} is defined as:




 \displaystyle E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)}:=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V\text{ and }\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in V\setminus\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V\}\qquad\text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}. 



The actual labeled directed graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the algebras \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series are defined as the subgraphs of the graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E,E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(0)}) , where the edge set E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} is removed. We then have the following result:



Lemma 62.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then, for each \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , the graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V,\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E) , obtained via Algorithm 3, can be associated with the \ell -th Lie algebra in the lower central series of \mathfrak{g} .





The core idea behind Algorithm 3 is to construct a graph associated with \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} not directly using the set \{v_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\} , but iteratively pruning the original graph G(V,E) . Starting with G(V,E) , one classifies the vertices from V into the two sets \mathcal{C}^{1}V:=\{v_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(1)}\} , which corresponds to the elements spanning \mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{g} , and the set V\setminus\mathcal{C}^{1}V , which do not. The vertices v\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V are precisely those for which there exists an edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v . Thus, to obtain the graph associated with \mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{g} , one must simply remove all vertices from V that are not endpoints of any edge e\in E . The corresponding edge set \mathcal{C}^{1}E is then acquired by removing all edges e\in E for which any of \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\notin\mathcal{C}^{1}V . The resulting graph G(\mathcal{C}^{1}V,\mathcal{C}^{1}E):=\mathcal{C}^{1}G(V,E) is clearly associated with \mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{g} .




To construct \mathcal{C}^{2}G(V,E) , we identify \mathcal{C}^{2}V:=\{v_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(2)}\} as the subset of \mathcal{C}^{1}V that consists of all vertices v\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V for which there exists an edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v and either \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V or \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V . Crucially, this step considers all edges e\in E to find the relevant ones, not just those in e\in\mathcal{C}^{1}E . To account for this, we introduce the aforementioned set E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(1)} , consisting of edges e\in E\setminus\mathcal{C}^{1}E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V and either \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V or \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V . By Proposition 32, these edges can be reduced to those originating from vertices w\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V , since any edge e\in E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(1)} with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in V\setminus\mathcal{C}^{1}V must satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V . Such an edge e belongs to E if and only if the edge e^{\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) belongs to E . Thus removing these edges e\in E\setminus\mathcal{C}^{1}E with \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in V\setminus\mathcal{C}^{1}V from E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(1)} does not lose any information since e^{\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e))\in E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(1)} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . Therefore, removing edges e from E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(1)} that satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\notin\mathcal{C}^{1}V does not result in loss of information and we can simply define E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(1)} , as the set of edges e\in E\setminus\mathcal{C}^{1}E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}V . This procedure can be iteratively applied to construct all graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V,\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E) associated with the algebras \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series. To visually distinguish edges from E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} , we represent them using dashed lines.



Proof.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra. Then \mathfrak{g} admits a (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}}\equiv V that satisfies the relations (7). We aim to show that the graph G(\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V,\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E) , constructed via Algorithm 3, can be associated with the Lie algebra \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . This can, due to Corollary 61, be shown by inductively proving that \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\} and \mathcal{C}^{\ell}E=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V\} . The base case \ell=0 is immediate. For the inductive step, consider the case \ell+1 . By Algorithm 3 and the induction hypothesis, the set \mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}V includes every vertex v\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\} for which there exists an edge e in \mathcal{C}^{\ell}E or E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v (cf. lines 14-17,29,32). Consider both cases separately:

• 



If e\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v , then there exist two vertices v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V such that [v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}}]\propto v . This implies that v\in\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\} , since \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\}\subseteq V .

• 



If e\in E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v , then there exists a vertex v_{\mathrm{s}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V and a vertex v_{\mathrm{l}}\in V\setminus\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V such that [v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}}]\propto v . This implies again that v\in\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\} , since V\setminus\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\}\subseteq V .



Thus one has \mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}V\subseteq\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\} . Conversely, suppose v\in\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\} . Then, by the induction hypothesis, there exists an element v_{\mathrm{s}}\in\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)}\}=\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V and an element v_{\mathrm{l}}\in V such that [v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}}]\propto v . Therefore, there exists an edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in V , implying e\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E\cup E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} , and hence v\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}V , since \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v . This shows \{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\}\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}V and consequently \mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}V=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\} . Finally, by Algorithm 3 (cf. lines 24-26,30,33), one has \mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}E=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}V\} , which completes the proof.
∎




Theorem 63.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra, and let G(V,E) be any labeled directed graph associated with \mathfrak{g} . Then, \mathfrak{g} is nilpotent if and only if the series of graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E) constructed by Algorithm 3 terminates after a finite number of steps.





Here, similar to Theorem 54, we need to clarify what is meant by termination in this context. The sequence of graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E) is said to terminate, if for some \ell_{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , the corresponding graph \mathcal{C}^{\ell_{*}}G(V,E) is trivial, i.e., both its vertex and edge sets are empty: \mathcal{C}^{\ell_{*}}V=\emptyset and \mathcal{C}^{\ell_{*}}E=\emptyset . We also recall that we adopted the convention that the trivial graph induces the trivial Lie algebra: \langle\emptyset\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}=\spn\{0\} . This aligns with the standard interpretation of the termination of the lower central series, where \mathcal{C}^{\ell_{*}}\mathfrak{g}=\{0\} for some \ell_{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} .



Proof.



This result is a direct consequence of the observation that the vertex set \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V spans the Lie algebra \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} , i.e., \spn\{\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V\}=\mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . Therefore, \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V=\emptyset if and only if \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}=\{0\} . Note that \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V=\emptyset implies trivially that \mathcal{C}^{\ell}E=\emptyset . This establishes the equivalence between the termination of the lower central series of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} and the corresponding graph sequence generated by Algorithm 3. The claim follows then directly from Definition 5.
∎





We can demonstrate Algorithm 3 for a simple example:




Example: Application of Algorithm 3


Example 64.

Consider the real seven-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , spanned by the basis \{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{7} , and defined by the non-trivial Lie bracket relations [v_{1},v_{j}]=v_{j+1} for all j\in\{2,\ldots,6\} . The lower central series of the Lie algebra is given by:




 \displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{0}\mathfrak{g} 
 \displaystyle=\spn\{\{v_{j}\}_{j=1}^{7}\},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{g} 
 \displaystyle=\spn\{\{v_{j}\}_{j=3}^{7}\},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\mathcal{C}^{2}\mathfrak{g} 
 \displaystyle=\spn\{\{v_{j}\}_{j=4}^{7}\}, 



 \displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{3}\mathfrak{g} 
 \displaystyle=\spn\{\{v_{j}\}_{j=5}^{7}\},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\mathcal{C}^{4}\mathfrak{g} 
 \displaystyle=\spn\{\{v_{j}\}_{j=6}^{7}\},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\mathcal{C}^{5}\mathfrak{g} 
 \displaystyle=\spn\{\{v_{j}\}_{j=7}^{7}\}, 


while \mathcal{C}^{6}\mathfrak{g}=\{0\}=\mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for all \ell\geq 7 .
This Lie algebra, similar to the Lie algebra considered in Example 53, is part of a family of nilpotent Lie algebras that can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{1} [99]. In fact, this algebra can be seen as the first derived algebra of the solvable eight-dimensional Lie algebra from the same family of algebras as the seven-dimensional Lie algebra from Example 53.


Algorithm 3 can be utilized to compute the sequence of labeled directed graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E,E_{\mathrm{r}}) that are associated with each term \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} in the lower central series. The graphs are depicted in Figure 13.


Figure 13: Application of Algorithm 3 to generate the graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the Lie algebras \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series from Example 64. Each graph corresponds to a step in the lower central series, from \mathcal{C}^{0}\mathfrak{g} down to the last non-trivial Lie algebra \mathcal{C}^{5}\mathfrak{g}\neq\{0\}. Note that the dashed edges in the figure represent elements of the supplementary edge sets E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)}=\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E_{\mathrm{r}} , which are not part of the graph \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E) itself but are used in the construction process to identify relevant vertices and edges for the next iteration, and highlight structural aspects of the Lie algebra.




Algorithm 3 Algorithm for generating a labeled directed graph associated with the \ell -th Lie algebra of the lower central series \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} 



Data: A (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} of the finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , satisfying the Lie bracket relations (7).


Result: A sequence of labeled directed graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with Lie algebras \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series for the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} for all \ell\geq 0 , constructed with respect to the given basis \mathcal{B} .



 1
 \mathcal{C} \leftarrow \emptyset 
/* Initialize the set that contains all graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E,E_{\mathrm{r}}) associated with each Lie algebra \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series */



 2
 V \leftarrow \mathcal{B} /* Initialize the vertex set for G(V,E) with the basis \mathcal{B} */



 3
 E \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the edge set for G(V,E) */



 4
 E_{\mathrm{r}} \leftarrow \emptyset 



 5
 foreach (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} with j<k do /* add all relevant edges to edge set E */


   
 6
 if [\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)} with \tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0 then


      
 7
 E \leftarrow E\cup\{(\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\}\cup\{(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\} 
/* Add edge to edge set E ; each edge is an ordered triple: (start vertex, edge-label, end vertex) */



 8
 \mathcal{C} \leftarrow \{G(V,E,\emptyset)\} /* Add initial graph associated with \mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{C}^{0}\mathfrak{g} to \mathcal{C} */



 9
 foreach \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} do


   
 10
 V_{\mathrm{old}} \leftarrow V 


   
 11
 E_{\mathrm{old}} \leftarrow E 


   
 12
 E_{\mathrm{r,old}} \leftarrow E_{\mathrm{r}} 


   
 13
 R_{V} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the set of vertices marked for removal */


   
 14
 foreach vertex v\in V_{\mathrm{old}} do


      
 15
 if there exists no edge e\in E_{\mathrm{old}}\cup E_{\mathrm{r,old}} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v then


         
 16
 R_{V} \leftarrow R_{V}\cup\{v\} /* Mark v for removal from V */


   
 17
 V_{\mathrm{new}} \leftarrow V_{\mathrm{old}}\setminus R_{V} 


   
 18
 R_{E} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the set of edges marked for removal from E */


   
 19
 R_{E_{\mathrm{r}}} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the set of edges marked for removal from E_{\mathrm{r}} */


   
 20
 A_{E_{\mathrm{r}}} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the set of edges marked for transfer from E to E_{\mathrm{r}} */


   
 21
 foreach edge e\in E_{\mathrm{r,old}} do


      
 22
 if there exist no vertices v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{e}}\in V_{\mathrm{new}} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{s}} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{e}} then


         
 23
 R_{E_{\mathrm{r}}} \leftarrow R_{E_{\mathrm{r}}}\cup\{e\} 
/* Mark e for removal from E_{\mathrm{r}} */


   
 24
 foreach edge e\in E_{\mathrm{old}} do


      
 25
 if there exist no vertices v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}}\in V_{\mathrm{new}} such that e=(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}}) then


         
 26
 R_{E} \leftarrow R_{E}\cup\{e\} /* Mark e for removal from E */


         
 27
 if there exist vertices v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{e}}\in V_{\mathrm{new}} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{s}} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v_{\mathrm{e}} then


            
 28
 A_{E_{\mathrm{r}}} \leftarrow A_{E_{\mathrm{r}}}\cup\{e\} /* Mark e for transfer from E to E_{\mathrm{r}} */


   
 29
 V \leftarrow V_{\mathrm{new}} 


   
 30
 E \leftarrow E_{\mathrm{old}}\setminus R_{E} 


   
 31
 E_{\mathrm{r}} \leftarrow 
 (E_{\mathrm{r,old}}\setminus R_{E_{\mathrm{r}}})\cup A_{E_{\mathrm{r}}} 


   
 32
 V^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V\leftarrow V 


   
 33
 E^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E\leftarrow E 


   
 34
 E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} \leftarrow E_{\mathrm{r}} 


   
 35
 \mathcal{C} \leftarrow \mathcal{C}\cup\{\mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E,E_{\mathrm{r}})=G(\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V,\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E,E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)})\} /* Add updated graph \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E) */



 36
 return \mathcal{C} /* Return the set of all graphs \mathcal{C}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the Lie algebras \mathcal{C}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the lower central series for all \ell\geq 0 */









Lemma 65.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra and let G(V,E) be any labeled directed graph associated with \mathfrak{g} . Then \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V=\mathcal{P}_{\ell} , where the sets \mathcal{P}_{\ell} are recursively defined by




 \displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\ell+1}:=\left\{\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\,\middle\mid\,e\in E,\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell}\right\},\qquad\text{with}\qquad\mathcal{P}_{0}:=V. 



For each \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} , the set \mathcal{P}_{\ell} denotes the set of ending vertices of directed walks in G(V,E) of length \ell .




Proof.



This can be shown by induction. The base case \ell=0 is clear, because \mathcal{C}^{0}V=V=\mathcal{P}_{0} by definition. Thus, we may assume that \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V=\mathcal{P}_{\ell} for some \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} and aim to show that \mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}V=\{v_{j}\,\mid\,j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)}\}=\mathcal{P}_{\ell+1} , where V=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} . Let j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell+1)} . Then there exists an index p\in\mathcal{M} and an index q\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{C}}^{(\ell)} such that \delta(p,q)=j and \tilde{\alpha}_{pq}\neq 0 . This implies that there exist some v_{p}\in V and v_{q}\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V such that [v_{p},v_{q}]=-[v_{q},v_{p}]=\kappa v_{j} with \kappa\neq 0 . By construction, it follows that there exists an edge e=(v_{q},v_{p},v_{j})\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v_{j} and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v_{q}\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V=\mathcal{P}_{\ell} . Hence, v_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell+1} , and therefore \mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}V\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\ell+1} . The reverse inclusion follows analogously, since every step in the argument above can be read in both directions.




To see that, for \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} , the set \mathcal{P}_{\ell} is precisely the set of ending vertices of directed walks in G(V,E) of length \ell , we again argue by induction. Notice first that \mathcal{P}_{1} is the set of vertices that are the ending points of at least one edge in E and therefore precisely the ending vertices of walks of length one. Assume now that the assertion holds for some \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} . If v\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell+1} , then there exists an edge e\in E that starts at some vertex w\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell} and ends at v . By the induction hypothesis, there exists a directed walk of length \ell that ends at w . Extending this walk by a traversal of e thus yields a walk of length \ell+1 that ends at v . Conversely, let a walk of length \ell+1 end at v , and let e be its last edge. Removing the final occurrence of v and this edge results in a walk of length \ell ending at w:=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e) . The induction hypothesis now implies that w\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell} and therefore v=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\mathcal{P}_{\ell+1} , which completes the proof by induction.
∎




Theorem 66.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra and let G(V,E) be any labeled directed graph associated with \mathfrak{g} . Then, \mathfrak{g} is nilpotent if and only if G(V,E) contains no non-trivial closed directed walk.




Remark.



Note that, similarly to Theorem 58, nilpotency and non-nilpotency are intrinsic properties of the Lie algebra under consideration. Thus, the result above holds for every proper labeled directed graph that can be associated with a graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} .




Proof.



Recall the definition of the sets \mathcal{P}_{\ell} in Lemma 65 and suppose G(V,E) contains a non-trivial closed directed walk W=(v_{0},e_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{s-1},e_{s-1},v_{0}) of length s . Then v_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{j+ks} for all j\in\{0,1,\ldots,s-1\} and k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} because the walk W can be repeated arbitrarily often to construct directed walks of length j+ks that end at the vertices v_{j} . Thus, \mathcal{P}_{\ell}\neq\emptyset for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Since \mathcal{P}_{\ell}=\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V by Lemma 65 and, by construction, \mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}V\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V , for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is, according to Theorem 63, not nilpotent.




Conversely, suppose G(V,E) contains no non-trivial closed directed walk. Since V is finite, every directed walk in G(V,E) has length at most m-1=|V|-1 . Indeed, if there existed a walk W of length s\geq m , then it would have to visit a vertex v\in V twice. However, the directed segment of W starting at the first occurrence of v and ending at the second occurrence of v is a non-trivial closed directed walk, contradicting the initial premise. This now implies that \mathcal{P}_{m}=\emptyset and therefore, by Lemma 65, \mathcal{C}^{m}V=\emptyset . Hence, it follows from Theorem 63 that \mathfrak{g} is nilpotent. The contraposition of this implication is thus also true, showing that if \mathfrak{g} is not nilpotent then G(V,E) contains a non-trivial closed directed walk.
∎




Corollary 67.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional, non-abelian, nilpotent Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) , and let W be a directed walk within G(V,E) . Then, \operatorname{len}(W)\leq\ell_{*} , where \ell_{*}+1 is the index of \mathfrak{g} . This inequality is sharp, i.e., there exists at least one directed walk W^{\prime} within G(V,E) such that \operatorname{len}(W^{\prime})=\ell_{*} . Furthermore, every non-trivial directed walk within G(V,E) is a directed trail and a directed path.




Proof.



Let \ell_{*}+1 be the index of \mathfrak{g} . Then \mathcal{C}^{k}\mathfrak{g}=\{0\} for all k>\ell_{*} , while \mathcal{C}^{\ell_{*}}\mathfrak{g}\neq\{0\} . Since \mathfrak{g} is assumed to be non-abelian, \ell_{*}\geq 1 . By Lemmata 62 and 65 it holds that \mathcal{P}_{k}=\mathcal{C}^{k}V=\emptyset for all k>\ell_{*} , while \mathcal{P}_{\ell_{*}}=\mathcal{C}^{\ell_{*}}V\neq\emptyset . Since, by construction, \mathcal{C}^{\ell+1}V\subseteq\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , it also holds that \mathcal{P}_{\ell}=\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V\neq\emptyset for all \ell\in\{0,1,\ldots,\ell_{*}\} . According to Lemma 65, for each \ell\in\{0,1,\ldots,\ell_{*}\} , there exists therefore a directed walk in G(V,E) of length s=\ell but no directed walk of length s>\ell_{*} , which proves the first part of the claim.




Finally, we want to show that every non-trivial directed walk within G(V,E) is a directed trail and a directed path. For this, we recall that Theorem 66 prohibits non-trivial closed directed walks within G(V,E) . If a directed walk visited a vertex more than once, the directed segment of the walk connecting the two occurrences of that vertex would be a non-trivial closed directed walk, which is prohibited. Thus, any directed walk within G(V,E) cannot visit a vertex more than once and therefore also not traverse the same edge more than once. Thus, by Definition 10, every non-trivial directed walk within G(V,E) is a directed trail and a directed path.
∎




Lemma 68.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . If G(V,E) contains a closed directed walk C=(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},e_{2},\ldots,e_{s},v_{1}) , then \dim(\operatorname{span}\{v_{j},\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{j})\}_{j=1}^{s})\geq 2 .




Proof.



This is an immediate consequence of the anti-symmetric property of the Lie bracket.
∎






IV.4 Identifying ideals




Another important structural feature of Lie algebras is the presence of ideals, which play a central role in classification, decomposition, and representation theory [126, 130, 109]. The graph-theoretic approach introduced here also provides insight into the identification and characterization of ideals.
Since every ideal is, by definition, a subalgebra, we decide to begin with a lemma that is concerned with graphs that can be associated with subalgebras.



Lemma 69.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) , and let \tilde{V} be a non-empty subset of V . Then, the subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) can be associated with the Lie algebra \mathfrak{v}:=\mathrm{span}\{\tilde{V}\}{} if and only if \tilde{E}=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\}=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)\in\tilde{V}\} .




Proof.



We omit the proof, as this claim follows straightforwardly from the definition of a Lie subalgebra and the construction rules for graphs associated with graph-admissible Lie algebras. The interested reader may verify this by checking that the closure under the Lie bracket corresponds precisely to the inclusion of all edges whose source, label and endpoint lie within \tilde{V} .
∎





A first result connecting ideals of a Lie algebra with the associated graph is presented in the following claim:



Lemma 70.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . If W\subseteq V is a subset such that no edge e\in E points from a vertex w\in W to a vertex v\in V\setminus W , then W spans an ideal of \mathfrak{g} .




Proof.



Let G(V,E) be a graph associated with a finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , and let W\subseteq V . Suppose that every edge e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in W also satisfies \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in W . This implies that for every w\in W and v\in V , the Lie bracket [w,v]=\kappa_{wv}\tilde{w} holds for some scalar \kappa_{wv}\in\mathbb{F} and \tilde{w}\in W . Therefore, for arbitrary linear combinations




 \displaystyle\left[\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}w,\sum_{v\in V}\lambda_{v}v\right]=\sum_{w\in W}\sum_{v\in V}\lambda_{w}\lambda_{v}\kappa_{wv}\tilde{w}\in\spn\{W\}\qquad\text{ for all }\qquad\lambda_{v},\lambda_{w}\in\mathbb{F}. 



This shows that [\spn\{W\},\spn\{W\}]\subseteq[\spn\{V\},\spn\{W\}]\subseteq\spn\{W\} and hence \spn\{W\}=\langle W\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} as well as [\mathfrak{g},\langle W\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}]\subseteq\langle W\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , demonstrating that \langle W\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is indeed an ideal of \mathfrak{g} .
∎





This lemma inspires us to formally define the following useful graphical concept in order to facilitate obtaining subsequent results:



Definition 71.



Let G(V,E) be a labeled directed graph associated with a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . A subset W\subseteq V is said to satisfy the ideal-graph-property if and only if no edge e\in E points from a vertex w\in W to a vertex v\in V\setminus W .




Lemma 72.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the minimal graph G(V,E) . If \mathfrak{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} is an ideal and \mathfrak{i}=\spn\{W\} for some W\subseteq V , then W satisfies the ideal-graph-property.




Proof.



Let W=\{v_{k}\}_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\subseteq V=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} be a basis of an ideal \mathfrak{i}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} , where G(V,E) is a minimal graph associated with the finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , and \mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{N} . Then, [v_{j},v_{k}]=\alpha_{jk}v_{\delta(j,k)} for all j\in\mathcal{N} and k\in\mathcal{K} . Since \mathfrak{i} is an ideal of \mathfrak{g} , it must be closed under the Lie bracket with all elements of \mathfrak{g} , which implies \delta(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{K})\subseteq\mathcal{K}\cup\{0\} . Thus, every edge e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in W also satisfies \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in W . Therefore, W satisfies the ideal-graph-property as defined in Definition 71.
∎




Lemma 73.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . If V contains no proper non-empty subset V^{\prime}\subsetneq V that satisfies the ideal-graph-property, and \dim(\mathfrak{g})\geq 2 , then, for every pair of vertices v,v^{\prime}\in V , there exists a directed walk W that starts at v and ends at v^{\prime} .




Proof.



Let v\in V be an arbitrary but fixed vertex, and define the set




 \displaystyle R_{v}:=\left\{w\in V\,\middle\mid\,\exists\,\text{directed walk in $G(V,E)$ starting at $v$ and ending at $w$}\right\}\cup\{v\}. 



Let e\in E be such that w:=\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in R_{v} . By definition there exists a directed walk W in G(V,E) that starts at v and ends at w . Appending e to this walk shows that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in R_{v} . Thus R_{v} satisfies the ideal-graph-property. Since v\in R_{v} , it must hold that R_{v}=V , as we assume that V contains no proper non-empty subset that satisfies the ideal-graph-property. But this also implies that each u\in V can be reached from v by following a directed walk W in G(V,E) . The claim thus follows immediately since v was arbitrary.
∎




Corollary 74.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) , and suppose \dim(\mathfrak{g})\geq 2 . Then, the vertex set V contains no proper non-empty subset W\subsetneq V that satisfies the ideal-graph-property if and only if there exists a closed directed walk C that induces the subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) , where \tilde{V}=V .




Proof.



Suppose there exists no proper non-empty subset W\subsetneq V=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} that satisfies the ideal-graph-property. Then, by Lemma 73, for every pair (j,k)\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N} , there exists a directed walk W_{jk}=(v_{j},e_{1}^{(jk)},v_{1}^{(jk)},\ldots,e_{s_{jk}}^{(jk)},v_{k}) that starts at v_{j} and ends at v_{k} . Using these walks, we can construct the closed directed walk




 \displaystyle C=(v_{1},e_{1}^{(12)},v_{1}^{(12)},\ldots,e_{s_{12}}^{(12)},v_{2},e_{1}^{(23)},\ldots,e_{s_{n-1,n}}^{(n-1,n)},v_{n},e_{1}^{(n,1)},v_{1}^{(n,1)},\ldots,e_{s_{n,1}}^{(n,1)},v_{1}), 



which induces the graph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) , where \tilde{V}=V .




Now suppose there exists a closed directed walk C=(v_{j_{1}}^{(1)},e_{1},v_{j_{2}}^{(2)},\ldots,e_{s},v_{j_{1}}^{(1)}) that induces the subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with \tilde{V}=V . Let W\subseteq V be a non-empty subset that satisfies the ideal-graph-property. Then there exists at least one vertex v_{1}\in W\cap\tilde{V}=W . Without loss of generality, let us focus on v_{1}=v_{j_{1}}^{(1)} . Since C is a closed directed walk that visits every vertex w\in V at least once, and \dim(\mathfrak{g})\geq 2 , there must exist an edge e\in\tilde{E}\subseteq E , such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v_{1} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\neq v_{1} . Iterating this process by replacing v with \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) yields eventually the set S=\{v_{1},\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e),\ldots\} . If S\neq V , then there must exist an edge e^{\prime}\in\tilde{E}\subseteq E , such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})\in S and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime})\notin S . This allows us to add the vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) to S and repeat the process. Importantly, since W satisfies the ideal-graph-property, one always has S\subseteq W . Due to the finiteness of V , this process must eventually terminate with S=W=V .
This shows that no proper non-empty subsets of V satisfy the ideal-graph-property, completing this proof.
∎





Instead of considering only closed directed walks that visit every vertex at least once, one can immediately conclude the following:



Corollary 75.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra with \dim(\mathfrak{g})\geq 2 , associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then, the vertex set V contains no proper non-empty subset W\subsetneq V that satisfies the ideal-graph-property if and only if there exists a closed directed walk C that induces the graph G_{C}\equiv G(V,E) itself.




Proof.



This result follows directly from the observation that the existence of a closed directed walk C that induces a subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with \tilde{V}=V is equivalent to the existence of a closed directed walk C^{\prime} that induces the entire graph G_{C^{\prime}}\equiv G(V,E) itself. The necessary condition of this claim is trivially satisfied: If a closed directed walk C induces the full graph G(V,E) , then clearly V=\tilde{V} .




Now, let us continue establishing the sufficient condition. Suppose there exists a closed directed walk C=(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},\ldots,e_{s},v_{1}) that induces a subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with V=\tilde{V} . If E\neq\tilde{E} , then there exists some e\in E\setminus\tilde{E} . Since V=\tilde{V} , one has \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in V , and both vertices are already visited by the walk C . We can now construct the closed directed walk C^{\prime} as follows: First, one repeats the walk C , then follows C again until reaching the vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e) . There, one traverses the edge e , such that one arrives at the vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . Then, one continues along the walk C , starting from \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) , until returning to the starting vertex v_{1} . This extended walk then induces the subgraph G_{C^{\prime}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) , where V=\tilde{V}^{\prime} and \tilde{E}^{\prime}=\tilde{E}\cup\{e\} . Repeating this procedure for every edge e^{\prime}\in E\setminus\tilde{E} , will eventually incorporate all edges e\in E into the extended walk. The resulting walk C^{\prime\prime} then induces the full graph G(V,E) itself, completing the proof.
∎





We now formalize a process for identifying ideals within finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebras by leveraging the structure of their associated labeled directed graphs. The following Algorithm 4 provides a systematic method for detecting all subsets of vertices that satisfy the ideal-graph-property, and consequently span ideals. This procedure is particularly useful for minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras associated with minimal graphs, as each resulting vertex set corresponds to a basis of the corresponding ideal.


Algorithm 4 Identifying ideals of a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra



Data: A labeled directed graph G(V,E) associated with the graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} .


Result: A set \mathcal{F} of ideals of \mathfrak{g} , represented as subspaces spanned by subsets of the vertex set V .



 1
 \mathcal{I} \leftarrow \{\emptyset,V\} 
/* Initialize the set \mathcal{I} with the generator-sets of the trivial ideals: the zero ideal and the full Lie algebra */



 2
 \mathcal{C} \leftarrow V 
/* Initialize the set of candidate starting vertices */



 3
 foreach vertex c\in\mathcal{C} do


   
 4
 \mathfrak{i} \leftarrow \{c\} /* Initialize the current ideal basis candidate \mathfrak{i} as the singleton \{c\} */


   
 5
 \mathfrak{i}_{\mathrm{new}} \leftarrow \{c\} /* Track newly added vertices in the current iteration */


   
 6
 while there exists an edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in\mathfrak{i} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\notin\mathfrak{i} do


      
 7
 \mathfrak{i}_{\mathrm{new}}^{\prime}\leftarrow\emptyset /* Reset the set \mathfrak{i}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{new}} collecting new vertices to be added */


      
 8
 foreach vertex w\in\mathfrak{i}_{\mathrm{new}} and e\in E do


         
 9
 if \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=w and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\notin\mathfrak{i} then


            
 10
 \mathfrak{i}_{\mathrm{new}}^{\prime} \leftarrow \mathfrak{i}_{\mathrm{new}}^{\prime}\cup\{\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\} /* Add the target vertex of the edge to the ideal candidates */



      
 11
 \mathfrak{i} \leftarrow \mathfrak{i}\cup\mathfrak{i}_{\mathrm{new}}^{\prime} /* Update the ideal basis candidate \mathfrak{i} with newly added vertices */


      
 12
 \mathfrak{i}_{\mathrm{new}} \leftarrow \mathfrak{i}_{\mathrm{new}}^{\prime} /* Prepare for the next iteration */


   
 13
 \mathcal{I} \leftarrow \mathcal{I}\cup\{\mathfrak{i}\} /* Add the complete ideal basis candidate to the set of ideals */


   
 14
 if there exists a closed directed walk W=(w_{1},e_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{p},e_{p},w_{1}) such that \bigcup_{j=1}^{p}\{w_{j}\}=\mathfrak{i} then


      
 15
 \mathcal{C} \leftarrow \mathcal{C}\setminus\mathfrak{i} /* Remove all vertices in \mathfrak{i} from the candidate set if they form a closed directed walk */



   
 16
 else


      
 17
 \mathcal{C} \leftarrow \mathcal{C}\setminus\{c\} /* Remove only the starting vertex if no such closed directed walk exists */




 18
 \mathcal{F} \leftarrow \emptyset 
/* Initialize the set that contains all ideals */



 19
 foreach subcollection \mathfrak{i}=\{\mathfrak{i}_{1},\mathfrak{i}_{2},\ldots,\mathfrak{i}_{s}\}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{I}) do


   
 20
 \mathcal{F} \leftarrow \mathcal{F}\cup\{\spn\{\mathfrak{i}_{1}\cup\mathfrak{i}_{2}\cup\ldots\cup\mathfrak{i}_{s}\}\} /* Add the span of the union of subsets as an ideal */




 21
 return \mathcal{F} /* Return the complete set of identified ideals */









Lemma 76.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then Algorithm 4 identifies precisely the ideals of \mathfrak{g} of the form \mathfrak{h}=\mathrm{span}\{W\}{} , where W\subseteq V satisfies the ideal-graph-property.




Proof.



We must show two claims:

(1) 



Every subalgebra \mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} computed by Algorithm 4 is spanned by a subset W\subseteq V that satisfies the ideal-graph-property.

(2) 



For every subset W\subseteq V satisfying the ideal-graph-property, the ideal \mathrm{span}\{W\}{} is computed by Algorithm 4.



Let us begin with (1). Suppose \mathfrak{h}\subseteq\mathfrak{g} is obtained via the application of Algorithm 4, where \mathfrak{g} is associated with the graph G(V,E) . Then, by construction, \mathfrak{h} is spanned by a vertex set W\subseteq V that is the union of several vertex sets W_{1},\ldots,W_{s} , i.e., \bigcup_{j=1}^{s}W_{j}=W (cf. lines 19-20). Henceforth, W\subseteq V , since the sets W_{j} are iteratively constructed from a starting vertex c\in V (cf. line 4), by collecting all vertices reachable via directed edges originating from c , i.e., all \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in V for each e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=c (cf. line 9), and repeating this procedure for the newly added vertices (cf. lines 6-12).




Now, assume by contradiction that there exists a vertex w\in W and an edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=w and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\notin W . Without loss of generality, let w\in W_{1} . This then contradicts the update rule (cf. lines 9-10) of Algorithm 4, which guarantees that any such target vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) would have been added to W_{1} during the iteration and consequently also been added to W . Therefore any edge e\in E originating from a vertex w\in W does not point outside of W , and W satisfies the ideal-graph-property. Since W\subseteq V and the Lie bracket of any element in W with any element in V remains in \spn\{W\} , it follows, by Lemma 70, that [\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{g}]\subseteq\mathfrak{h} , and hence [\mathfrak{h},\mathfrak{h}]\subseteq\mathfrak{h} . Thus, \mathfrak{h} is an ideal and a subalgebra of \mathfrak{g} .




Let us proceed with (2), and let \mathfrak{h} be a subalgebra of \mathfrak{g} spanned by a subset W\subseteq V that satisfies the ideal-graph-property. The trivial case W=\emptyset is immediate. This allows us to write: W=\{w_{j}\}_{j=1}^{r}\subseteq\{w_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}=V for 1\leq r\leq m . For each w_{j}\in W define the recursively constructed sets




 \displaystyle V_{j}^{(0)}:=\{w_{j}\}\qquad V_{j}^{(\ell+1)}:=\left\{v\in V\setminus\bigcup_{k=0}^{\ell}V_{j}^{(k)}\,\middle|\,\exists\,e\in E\text{ such that }\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\in V_{j}^{(\ell)}\text{ and }\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v\right\}\quad\text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}. 

(17)


These sets collect all vertices reachable from w_{j} via a directed walk of length \ell , but by no shorter directed walk. The following observations hold:

(a) 



One has V_{j}^{(\ell)}\cap V_{j}^{(k)}=\emptyset if \ell\neq k .

(b) 



If V_{j}^{(\ell)}=\emptyset , then V_{j}^{(k)}=\emptyset for all k\geq\ell .

(c) 



Since V is finite, one must have that V_{j}^{(\ell)}=\emptyset for all \ell\geq|V| .

(d) 



Every vertex w\in V for which there exists a directed walk starting at w_{j} and ending at w belongs to the set W_{j}^{(\infty)}:=\bigcup_{\ell=0}^{\infty}V_{j}^{(\ell)} : To see this, let W^{\prime}=(w_{j},e_{1},v_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{s},w) be such a directed walk connecting w_{j} and w . Then, clearly, v_{k}\in W_{j}^{(k)}:=\bigcup_{\ell=0}^{k}V_{j}^{(\ell)} for all k\in\{1,\ldots,s\} , and consequently w\in W_{j}^{(s)}\subseteq W_{j}^{(\infty)} .

(e) 



For every vertex w\in V_{j}^{(\ell)} with \ell\geq 1 , there exists a directed trail that starts at w_{j} and ends at w , which is also a directed path. This follows from the recursive construction of the sets V_{j}^{(\ell)} , and can be shown by induction on \ell . The base case \ell=1 is immediate from the definition, and the induction step follows by chaining edges and vertices from earlier levels.






Suppose now that for some j\in\{1,\ldots,r\} , one has w\in W_{j}^{(\infty)} . Then there exists an integer \ell\in\{0,\ldots,|V|-1\} such that w\in V_{j}^{(\ell)} . If \ell=0 , then w_{j}=w\in W . If \ell\geq 1 , then by the previous observations (a)-(e), there exists a directed walk W^{\prime\prime}=(w_{j},e_{j_{0}},w_{j_{1}},\ldots,w_{j_{\ell-1}},e_{j_{\ell-1}},w) such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j_{k}})=w_{j_{k}}\in V_{j}^{(k)} , \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{j_{k}})=w_{j_{k+1}}\in V_{j}^{(k+1)} , \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{j_{0}})=w_{j} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{j_{\ell-1}})=w .
Since w_{j}\in W and W satisfies the ideal-graph-property, all vertices along this walk w_{j_{1}},\ldots,w_{j_{\ell-1}},w , in particular w , must belong to W . Hence, W_{j}^{(\infty)}\subseteq W for all j\in\{1,\ldots,r\} .




Following Algorithm 4, the set \mathcal{I} generally contains the sets W_{j}^{(\infty)} . That is, \{W_{j}^{(\infty)}\}_{j\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}}\subseteq\mathcal{I} (cf. line 13), where \tilde{\mathcal{M}}\subseteq\mathcal{M} indexes the starting vertices used in the algorithm (cf. line 4). All j\in\mathcal{M} are included in \tilde{\mathcal{M}} , except for those j^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M} for which there exists a closed directed walk C^{\prime}=(w_{p_{0}},e_{p_{0}},w_{p_{1}},\ldots w_{p_{s}},e_{p_{s}},w_{p_{0}}) such that \bigcup_{k=0}^{s}\{w_{p_{k}}\}=W_{j}^{(\infty)} for some j=p_{0} with W_{j}^{(\infty)}\in\mathcal{I} , and j^{\prime}\in\{p_{k}\}_{k=1}^{s} (cf. lines 14-15). In such cases, the algorithm avoids recomputing the same ideal multiple times by removing redundant starting vertices.




Suppose now that for some k\in\{1,\ldots,r\} , the set W_{k}^{(\infty)}\notin\mathcal{I} . Then, by the logic of Algorithm 4, there exists some j\in\mathcal{M} and a closed directed walk C^{\prime\prime}=(w_{k_{0}},e_{k_{0}},w_{k_{1}},\ldots,e_{k_{s}},w_{k_{0}}) such that k_{0}=j , and \bigcup_{q=0}^{s}\{w_{k_{q}}\}=W_{j}^{(\infty)} with k=k_{q} for some q\in\{1,\ldots,s\} . We now show that in this case, W_{j}^{(\infty)}=W_{k}^{(\infty)} . Let w\in W_{j}^{(\infty)} . If w=w_{j} , then the segment of the closed directed walk C^{\prime\prime} that starts at w_{k} and ends at w_{j} provides a directed walk from w_{k} to w_{j} , showing that w_{j}\in W_{k}^{(\infty)} . If w\neq w_{j} , then there exists a directed walk W^{\prime\prime\prime} that starts at w_{j} and ends at w . This walk can be adapted such that it starts at w_{k} instead, passes through w_{j} , and ends at w , by prepending the segment of C^{\prime\prime} that starts at w_{k} and ends at w_{j} . Hence, w\in W_{k}^{(\infty)} . By the same argument, any w\in W_{k}^{(\infty)} also belongs to W_{j}^{(\infty)} , since C^{\prime\prime} is a closed directed walk. We conclude: W_{k}^{(\infty)}=W_{j}^{(\infty)} . Therefore, even if for some k\in\{1,\ldots,r\} the set W_{k}^{(\infty)} does not belong to \mathcal{I} via a construction starting at the vertex w_{k} , it is represented via W_{j}^{(\infty)} for some j\in\mathcal{M} ; thus, all such sets are accounted for.




We now conclude by showing that the full set W is recovered as the union of all reachable sets: W=\bigcup_{k=1}^{r}W_{k}^{(\infty)} . The inclusion W\subseteq\bigcup_{k=1}^{r}W_{k}^{(\infty)} is trivial, since W=\{w_{k}\}_{k=1}^{r} and \{w_{k}\}=W_{k}^{(0)}\subseteq W_{k}^{(\infty)} . Conversely, suppose w\in\bigcup_{k=1}^{r}W_{k}^{(\infty)} . Then, either w=w_{j} for some j\in\{1,\ldots,r\} , or there exists a directed path P=(w_{j},\ldots,w) that is also a directed trail, starting at some w_{j}\in W for some j\in\{1,\ldots,r\} and ending at w . Since W satisfies the ideal-graph-property, all vertices along the directed path P must also belong to W . Hence, w\in W , and we conclude W=\bigcup_{k=1}^{r}W_{k}^{(\infty)} . This shows that W is the union of sets already included in \mathcal{I} , and therefore the ideal \mathfrak{h}=\spn\{W\} is obtained by Algorithm 4 (cf. lines 19-21), completing the proof.
∎





Example: Application of Algorithm 4 to identify ideals


Example 77.

We now demonstrate the application of Algorithm 4 to an exemplary finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , utilizing the graph depicted in Figure 14. In this example, we select two distinct vertices to initiate the algorithm, namely y and p . In both panels of the figure, the respective starting vertex is visually distinguished by coloring it red. During the first iteration of the algorithm, the vertices added to the candidate ideal basis \mathfrak{i} , apart from the initial vertex, are colored blue, along with the edges considered in this step. In the subsequent iteration, additional vertices and edges are added to \mathfrak{i} ; these are colored green to indicate their inclusion in the second step of the expansion process.


Figure 14: Application of Algorithm 4 to identify ideals of a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra using different initial vertices. The ideals are V=\{x,y,h,q,p,z\} in panel (a) and \{q,p,z\} in panel (b). According to [99], the depicted graph can be associated with the Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{1} , where the first ideal would coincide with the complete algebra, i.e., \spn{V}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{1} . The second ideal, on the other hand, represents the radical \spn\{q,p,z\}\cong\mathfrak{h}_{1} , the Heisenberg algebra.


We observe that there is potential to optimize Algorithm 4: For instance, if the vertex p belongs to an ideal of the Lie algebra, then the vertex q must also belong to the same ideal, and vice versa. This follows from the existence of a closed directed walk connecting p and q . Therefore, it is sufficient to include only one of these two vertices in the initial set of candidate vertices \mathcal{C} (cf. line 2). While a related reduction of \mathcal{C} is currently implemented (cf. lines 14-17), the one discussed here is not, but would be a feasible and potentially a beneficial enhancement.






The result above motivates the following classification of vertices and edges of a labeled directed graph G(V,E) , associated with a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , into three distinct structural categories:

• 



L-type (Lake-type): An edge e\in E is classified as L-type if there exists a closed directed walk W=(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},\ldots,e_{s},v_{1}) , such that e\in\tilde{E} , where G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) is the subgraph induced by the walk W . A vertex v\in V is similarly classified L-type if v\in\tilde{V} .

• 



T-type (Tributary-type): A vertex v\in V is classified as T-type if it is not L-type and there exists a directed walk W=(v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},\ldots,e_{s},v_{s+1}) , such that v_{1}=v and v_{s+1} is an L-type vertex. An edge e\in E is classified T-type if it is not L-type and belongs to a directed walk that starts at a T-type vertex and ends at an L-type vertex.

• 



D-type (Disappearing-stream-type): Vertices and edges that are neither L-type nor T-type are classified as D-type.



The terminology used (L-type, T-type, and D-type) is inspired by the classification of water systems in river networks. The underlying idea behind this naming scheme is conceptually simple yet structurally insightful: One can envision the graph as a dynamic system in which water flows along the direction of the edges. In this analogy, vertices represent specific locations in the landscape, while edges represent the flow of water from one vertex to another, just as the Lie bracket operations connect elements of the algebra. This perspective enables the following interpretation of the graph’s structure: L-type elements correspond to lakes [133]. These are regions of the graph where flow circulates. Specifically, a closed directed walk exists, allowing flow to traverse all involved vertices and return to the starting point, similar to circular currents found in lakes or oceans. In contrast, T-type elements represent tributaries [144]. These are vertices or edges that lead into a lake but are not themselves part of any closed walk; they feed into cyclic structures without directly participating in them. Consequently, it is generally impossible to start at one vertex of a tributary and find a directed walk that reaches every other vertex within the same tributary. This behavior is analogous to a river feeding into a lake: drifting along the river following its current does not lead to upstream regions. Vertices classified as D-type can be further subdivided into two subcategories: Either there exists a stream that originates at a tributary or a lake and terminates at a given vertex, making it part of a “distributary” [144], or no such stream exists. These vertices share the common characteristic of belonging to rivers that terminate without ever reaching a lake, thereby forming “disappearing streams” [4]. Accordingly, vertices from which no edges originate can be referred to as sinkholes.




This nomenclature also facilitates the introduction of the notions of upstream and downstream: A vertex v is said to be downstream of a vertex w if there exists a directed walk that starts at w and ends at v , but not vice versa. Analogously, the vertex w is said to be upstream of the vertex v . Clearly, vertices that belong to the same closed directed walk are neither upstream nor downstream of each other. If the need arises, one can further subdivide L-type vertices and edges into distinct lakes, and similarly classify T-type and D-type vertices and edges into distinct rivers. This classification can also aid in easily identifying ideals visually. For instance, a lake together with all its distributaries and downstream vertices typically forms an ideal. Figure 15 visualizes the conceptual framework introduced here.


Figure 15: Schematic representation of the flow-based classification of the graph elements in the context of Lie algebraic structures. Edges are color-coded for visual clarity: L-type edges (blue) represent cyclic flows analogous to lakes; T-type edges (green) correspond to tributaries feeding into closed walks; D-type edges (orange) indicate disappearing streams. Note that the lack of labels is imposed to emphasize flow dynamics, and that the figure serves as a conceptual sketch rather than a representation of a valid graph.




To further illustrate the concept of interpreting edges as streams, we consider the derived series of a Lie algebra. Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the minimal labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then, there exists an integer \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} such that \mathcal{D}^{\ell}V=\mathcal{D}^{\ell+j}V for all j\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . We define the smallest such integer \ell as \ell_{*} . This allows us to introduce the following decomposition of the vertex set V :




 \displaystyle S^{(1)}:=\left\{\begin{matrix}V&\text{, if }\ell_{*}=0\\
V\setminus\mathcal{D}^{1}V&\text{, otherwise}\end{matrix}\right.\qquad\text{and}\qquad S^{(j)}:=\left\{\begin{matrix}V\setminus\left(\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{j-1}S^{(\ell)}\cup\mathcal{D}^{j}V\right)&,\text{ for all }j\in\{2,\ldots,\ell_{*}\}\\
V\setminus\bigcup_{\ell=1}^{j-1}S^{(\ell)}&,\text{ for }j=\ell_{*}+1\end{matrix}\right.. 

(18)


It is evident that the sets S^{(j)} are pairwise disjoint, i.e., S^{(j)}\cap S^{(k)}=\emptyset if j\neq k , and their union reconstructs the entire vertex set, i.e., \bigcup_{\ell=1}^{\ell_{*}+1}S^{(\ell)}=V . Moreover, the vertices in S^{(\ell)} are generally downstream of at least one vertex in some S^{(j)} with j<\ell , but never downstream of any vertex in S^{(k)} for k>\ell . These relationships can be schematically visualized as shown in Figure 16. Specifically, the set S^{(1)} is generally upstream of the sets S^{(2)},\ldots,S^{(\ell_{*}+1)} , and this pattern continues such that S^{(\ell)} is generally upstream of the subsequent sets S^{(j)} with j>\ell , in the sense that there can only exist edges that point from vertices in S^{(\ell)} to S^{(j)} but never in the converse direction. Conversely, the set S^{(\ell_{*}+1)} is not upstream of any other set, but at most downstream of all preceding sets S^{(\ell)} . A similar decomposition can be constructed using other series of ideals, such as the lower central series, by replacing the operation \mathcal{D}^{\ell} with the operation \mathcal{C}^{\ell} .




We can also interpret the graphs through the lens of the Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition theorem, which states that any Lie algebra over a field of characteristic zero can be expressed as the semidirect sum of a semisimple component and its radical [130]. In the graphical representation, the semisimple component is typically depicted as a collection of L-type vertices and edges, since non-solvable Lie algebras must contain a closed directed walk (cf. Theorem 58). The semidirect sum structure manifests then in the observation that the portion of the graph corresponding to the radical is generally positioned downstream from the semisimple part. Furthermore, if the radical is nilpotent, this part will generally be represented by D-type edges and vertices.


Figure 16: Schematic depiction of the relations among the sets S^{(\ell)} , defined via the derived series \mathcal{D}^{\ell}V of the vertices from a minimal graph G(V,E) associated with a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra. This diagram illustrates that edges which originate at a vertex in the set S^{(\ell)} only point to vertices in the sets S^{(j)} with j\geq\ell , but never to vertices in sets S^{(k)} with k<\ell .





IV.5 Criteria for simplicity and semisimplicity




The results of the previous subsection allow us to formulate criteria for simplicity and semisimplicity, both of which fundamentally depend on the structure and existence of ideals within a Lie algebra. Note that, as mentioned earlier, we only consider Lie algebras that are taken over a field of characteristic zero, which provides us with several well-known conditions for semisimplicity [170].




We begin by presenting a necessary criterion, derived from Lemma 70, which applies to minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras. However, it is important to emphasize that the simplicity of a Lie algebra does not imply that its associated graph is a directed simple graph, nor does the converse hold. A directed simple graph is defined as a directed graph that neither contains multiple edges between the same pair of source and target vertices nor loops [183]. A counterexample illustrating the non-equivalence between graph simplicity and algebraic simplicity is the graph associated with the simple Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) depicted in Figure 2 (b). Conversely, the simplicity of a directed graph does not rule out the simplicity of the corresponding Lie algebra. For instance, the graph associated with the simple Lie algebra \mathfrak{su}(2) , shown in Figure 2 (a), is also a simple directed graph. These examples demonstrate that, within this framework, there is no causal or structural correspondence between the simplicity of a graph and the simplicity of the associated Lie algebra.



Lemma 78.



Let \mathfrak{g} be an n -dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the minimal graph G(V,E) , and suppose n\geq 2 . If \mathfrak{g} is simple, then the only subsets W\subseteq V that satisfy the ideal-graph property are W=V and W=\emptyset .




Proof.



Let G(V,E) be a minimal graph associated with the finite-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . First, observe that the sets V and \emptyset both trivially satisfy the ideal-graph-property. Suppose, for contradiction, that there exists a proper non-empty subset W\subsetneq V that satisfies the ideal-graph property. By Lemma 70, the subset W spans an ideal of \mathfrak{g} , which is a proper non-zero ideal, since V is a basis of \mathfrak{g} and W is a proper non-empty subset of V . This contradicts the simplicity of \mathfrak{g} , as defined in Definition 8, and thus concludes this proof.
∎





We now restate Lemma 78 in a way that highlights the structural properties directly observable from the graph associated to the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} :



Proposition 79.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the minimal graph G(V,E) , and suppose \dim(\mathfrak{g})\geq 2 . If \mathfrak{g} is simple, then there exists a closed directed walk W that induces a subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with V=\tilde{V} .




Proof.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a simple finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the minimal graph G(V,E) . Then, by Lemma 78, there exists no proper non-empty subset W\subsetneq V=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} that satisfies the ideal-graph-property. By Corollary 74, it follows that the graph must contain a closed directed walk W that traverses all vertices in V , thereby inducing a subgraph G_{W}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with V=\tilde{V} .
∎





We now introduce a sufficient but weak condition for non-semisimplicity that applies not only to minimal graphs but also to redundant ones. This condition is based on the presence of sinkholes in the associated graph structure.



Proposition 80.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . If G(V,E) contains sinkholes, i.e., vertices v\in V for which there is no edge e\in E with \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v , then \mathfrak{g} is not semisimple.




Proof.



Let v be a sinkhole. Then, the singleton set \{v\} satisfies the ideal-graph-property. By Lemma 70, this implies that \{v\} spans a non-zero ideal of \mathfrak{g} . Moreover, the Lie subalgebra \spn\{v\} is trivially solvable, as it is abelian. Therefore, \mathfrak{g} contains a solvable non-zero ideal, and by Definition 8, it follows that \mathfrak{g} is not semisimple.
∎





We now generalize the criterion provided in Proposition 80 by considering not just sinkholes, but a broader class of vertices, which leads to a more robust condition for detecting non-semisimplicity in graph-admissible Lie algebras.



Proposition 81.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Suppose there exists a proper non-empty subset W\subsetneq V that spans a proper subspace \mathfrak{w}:=\spn\{W\}\subsetneq\mathfrak{g} and satisfies the ideal-graph-property. If there exists no closed directed walk C in G(V,E) that induces a self-contained subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with \tilde{V}\subseteq W , then \mathfrak{g} is not semisimple.




Proof.



By Lemma 70, the proper non-empty subset W\subsetneq V spans an ideal of \mathfrak{g} . This ideal \mathfrak{w}:=\spn\{W\} is, by assumption, a proper non-zero subalgebra of \mathfrak{g} . Suppose there exists no closed directed walk C in G(V,E) that induces a self-contained subgraph G_{C}\equiv G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with \tilde{V}\subseteq W . Then, in particular, there exists also no closed directed walk C^{\prime} in the subgraph G(W,{E}^{\prime}) that induces a self-contained subgraph G_{C^{\prime}}\equiv G(\tilde{V}^{\prime},\tilde{E}^{\prime}) , where {E}^{\prime}:=\{e\in E\,\mid\,\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\in W\} . By Lemma 69, the graph G(W,{E}^{\prime}) is associated with the subalgebra \mathfrak{w} . Then, by Theorem 58, it follows from the absence of a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph that \mathfrak{w} is solvable, making \mathfrak{g} , by Definition 8, not semisimple.
∎





As a complementary condition to Proposition 80 which is based on the presence of sinkholes, we now consider a criterion for non-semisimplicity that relies on the existence of loose ends and isolated vertices, that is, on vertices that are never targets of edges. Note that this includes those vertices that are never sources of any edges.



Proposition 82.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . If the set \tilde{V}:=\{v\in V\,\mid\,\forall\,e\in E:\,\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\neq v\} of loose ends and isolated vertices is such that \mathfrak{v}:=\spn\{V\setminus\tilde{V}\} is a proper non-zero subspace of \mathfrak{g} , then \mathfrak{g} is not semisimple.





This also implies that the existence of loose ends in a minimal graph implies that the associated Lie algebra is not semisimple.



Proof.



Lemma 52 informs us that applying Algorithm 2 to the graph G(V,E) yields the (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{D}^{1}V of the first derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} . The basis is given by \mathcal{D}^{1}V=V\setminus\tilde{V} , and therefore spans a proper subspace of \mathfrak{g} . Since \mathfrak{g}\neq\mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} , it follows from Corollary 2 in [170, page 45] that \mathfrak{g} is not semisimple.
∎





The criteria developed for semisimple Lie algebras can be naturally extended to reductive Lie algebras. Recall that the first derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} of a reductive Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is semisimple, and it decomposes as \mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) [126]. Consequently, one can employ Algorithm 2 to obtain the first derived graph \mathcal{D}^{1}G(V,E) associated with the first derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{1}\mathfrak{g} of a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} and its associated graph G(V,E) . Applying the previously established conditions for semisimplicity to the graph \mathcal{D}^{1}G(V,E) , and finding that it does not correspond to a graph of a semisimple Lie algebra, one can conclude that \mathfrak{g} is not reductive.




Note that we can also deduce local criteria for semisimplicity, i.e., criteria that depend on the existence of special subgraphs, which are based on considerations involving the Killing form. For this see Proposition 108 in Appendix H.




We want to conclude this section with a conjecture that proposes a strong condition for semisimplicity, based on the associated graphs.



Conjecture 83.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with a minimal labeled directed graph G(V,E) . Then \mathfrak{g} is semisimple if and only if every vertex is part of a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph of G(V,E) .







V Prominent examples




We present here several examples of graph-admissible Lie algebras that play a significant role in many areas of physics, including general relativity and quantum mechanics. These examples are analyzed utilizing the graph-theoretic framework developed in this work.
