Our objective is to underscore the practical utility of the proposed methods and to demonstrate that their strength lies not only in computational applicability but also in their capability to visually represent the structural properties, thereby facilitating deeper structural insight. This graphical perspective can be particularly advantageous for those encountering these algebraic concepts for the first time.




V.1 The Schrödinger algebra




We begin by discussing the real Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} , where \mathfrak{h}_{m} denotes the real (2m+1) -dimensional Heisenberg algebra and \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) the real three-dimensional special linear algebra [91, 131]. For m=1 , this algebra is physically particularly relevant as it is associated with the symmetries of the Schrödinger equation [5]. Moreover, it represents the largest non-solvable Lie algebra that can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{1} —the algebra of all skew-hermitian polynomials made from the bosonic creation and annihilation operators of one mode [99]. The term “Schrödinger algebra” used to describe the Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} is somewhat ambiguous, as it is often used to refer to different and distinct Lie algebras [99, 73, 150, 75, 5, 174]. However, for the simplest case m=1 , these definitions all coincide. There are generally two approaches to extending this basic Schrödinger algebra. Both stem from the observation that the fundamental Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{1} encodes the symmetries of the free Schrödinger equation in a (1+1) -dimensional flat spacetime [136]. Thus, a natural extension of this algebra is to consider the groups associated with the symmetries of the Schrödinger equation in an (n+1) -dimensional flat spacetime [150, 75, 5, 136]. To distinguish these and related algebras, they are sometimes referred to as the extended or super Schrödinger algebra. Another approach is to extend the dimension of the Heisenberg algebra, i.e., by replacing the algebra \mathfrak{h}_{1} in the first Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{1} with the higher-dimensional Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{m} [174], or similar extensions [136].




The Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} is defined over \mathbb{R} with basis elements \mathcal{B}=\{h,x,y,q_{j},p_{j},z\,\mid\,j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}\} , and Lie bracket relations given by




 \displaystyle[h,x] 
 \displaystyle=2x,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[h,y] 
 \displaystyle=-2y,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x,y] 
 \displaystyle=h,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[h,q_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=q_{j}, 



 \displaystyle[y,q_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=p_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[h,p_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=-p_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x,p_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=q_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[q_{j},p_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}z, 

(19)


while all other Lie brackets vanish [174]. Note that the subalgebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) is spanned by the three elements h,x,y , while the Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{m} is spanned by the remaining elements q_{1},p_{1},\ldots,q_{m},p_{m},z . This algebra is physically relevant because it can be faithfully realized within the Weyl algebra A_{m} , which is used to describe all bosonic polynomial Hamiltonians [187, 42] (see Appendix F for more details). The basis choice in (19) clearly implies that the algebra is minimal-graph-admissible, and its associated graph for m=2 is depicted in Figure 17.


Figure 17: Depiction of the minimal graph associated with the Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{2} , using the basis given in (19).




From this graph, we observe that this algebra is not solvable, as established by the criteria in Theorem 58: the subgraph with blue edges and vertices contains a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph. Furthermore, according to Proposition 80, the algebra is also not semisimple and therefore not simple, since the vertex z acts as a sinkhole (and thus it spans a nontrivial ideal of the full algebra).




This structure adheres to the Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition theorem, which states that any Lie algebra is the semi-direct sum of a semisimple Lie algebra and its radical [130]. In this case, the semisimple component is \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) and the radical is \mathfrak{h}_{2} . The graph illustrates this decomposition: vertices and edges in green correspond to basis elements from \mathfrak{h}_{2} , while those in blue represent elements from \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) . Dashed orange edges indicate non-vanishing brackets between elements of the semisimple part and the radical, thereby encoding the semidirect sum. Notably, the subgraph associated with \mathfrak{h}_{2} satisfies the ideal-graph-property, and its structure confirms that \mathfrak{h}_{2} is solvable and, in fact, nilpotent, as there exists no closed directed walk within this subgraph (cf. Theorem 66). It should therefore be evident that the graph-theoretic framework provides a clear visualization of the Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition.





V.2 The Lie algebra of the Lorentz group




A second example we want to discuss is the complexified Lie algebra of the Lorentz group, denoted by \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}} [171], and hereafter referred to as the Lorentz algebra for convenience. This algebra is of paramount importance to quantum field theory [173] and general relativity [176, 49] as it encodes symmetries of Minkowski spacetime (i.e., flat spacetime). This algebra is generated by six elements: three boost operators K_{1},K_{2},K_{3} , and the three angular momentum operators J_{1},J_{2},J_{3} . Their Lie bracket relations are given by




 \displaystyle[J_{j},J_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}J_{\ell},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[J_{j},K_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}K_{\ell},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[K_{j},K_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=-i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}J_{\ell}, 



where \varepsilon_{jk\ell} denotes the completely antisymmetric Levi-Civita symbol [173]. This algebra is therefore minimal-graph-admissible, and its corresponding graph G_{1}(V_{1},E_{1}) is depicted in Figure 18 (a). The graph representation provides an intuitive visualization of the corresponding non-abelian structure: The presence of closed directed walks that induce self-contained subgraphs indicates non-solvability. Nevertheless, this example illustrates that Proposition 79 offers only a necessary condition, not a sufficient one: although the graph G_{1}(V_{1},E_{1}) contains a closed directed walk that induces the entire graph G_{1}(V_{1},E_{1}) itself, the algebra \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}} is only semisimple, not simple.


Figure 18: Panel (a): Depiction of the graph G_{1}(V_{1},E_{1}) associated with the complex Lorentz algebra \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}} , constructed using the basis \{J_{j},K_{j}\}_{j=1}^{3} corresponding to the angular momentum and boost operators. Panel (b): Depiction of the graph G_{2}(V_{2},E_{2}) associated with the complex Lorentz algebra \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}} , constructed using the basis \{N_{j}^{(1)},N_{j}^{(2)}\}_{j=1}^{3} , where N_{j}^{(1)}=J_{j}-iK_{j} and N_{j}^{(2)}=J_{j}+iK_{j} . This representation reveals the decomposition \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}) , as the graph splits into two unconnected components. Although the physical interpretation of these new generators is obscure [173], the graph-theoretic perspective makes the algebraic structure explicit.




To reveal its deeper structure, we can construct a second graph that can be associated with \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}} . To this end, we perform a change of basis by introducing the elements N_{j}^{(1)}:=J_{j}-iK_{j} and N_{j}^{(2)}:=J_{j}+iK_{j} for j\in\{1,2,3\} . These elements satisfy the following Lie brackets:




 \displaystyle[N_{j}^{(1)},N_{k}^{(1)}] 
 \displaystyle=2i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}N_{\ell}^{(1)},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[N_{j}^{(2)},N_{k}^{(2)}] 
 \displaystyle=2i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}N_{\ell}^{(2)},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[N_{j}^{(1)},N_{k}^{(2)}] 
 \displaystyle=0. 



This decomposition shows that \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}) , as noted in [94], and is a demonstration of Proposition 30, since the associated graph G_{2}(V_{2},E_{2}) now splits into two unconnected components, as depicted in Figure 18 (b).




Importantly, regardless of the chosen basis \{J_{j},K_{j}\}_{j=1}^{3} or \{N_{j}^{(1)},N_{j}^{(2)}\}_{j=1}^{3} , every vertex belongs to a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph, supporting Conjecture 83, which asserts that every minimal graph associated with a semisimple Lie algebra exhibits this property. Furthermore, by Proposition 79, we can confirm that the Lorentz algebra is not simple, as the graph G_{2}(V_{2},E_{2}) in Figure 18 (b) does not contain a single closed directed walk encompassing all vertices of the original graph G_{2}(V_{2},E_{2}) .




The Lorentz algebra can be extended to the Poincaré algebra by adding the energy and momentum operators. For a further discussion on this topic see Appendix G, where we also consider the Galilei algebra, which can be obtained as the non-relativistic limit of the Poincaré algebra.





V.3 Graphical representation for the search of finite-dimensional physically relevant Lie algebras




One of the main goals of the approach presented in this work is to apply graph-based representations to Lie algebras that arise in a physically relevant context. One such context is that of the Lie algebras generated by the defining operators of Hamiltonians, which are hermitian operators that describe the dynamics of coupled bosonic systems. This avenue has been the object of study of a seminal work [42], further applied to the single mode case in subsequent work [99]. There, the focus is on the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{n} of n modes, which consists of all skew-hermitian polynomials in the complex Weyl algebra A_{n} . The complex Weyl algebra, in turn, plays a central role in the description of bosonic systems [187]. It is generated by the creation and annihilation operators \hat{a}_{k}^{\dagger} and \hat{a}_{k} that satisfy the canonical commutation relations [\hat{a}_{k},\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\ell}]=\delta_{k\ell} , while all others vanish. In this context, the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g}:=\langle\{\hat{a}_{1},\ldots,\hat{a}_{n},\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{1},\ldots,\hat{a}_{n}^{\dagger},1\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is the (2n+1) -dimensional Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{n} . Consequently, the Weyl algebra A_{n} can be identified with the universal enveloping algebra of \mathfrak{h}_{n} , provided the central basis element is chosen to be the identity, i.e., A_{n}=U(\mathfrak{h}_{n})/\langle z-1\rangle , where z denotes the central basis element of the abstract Heisenberg algebra [86]. Since bosonic systems are governed by Hamiltonian operators, which are defined in this context as hermitian operators in A_{n} , the operators of interest belong to a specific subspace of the Weyl algebra: the real hermitian Weyl space, which is isomorphic to the real skew-hermitian Weyl algebra. Hermiticity can, for simplicity in this context, be formalized by introducing the conjugate-linear anti-automorphism (\cdot)^{\dagger}:A_{n}\to A_{n} with action (\cdot)^{\dagger}:p\mapsto p^{\dagger} that satisfies the following properties: 1^{\dagger}=1 , (\hat{a}_{k})^{\dagger}=\hat{a}_{k}^{\dagger} , (\hat{a}_{k}^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=\hat{a}_{k} , i^{\dagger}=-i , and the order of multiplication is reversed, i.e., (\hat{p}_{1}\hat{p}_{2})^{\dagger}=\hat{p}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{p}_{1}^{\dagger} . An operator \hat{O} is hermitian if it obeys \hat{O}^{\dagger}=\hat{O} , while a skew-hermitian operator \hat{O} satisfies \hat{O}^{\dagger}=-\hat{O} .




The central question posed in the literature [42], which we address here from a motivational and outlook perspective, is


Q: Given a Hamiltonian \hat{H}(t)=i\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}u_{j}(t)\hat{g}_{j} , where u_{j}(t) are real-valued time-dependent functions and \hat{g}_{j} are time-independent and linearly independent skew-hermitian operators, is the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g}:=\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} generated by the set \mathcal{G}:=\{\hat{g}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}} finite dimensional? 


This question is of profound importance because its answer determines whether the dynamics of the quantum system can be effectively characterized or even controlled via a finite number of degrees of freedom [42]. Such considerations are central to quantum optimal control [36, 108], a rapidly growing field driven by the recent advances in quantum information science [1, 185, 122].




In order to connect this aforementioned avenue with our work on graphical representations of Lie algebras, we now consider the prototypical Hamiltonian




 \displaystyle\hat{H}(t)=\omega_{1}(t)\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1}+\omega_{2}(t)\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}+g_{2}(t)\left(\hat{a}_{1}^{2}+(\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger})^{2}\right)+i(\hat{a}_{1}-\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger})\left(g_{1}(t)+g_{3}(t)\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}+g_{4}(t)(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{2}\hat{a}_{2}^{2}\right), 



which models an optomechanical system [132] that has been extended by the addition of nonlinear interaction terms. Optomechanical systems are paradigmatic models of the interaction between the radiation pressure of photons and one or more vibrating mirrors in a cavity [17], and they are now the subject of great theoretical and experimental efforts due to their potential as core components of future quantum technologies [25, 12, 162]. The set of generators \mathcal{G} of \hat{H}(t) is given by




 \displaystyle\mathcal{G}:=\left\{i\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1},i\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2},(\hat{a}_{1}-\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}),i\left(\hat{a}_{1}^{2}+(\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger})^{2}\right),(\hat{a}_{1}-\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger})\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2},(\hat{a}_{1}-\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger})(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{2}\hat{a}_{2}^{2}\right\}. 



We can apply Theorem 54 of the cited work [42] to confirm that the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g}:=\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is finite dimensional. A direct computation shows that \dim(\mathfrak{g})=14 . A convenient (overcomplete) basis is:




 \displaystyle a 
 \displaystyle:=i\left(\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\right),\; 
 \displaystyle\;b 
 \displaystyle:=\hat{a}_{1}^{2}-(\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger})^{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;c 
 \displaystyle:=i\left(\hat{a}_{1}^{2}+(\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger})^{2}\right), 



 \displaystyle p_{1} 
 \displaystyle:=\hat{a}_{1}-\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger},\; 
 \displaystyle\;q_{1} 
 \displaystyle:=i\left(\hat{a}_{1}+\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\right),\; 
 \displaystyle\;z_{1} 
 \displaystyle:=i, 



 \displaystyle p_{2} 
 \displaystyle:=\left(\hat{a}_{1}-\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\right)\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;q_{2} 
 \displaystyle:=i\left(\hat{a}_{1}+\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\right)\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;z_{2} 
 \displaystyle:=i(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{2}\hat{a}_{2}^{2}+i\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2}, 



 \displaystyle p_{3} 
 \displaystyle:=\left(\hat{a}_{1}-\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\right)(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{2}\hat{a}_{2}^{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;q_{3} 
 \displaystyle:=i\left(\hat{a}_{1}+\hat{a}_{1}^{\dagger}\right)(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{2}\hat{a}_{2}^{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;z_{3} 
 \displaystyle:=i(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{4}\hat{a}_{2}^{4}+4i(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{3}\hat{a}_{2}^{3}+2i(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{2}\hat{a}_{2}^{2}, 



 \displaystyle y_{1} 
 \displaystyle:=i(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{2}\hat{a}_{2}^{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;y_{2} 
 \displaystyle:=i\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;y_{3} 
 \displaystyle:=i(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{3}\hat{a}_{2}^{3}+2i(\hat{a}_{2}^{\dagger})^{2}\hat{a}_{2}^{2}. 



Computing the Lie brackets confirms that this algebra is graph-admissible. However, because certain elements (e.g.,  z_{2} ) can be expressed as a linear combination of others (such as y_{1} and y_{2} ), the associated graph G(V,E) is redundant. Figure 19 gives an intuitive illustration of this graph.


Figure 19: Depiction of the redundant graph G(V,E) associated with the fourteen-dimensional Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} and the overcomplete basis \{a,b,c,p_{j},q_{j},z_{j},y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{3} . Blue vertices and edges correspond to the semisimple subalgebra spanned by \{a,b,c\} , while green vertices and edges indicate the radical \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{g})=\langle\{p_{j},q_{j},z_{j},y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{3}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , a two-step nilpotent ideal. Dashed orange edges depict interactions between the semisimple part and the radical, illustrating the Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition as a semidirect sum. The subgraph of the radical \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{g}) exhibits rotational symmetry, suggesting a connection to the alternating/cyclic group A_{3}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{3} .




We now apply the tools developed in this work to analyze the structural properties of the Lie algebra associated with the Hamiltonian above:

(i) 



Non-trivial center. According to Lemma 45, the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} has a non-trivial center, as the vertices labeled by the elements \{y_{j},z_{j}\}_{j=1}^{3} are sinkholes in the associated graph G(V,E) .

(ii) 



Presence of a simple subalgebra. The three vertices \{a,b,c\} are linearly independent. Thus, the blue subgraph in Figure 19 is a proper-minimal graph of Type IX and the corresponding Lie algebra is according to Corollary 42 simple. Moreover, the Lie algebra \spn\{a,b,c\} is isomorphic to \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})  [99].

(iii) 



Non-solvability. The Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is non-solvable, as the associated graph G(V,E) contains a closed directed walk that induces a self-contained subgraph. For instance, starting at vertex a , one traverses the edge labeled by b to reach c , then follows the edge labeled by a to reach b , and finally traverses the edge labeled by c to return to a . By Theorem 58, this confirms the non-solvability of \mathfrak{g} .

(iv) 



Existence of a nilpotent ideal (the radical). Due to Lemma 70, the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} contains an ideal that is spanned by the vertices \{p_{j},q_{j},z_{j},y_{j}\}_{j=1}^{3} , which is highlighted in the graph G(V,E) in Figure 19 by coloring the corresponding edges and vertices green. This ideal is nilpotent, as its associated subgraph contains no closed directed walk (Theorem 66). A close inspection reveals that this ideal is the radical \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{g}) of this Lie algebra. Furthermore, employing Algorithm 3 and Lemma 62 reveals that \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{g}) is a two-step nilpotent Lie algebra.




This observation enables once more an interpretation of the structure of the algebra via the Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition theorem: the semisimple component (indicated with the blue edges and vertices) interacts with its radical (indicated with the green edges and vertices) exclusively through brackets that result in elements from the radical (illustrated with dashed orange edges). This provided a clear graphical illustration of the semidirect sum decomposition that governs the internal structure and the interplay of the two components.

(v) 



Symmetry and conjecture.
The graph of the radical \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{g}) exhibits an intrinsic symmetry: rotations by \frac{2}{3}\pi combined with the corresponding permutations of the labels that leave the graph invariant. Thus, its symmetry group corresponds to that of an equilateral triangle restricted to rotations, suggesting a possible connection between this two-step nilpotent Lie algebra and the dihedral group D_{3} , the smallest non-abelian group [169], and its subgroup \mathbb{Z}_{3}\cong A_{3} [82].

(vi) 



Non-semisimplicity and non-reductiveness. The Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is not semisimple, which is consistent with the presence of a non-trivial radical. Furthermore, by Algorithm 2, the first derived graph \mathcal{D}G(V,E) coincides with the original graph G(V,E) (i.e., there are no loose ends), which implies by Lemma 52 that the first derived algebra \mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g} coincides with the original Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . Since \mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g})\neq\{0\} by item (i), \mathfrak{g} is non-reductive.








VI Discussion




Before concluding we aim to provide a comprehensive discussion of the work presented. Specifically, we wish to emphasize the essential results, highlight the strengths of the proposed approach, and critically examine its limitations and weaknesses. In addition, we outline possible avenues for extending the framework to mitigate these weaknesses while preserving its main advantages. Finally, we identify several interesting open questions that remain unsolved and merit further investigation.




VI.1 Considerations




We begin by highlighting the principal strengths of the proposed framework for associating Lie algebras with labeled directed graphs that encode important structural properties of the algebra.
First, the algorithm for generating a labeled directed graph associated with a given Lie algebra is conceptually simple and easy to implement, provided a suitable basis is available.
Furthermore, the present framework enables the detection of key structural properties of the Lie algebras through a visual method.
In particular, we presented necessary and sufficient criteria for solvability and nilpotency (cf. Theorems 58 and 66, respectively), which are key criteria in the classification of Lie algebras [126].
Moreover, we introduced concrete and straightforward algorithms to construct graphs associated with the Lie algebras of the derived and lower central series of a given algebra (cf. Algorithms 2 and 3, respectively).
We also established the equivalence between the termination of these algebraic series and the corresponding graph sequences (cf. Theorem 54 and Theorem 63, respectively).
In addition, this approach allows for the identification of central elements (cf. Lemma 45) and ideals (cf. Lemma 70), where Algorithm 4 can assist in finding ideals. This framework further allows one to derive multiple conditions to recognize simple, semisimple and reductive Lie algebras (cf. Section IV.5).




These results provide an intuitive way to understand the internal structure of a given Lie algebra, thereby offering significant concrete benefits by presenting a visual characterization that can be understood by experts and non-experts alike.
Furthermore, each criterion discussed above can, in principle, be implemented through straightforward algorithms and therefore readily integrated into computer-based classification methods even in the case of Lie algebras of large dimensions.




Finally, as indicated in the case of the Schrödinger algebra in Section V.1, this graph-based approach allows us to visualize and understand a fundamental structural property of Lie algebras established by the Levi-Mal’tsev theorem: any Lie algebra over a field of characteristic zero decomposes as the semidirect sum of a semisimple subalgebra and its radical [130].
The graph representation not only highlights the structure of the semisimple component and the radical but also reflects the interaction between them induced by the semidirect sum.





VI.2 Weaknesses and limitations




Now, let us discuss the weaknesses and limitations of the proposed approach.
An obvious limitation concerns the range of applicability, as established by Theorem 23, which demonstrates the existence of finite-dimensional non-graph-admissible Lie algebras.
Although, as shown in Section II.1 and Section V, a broad class of physically relevant Lie algebras falls within the graph-admissible category, the restriction to graph-admissible Lie algebras remains significant.
Another weakness arises from the existence of Lie algebras that are not minimal-graph-admissible but only redundant-graph-admissible, as established by Corollary 17.
Graphs associated with redundant-graph-admissible but not minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras, or redundant graphs in general, lack several useful properties that minimal graphs possess, which complicates both the visualization and interpretation of their structure.
For instance, in a minimal graph, the number of vertices coincides with the dimension of the associated Lie algebra. Redundant graphs, on the other hand, require additional vertices, which makes the representation by the associated graph less transparent.
These extra vertices correspond to elements that are no longer linearly independent, which introduces difficulties in interpreting the graph and building intuition.
Furthermore, the rules for verifying the validity of a redundant graph, as discussed in Section III, are also more involved than those for minimal graphs, especially those induced by the Jacobi identity. This added complexity makes redundant graphs less practical for quick structural analysis compared to minimal graphs.




The current framework is restricted to finite-dimensional Lie algebras, leaving infinite-dimensional cases outside its scope. This limitation excludes several important classes of Lie algebras that arise in mathematical physics and functional analysis, such as the Weyl algebra [72], universal enveloping algebras [94], or the Lie algebra of compact operators on an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space [97].




In practice, this approach may, in certain cases, be difficult to implement, as it relies on finding a specific basis that satisfies the structural requirements given by equations (6) or, alternatively, constructing an overcomplete basis that satisfies equations (7).
Such bases are not always easy to obtain, and not every basis is suitable for this method.
Another significant weakness is the non-uniqueness of the associated graphs: a single Lie algebra can, in principle, admit multiple bases that correspond to distinct graphs.
The complex Lorentz algebra \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}} , as demonstrated in Section V.2, clearly illustrates this phenomenon.
Conversely, the same graph may be associated with several distinct Lie algebras, as shown in Example 28.
While this non-uniqueness complicates the classification of Lie algebras, it can also be viewed as a strength, as it reveals structural similarities between different Lie algebras.
Moreover, this property can also be exploited to differentiate simple from non-simple but semisimple Lie algebras in the complex setting. Specifically, Proposition 30 guarantees that a semisimple but non-simple Lie algebra can be associated with a labeled directed graph G(V,E) that decomposes into multiple (at least two) mutually unconnected subgraphs G(V_{j},E_{j}) , where each subgraph corresponds to a simple ideal. In contrast, Proposition 79 shows that a minimal graph associated with a simple Lie algebra can never be decomposed in such a way.




This approach is effective in highlighting certain structural properties of the Lie algebra, such as solvability or nilpotency, but it does not capture all aspects of the algebraic structure.
In particular, the criteria derived from the graph representation are not sufficient to reliably distinguish between simple and semisimple Lie algebras when only a single graph is considered. Furthermore, we could not provide any criterion that aids in determining whether a Lie algebra corresponds to a compact or non-compact Lie group using solely the graph structure.
For instance, the two real Lie algebras \mathfrak{su}(2) and \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) can both be associated with the same graph (cf. Example 28), despite the fact that their corresponding Lie groups differ fundamentally: the special unitary group \operatorname{SU}(2) is compact, whereas the special linear group \operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}) is not [187]. One possible refinement to address this limitation might be obtained by introducing an edge-coloring scheme to encode additional information, such as the sign or magnitude of the structure constants. For example, the Lie algebras \mathfrak{su}(2) and \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) differ, with respect to the bases used here, only by a sign factor in their defining brackets (cf. equations (9) and (11)), which could be indicated by distinct edge colors.





VI.3 Potential extensions




The observations presented above naturally lead to the discussion of possible extensions of the proposed framework.
One promising direction is to generalize labeled directed graphs to a labeled version of directed hypergraphs [39], as this allows a single edge to point towards multiple vertices.
Such a generalization is necessary whenever one works with a basis where the adjoint action of one basis element on another yields a linear combination of several basis elements, a situation that occurs, for example, for all finite-dimensional non-graph-admissible Lie algebras and can also arise for arbitrary basis choices.
In this setting, the corresponding edge would indicate that the Lie bracket of the element labeling the vertex from which the edge originates and the element labeling the edge itself produces a linear combination of the elements labeling all vertices to which the edge points. However, this approach has certain drawbacks.
For instance, the weights of the individual basis elements in a linear combination are not clearly represented in the hypergraph, even though they carry more structural significance than the scalar coefficient of a single resulting element. In the current approach, where the basis is chosen so that the Lie bracket of two basis elements produces at most one basis element, the exact scalar factor is irrelevant for the construction of the associated graph. This omission is justified by the bilinearity of the Lie bracket, as any non-zero scalar multiple can be absorbed without changing the underlying structure of the graph. In contrast, the relative weights in a linear combination determine which elements appear in the bracket and how strongly they contribute, making them essential for accurately capturing the algebra’s structure. Additionally, hypergraphs are generally less visually intuitive, making it harder to develop a clear understanding of the structure of the associated Lie algebra.
Despite these challenges, this generalization is worth investigating as it could significantly broaden the scope of applicability of the framework.




Another immediate generalization would be achieved by relaxing the requirement that the (possibly overcomplete) basis satisfying relations (6) (or (7) respectively) is finite and allowing, instead, also suitable countably infinite bases. This extension would enable the construction of graphs with countably infinite vertices and edges, thereby covering also certain finite but non-graph-admissible Lie algebras as well as other countably infinite-dimensional Lie algebras.
Such generalizations would not only alleviate the difficulty of finding a suitable basis but also allow the visualization of additional structural features, such as chains of Lie brackets, which are particularly relevant in the study of derived and lower central series or the classification of finite-dimensional subalgebras of an infinite-dimensional Lie algebra [42, 99]. For a more detailed explanation, see Example 84.




Example: Visualizing Commutator Chains


Example 84.

The seminal work [42] investigates whether a specific set of elements in the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{n} generates an infinite-dimensional subalgebra. The key tool in this analysis is provided by Commutator Chains, which were later generalized in [99]. The idea behind these objects is to start with two seed elements and iteratively calculate nested Lie brackets. If the resulting elements are of strictly increasing degree, one can conclude that the two initial elements generate an infinite-dimensional Lie algebra, since non-zero terms of differing degree are linearly independent. Here, in Figure 20, we illustrate a Commutator Chain of Type II by representing only edges relevant to the construction of the chain. All elements are linearly independent since they have strictly monotonically increasing degree, and therefore the chain never terminates. Thus, the corresponding graph consists of countably infinitely many vertices and edges. For details, see [42].


Figure 20: Commutator Chain of Type II generated by the seed elements y_{+}^{(0)}:=g_{+}^{\gamma} and y_{-}^{(0)}:=g_{-}^{\gamma} .






These extensions generally preserve the strengths of the presented approach and could provide a richer and more flexible framework for representing Lie algebras using labeled directed graphs. Nevertheless, in the next Subsection VI.4 we want to focus on the most promising generalization, which suggests a direct connection to the concept of Lie algebra gradations.





VI.4 Generalization via magma-gradings




Another idea that naturally arises when attempting to generalize the graph-theoretic approach developed in this work is inspired by the concept of group gradings, which play a central role in various areas of mathematics and theoretical physics, such as representation theory [117], supersymmetry [166], and the theory of algebraic groups [23, 106]. In particular, gradations provide a powerful tool for organizing the internal structure of Lie algebras [79] and aid in classification efforts [161, 22]. We begin by recalling the standard definition:



Definition 85 (graded Lie algebra).



Let \mathfrak{g} be a Lie algebra over the field \mathbb{F} and let (G,\circ) be an abelian group. A G -gradation of \mathfrak{g} is a direct vector-space decomposition




 \displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j\in G}\mathfrak{g}_{j},\qquad\text{such that}\qquad[\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]\subseteq\mathfrak{g}_{j\circ k}\qquad\text{for all }j,k\in G. 



A Lie algebra is called a G -graded Lie algebra if such a decomposition exists for a given abelian group G [103].





It is common practice to refer to a G -graded Lie algebra simply as a graded Lie algebra when either the specific grading group G is clear from the context or deemed unimportant. In particular, any Lie algebra can be trivially regarded as a graded Lie algebra by considering the trivial group G=\{0\} , where the entire algebra is concentrated in a single component. It is important to note that in the literature, the term graded Lie algebra is sometimes also used to refer to the concept of a graded Lie superalgebra [116, 51, 166, 148, 149]. While closely related, the latter is equipped with different constraints on the Lie bracket of individual spaces. One notable example is the modification of the Jacobi identity to the graded Jacobi identity [149].




The concept of graded Lie algebras bears a structural resemblance to that of minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras, as these admit a finite basis \mathcal{B}=\{e_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}} , with an index set \mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} , such that the basis elements e_{j}\in\mathcal{B} satisfy the relations (6). Accordingly, one can decompose a minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} as \mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathfrak{g}_{j} , where each vector-space component is defined by \mathfrak{g}_{j}:=\spn\{e_{j}\} . These spaces satisfy either the bracket relation [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)} or [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=\{0\} for a symmetric function \delta:\mathcal{J}\times\mathcal{J}\to\mathcal{J} . For pairs (j,k) satisfying [\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=\{0\} , the value of \delta(j,k) may be chosen arbitrarily, provided that it preserves the symmetry of \delta .
Thus, a minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra satisfies a generalized notion of a graded Lie algebra, where the abelian group (G,\circ) is replaced by the abelian magma (\mathcal{J},\delta) [170]. Such a generalization of a Lie-algebra grading is, in some contexts, also referred to as a graded Lie algebra [57]. Nevertheless, since group-gradings remain the most widely used and standard formulation, we adopt the more descriptive term “magma-graded” to emphasize the generalization and to avoid ambiguity. Abelian magmas are also referred to as abelian binars [29] or abelian groupoids [38]. The use of an abelian magma rather than an abelian group allows \delta to be non-associative and not to possess an identity element or inverses. This motivates the following definition:



Definition 86 (magma-graded-graph-admissible Lie algebra).



Let \mathfrak{g} be a Lie algebra over the field \mathbb{F} . Then \mathfrak{g} is called an \mathcal{M} -magma-graded-graph-admissible ( \mathcal{M} -mgga) Lie algebra if there exists an abelian magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) such that \mathfrak{g} admits a direct vector-space decomposition




 \displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathcal{M}}\mathfrak{g}_{j},\qquad\text{such that either}\qquad[\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=\mathfrak{g}_{\delta(j,k)}\qquad\text{or}\qquad[\mathfrak{g}_{j},\mathfrak{g}_{k}]=\{0\},\qquad\text{for all }j,k\in\mathcal{M}, 



where \mathfrak{g}_{j}\neq\{0\} for all j\in\mathcal{M} .
Furthermore, if there exists a magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) such that |\mathcal{M}|=\dim(\mathfrak{g}) and \mathfrak{g} is an \mathcal{M} -mgga Lie algebra, then \mathfrak{g} is called a minimal-magma-graded-graph-admissible (mmgga) Lie algebra.





From the definition above, it follows that a finite-dimensional Lie algebra is minimal-graph-admissible if and only if it is an mmgga Lie algebra. Indeed, every minimal-graph-admissible basis \{e_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{N}} induces such a decomposition by setting \mathfrak{g}_{j}:=\spn\{e_{j}\} . Conversely, if |\mathcal{M}|=\dim(\mathfrak{g}) , then every non-zero component \mathfrak{g}_{j} of the direct decomposition must be one-dimensional, and choosing one non-zero element from each component produces a minimal-graph-admissible basis. Thus, we can employ the same methods to associate a labeled directed graph with an mmgga Lie algebra.




Note also that Definition 86 also encompasses Lie algebras that are non-graph-admissible in the sense of the original Definition 15, as illustrated in Example 87 below. In this example, formally applying suitably modified versions of the methods developed above reproduces several structural properties of the Lie algebra. At present, however, this remains a heuristic observation and requires further investigation. In particular, the example motivates the following questions: Is every redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebra an \mathcal{M} -mgga Lie algebra for some finite abelian magma (\mathcal{M},\delta) ? Does every \mathcal{M} -mgga Lie algebra admit a natural finest magma-gradation? The latter question is nontrivial because every perfect Lie algebra admits the coarse one-component magma-gradation \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{1} with [\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}]=\mathfrak{g}_{1} . The corresponding modified graph consists of a single vertex with a self-loop labeled by that vertex, but it does not retain the internal bracket structure of \mathfrak{g} .




Example: \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation for non-graph-admissible Lie algebras


Example 87.

To establish the existence of non-graph-admissible Lie algebras as formulated by Theorem 23, in the corresponding proof we consider the family L_{\alpha}^{3} of solvable real three-dimensional Lie algebras [63] for specific non-zero choices of the real parameter \alpha . The Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} is spanned by the three elements \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\} that satisfy the Lie brackets:




 \displaystyle[e_{1},e_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[e_{1},e_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=-e_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[e_{2},e_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=-\alpha e_{1}-e_{2}. 


For \alpha\neq 0 , we can now choose the subspaces \mathfrak{g}_{1}:=\spn\{e_{1},e_{2}\} and \mathfrak{g}_{2}:=\spn\{e_{3}\} . It is straightforward to verify that




 \displaystyle[\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1}] 
 \displaystyle=\{0\},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{1},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{1}] 
 \displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{1},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{g}_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=\{0\}. 


Thus, L_{\alpha}^{3} admits an \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation, and is in particular an \mathcal{M} -mgga Lie algebra, where \mathcal{M}=\{1,2\} and the binary operation \delta:\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M} must satisfy \delta(1,2)=1=\delta(2,1) , while the values of \delta(1,1) and \delta(2,2) may be chosen arbitrarily. The resulting labeled directed graph G(V,E) , obtained by formally applying the construction rules of Algorithm 1 with each homogeneous subspace \mathfrak{g}_{j} treated as a single vertex, is depicted in Figure 21.


Figure 21: Modified labeled directed graph corresponding to the \mathcal{M} -magma-gradation of L_{\alpha}^{3} , where each blue vertex represents an entire homogeneous subspace.


Note that we color the vertices blue to highlight that they represent entire homogeneous subspaces rather than individual basis elements.


We can now analyze this graph using suitably modified versions of the central results of this work, as if it were obtained from Algorithm 1 for a valid basis of a Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . We find that the analogue of Theorem 58 would correctly identify L_{\alpha}^{3} as solvable, since the graph G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraph that is induced by a closed directed walk. Similarly, an appropriately modified analogue of Theorem 66 would correctly imply that L_{\alpha}^{3} is not nilpotent because the graph G(V,E) contains a closed directed walk. Moreover, an appropriately modified version of Lemma 52, together with Algorithm 2, would imply that the derived algebras of L_{\alpha}^{3} are \mathcal{D}^{0}L_{\alpha}^{3}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}=L_{\alpha}^{3} , \mathcal{D}^{1}L_{\alpha}^{3}=\mathfrak{g}_{1} , and \mathcal{D}^{\ell}L_{\alpha}^{3}=\{0\} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2} , which is correct. Similarly, an appropriately modified version of Lemma 62, together with Algorithm 3, correctly constructs the lower central series of L_{\alpha}^{3} , given by \mathcal{C}^{0}L_{\alpha}^{3}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{2}=L_{\alpha}^{3} and \mathcal{C}^{\ell}L_{\alpha}^{3}=\mathfrak{g}_{1} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} . Since this series does not terminate, L_{\alpha}^{3} is correctly identified as non-nilpotent. Furthermore, an appropriately modified version of Lemma 70 would correctly imply that \mathfrak{g}_{1} is a nonzero proper ideal of L_{\alpha}^{3} . Finally, the analogue of Proposition 79 would imply that L_{\alpha}^{3} is not simple, as G(V,E) contains no closed directed walk that induces a subgraph G(\tilde{V},\tilde{E}) with V=\tilde{V} , which is also correct.


Note that we do not claim that these results hold in general for coarse \mathcal{M} -magma-gradings. Nonetheless, the example suggests that suitably modified analogues of the results considered above may remain valid in this generalized setting. Establishing their precise formulations and validity requires further investigation.





Remark.



We emphasize that this \mathcal{M} -magma-graded construction is not used in the proofs of the main results of this work. Rather, it should be understood as a possible extension of the present framework beyond graph-admissible Lie algebras. The precise formulation, the modified graph-construction algorithms, and graph-theoretic classification results are left for future investigation.






VI.5 Potential applications to quantum physics




Finally, we aim to highlight potential applications of the present framework in analyzing the dynamics of a quantum mechanical system and fostering a deeper intuition for the underlying calculations. The following approach can be especially beneficial as a first approach to better understand the subject, because associated graphs can provide an initial tool for visualizing dynamical Lie algebras [9, 177, 6, 77], analyzing their solvability, and organizing the similarity transformations that arise in Lie-algebraic factorization methods [179, 180, 53].




To proceed, we consider a complex Hilbert space \mathcal{H} that contains all physical states of the system. Let the initial state at time t_{0}=0 be denoted by |\psi_{0}\rangle{}\equiv|\psi(0)\rangle{}\in\mathcal{H} . The central objective in quantum dynamics is generally to determine the state |\psi(t)\rangle{} that describes the system at a later time t\in\mathbb{R} . This is achieved by solving the Schrödinger equation




 \displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}|\psi(t)\rangle{}=-i\hat{H}(t)|\psi(t)\rangle{},\quad\text{with}\quad|\psi(0)\rangle{}=|\psi_{0}\rangle{}, 



where \hat{H}(t) denotes the Hamiltonian governing the system’s evolution [56].
This equation can be reformulated by introducing the unitary time-evolution operator \hat{U}(t) , with the initial time fixed at t_{0}=0 , defined by \hat{U}(t)|\psi_{0}\rangle{}=|\psi(t)\rangle{} . The operator \hat{U}(t) is a unitary propagator satisfying




 \displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}\hat{U}(t)=-i\hat{H}(t)\hat{U}(t)\quad\text{with}\quad\hat{U}(0)=\hat{\mathds{1}}, 

(20)


where \hat{\mathds{1}} denotes the identity operator.
In most settings, the Hamiltonian \hat{H}(t) admits a decomposition of the form




 \displaystyle\hat{H}(t)=-i\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}u_{j}(t)\hat{g}_{j}, 



where u_{j}(t) are real-valued (potentially) time-dependent functions and \hat{g}_{j} are time-independent skew-hermitian generators belonging to a particular Lie algebra. This representation therefore emphasizes the deep connection between quantum dynamics and Lie-algebraic structures.
The differential equation (20) can, in principle, be solved using formal expansions such as the Magnus series [32] or the time-ordered Dyson series [76]. However, these solutions are often not conducive to obtaining an exact closed-form expression for |\psi(t)\rangle{}=\hat{U}(t)|\psi_{0}\rangle{} , due to the lack of analytical control. Consequently, one often resorts to truncating these expansions to obtain approximate solutions [32, 123]. Depending on the Hamiltonian and the relevant operator assumptions, the convergence and practical error control of such expansions may be restricted to finite time intervals [142, 181].
An alternative approach, pioneered by Wei and Norman [179, 180], offers a constructive method for solving the time-dependent Schrödinger equation by means of factorizing the time-evolution operator into exponentials of elements of the dynamical Lie algebra. This technique provides a local Wei-Norman factorization whenever the Hamiltonian Lie algebra \mathfrak{g}:=\langle\{\hat{g}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is finite dimensional, and, importantly, yields, after choosing a suitable basis and under an appropriate ordering of the corresponding basis elements, a global solution when the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is solvable [180]. The core idea is to select a basis \mathcal{B}=\{\hat{g}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathcal{K}} of \mathfrak{g} and seek a factorization of the form




 \displaystyle\hat{U}(t)=\prod_{k\in\mathcal{K}}\hat{U}_{k}(t)\qquad\text{with}\qquad\hat{U}_{k}(t)=\exp(-f_{k}(t)\hat{g}_{k}), 



where the real-valued time-dependent functions f_{k}(t) encode the dynamics. To determine these functions, one differentiates the factorized form of \hat{U}(t) , uses the unitarity of the factors, and compares it with the differential equation (20), obtaining:




 \displaystyle\left(-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\hat{U}(t)\right)\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t) 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}f_{k}(t)\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\hat{U}_{j}(t)\right)\hat{g}_{k}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\hat{U}_{k-j}^{\dagger}(t)\right)=i\hat{H}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}u_{j}(t)\hat{g}_{j}, 



where, without loss of generality, we have set
 \mathcal{K}=\{1,\ldots,m\} and
 \mathcal{J}=\{1,\ldots,n\} , and have removed any linear redundancies
among the generators \{\hat{g}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}} , so that
 m=\dim(\mathfrak{g})\geq n . Thus, the functions f_{k}(t) are determined by a system of coupled differential equations, whose structure depends on the similarity transformations \hat{U}_{j}(t)\hat{g}_{k}\hat{U}_{j}^{\dagger}(t) .




To illustrate this approach, consider the bosonic Hamiltonian of the displaced harmonic oscillator




 \displaystyle\hat{H}(t)=\omega\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\alpha(t)\hat{a}+\alpha^{*}(t)\hat{a}^{\dagger}=\omega\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\mathrm{Re}(\{\alpha(t)\})(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger})+i\mathrm{Im}(\{\alpha(t)\})(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}), 



obtained via the displacement operation introduced in [45]. Here, \hat{a}^{\dagger} and \hat{a} denote the bosonic creation and annihilation operators respectively, which satisfy the canonical commutation relation [\hat{a},\hat{a}^{\dagger}]=1 . For this system, we can choose the generating set of operators as \mathcal{G}:=\{i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a},i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}\} and a basis of \mathfrak{g}:=\langle\mathcal{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is clearly given by \mathcal{B}=\{i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a},i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger},i\} . This Lie algebra is known as the Wigner-Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} [99]. Moreover, the basis \mathcal{B} satisfies the structural relations (6), confirming that the Lie algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} is minimal-graph-admissible.




The framework developed in this work offers three direct applications in this setting.

1. 



Visualization of the Lie algebra. Constructing the associated graph provides immediate insights into the structure of the Lie algebra and offers an intuitive starting point for understanding its properties. For example, for the Wigner-Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} with the basis \mathcal{B} , the corresponding graph clearly encodes the commutation relations among these generators. Such a visualization is particularly useful for identifying subalgebras, connectivity patterns, and structural features. The corresponding graph is illustrated in Figure 22.


Figure 22: Depiction of the graph associated with the Wigner-Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} , constructed using the basis \mathcal{B}=\{i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a},i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger},i\} and Algorithm 1. Here, we used, for visual simplicity, the convention to depict vertices by black circles instead of writing the name of the vertex inside a circle that represents it, as would be the correct convention for this graph.

2. 



Solvability analysis. If the dynamical Lie algebra \langle\{\hat{g}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is graph-admissible, one can construct an associated graph and employ Theorem 58 to decide whether the algebra is solvable or not. This, in turn, allows us to infer whether a global Wei-Norman factorization of the time-evolution operator can be obtained after a suitable choice and ordering of basis elements. Returning to the example of the displaced harmonic oscillator and the Lie algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} , we observe that the graph depicted in Figure 22 contains no closed directed walks that induce self-contained subgraphs. Thus, the Lie algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} is solvable and a global Wei-Norman factorization of the time evolution can be obtained after a suitable choice and ordering of the basis elements.

3. 



Similarity transformations. When calculating the time-evolution operator \hat{U}(t) using the Wei-Norman method, one needs to determine the real time-dependent function f_{k}(t) , whose determination requires computing multiple similarity relations. As explained in the literature [2], the following identity holds:




 \displaystyle\exp(-s_{j}\hat{g}_{j})\hat{g}_{k}\exp(s_{j}\hat{g}_{j}) 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-s_{j})^{n}}{n!}\operatorname{ad}_{\hat{g}_{j}}^{n}(\hat{g}_{k})=\exp(-s_{j}\,\operatorname{ad}_{\hat{g}_{j}})(\hat{g}_{k}), 



where \operatorname{ad}_{x}^{n+1}(y)=[x,\operatorname{ad}_{x}^{n}(y)] , and one adopts the convention \operatorname{ad}_{\hat{g}_{j}}^{0}(\hat{g}_{k})\equiv\hat{g}_{k} . Note that, for the identity above, we implicitly assume that the dynamical Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is realized in an associative operator algebra or a suitable representation in which the relevant products and exponentials are well defined. For Weyl-algebra realizations (cf. [72]) and other unbounded operator realizations, this identity may instead be understood in a suitable completion, on a common invariant domain, or formally when only the algebraic commutation relations are used.




The associated graph provides a direct intuitive interpretation of this relation: the similarity transformation \exp(-\hat{g}_{j})\hat{g}_{k}\exp(\hat{g}_{j}) can be visualized as a possibly infinite walk through the graph. Starting at the vertex corresponding to \hat{g}_{k} , one iteratively traverses edges labeled by \hat{g}_{j} , reaching each time the resulting vertex corresponding to the next nested commutator. The contribution of each step in the walk is weighted by the constants \alpha_{jk} . To retain this information, we equip the associated graph G(V,E) with the weight function \omega:E\to\mathbb{F} with \mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\} defined by the concrete action




 \displaystyle\omega:e\mapsto\omega(e):=\alpha_{jk},\qquad\text{where}\qquad[\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)]=\alpha_{jk}\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e). 



The resulting weighted walk encodes both the successive output directions and the coefficients of the nested commutators and therefore allows one to compute the similarity transformation \exp(-sv_{j})v_{k}\exp(sv_{j}) . Let us formalize this with the following lemma.



Lemma 88.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra over \mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\} , let \mathcal{B}=\{v_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{J}} be an admissible, possibly overcomplete, spanning set, and let G(V,E) be the corresponding associated labeled directed graph equipped with the weight function defined above. Fix two vertices v_{j},v_{k}\in V , and set w_{0}:=v_{k} . Starting from w_{0} , recursively proceed as follows. If, for some p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , there exists an edge e\in E satisfying both \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=w_{p} and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)=v_{j} , let e_{p}\equiv e denote this unique edge and define w_{p+1}:=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{p}) . If, at some first index L\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , no such edge exists, the recursion terminates at w_{L} , and we set \mathcal{L}:=\{0,1,\ldots,L\} . If such an edge exists at every step, we set \mathcal{L}:=\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . For every \ell\in\mathcal{L} , define the corresponding directed walk W_{\ell}^{(j,k)}:=(w_{0},e_{0},w_{1},e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell-1},w_{\ell}) , where W_{0}^{(j,k)}:=(w_{0}) .




Then, for every \ell\in\mathcal{L} , one has




 \displaystyle\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}^{\ell}(v_{k})=(-1)^{\ell}\left(\prod_{q=0}^{\ell-1}\omega(e_{q})\right)w_{\ell}, 

(21)


where the empty product is understood as one. Consequently, for every
 s\in\mathbb{F} , one has




 \displaystyle\exp\left(-s\,\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}\right)(v_{k})=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}}\frac{s^{\ell}}{\ell!}\left(\prod_{q=0}^{\ell-1}\omega(e_{q})\right)w_{\ell}. 






Proof.



First observe that by Proposition 32, the walks W_{\ell}^{(j,k)} are uniquely defined because the construction above allows only for unique edges e_{p} . Now, let us start to prove the formula for the iterated adjoint action (21) by induction over \ell . The base case \ell=0 follows from definition because \operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}^{0}(v_{k})=v_{k}=w_{0}=(-1)^{0}w_{0} . Thus, we proceed with the induction step and assume that the formula (21) holds for some \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} and that the edge e_{\ell} exists. By definition of the weight function, one has [w_{\ell},v_{j}]=\omega(e_{\ell})w_{\ell+1} . The antisymmetry of the Lie bracket therefore implies that [v_{j},w_{\ell}]=-\omega(e_{\ell})w_{\ell+1} . Hence,




 \displaystyle\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}^{\ell+1}(v_{k}) 
 \displaystyle=\left[v_{j},\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}^{\ell}(v_{k})\right]=(-1)^{\ell}\left(\prod_{q=0}^{\ell-1}\omega(e_{q})\right)[v_{j},w_{\ell}]=(-1)^{\ell+1}\left(\prod_{q=0}^{\ell}\omega(e_{q})\right)w_{\ell+1}, 



which completes the induction step and therefore proves the validity of the induction hypothesis for all applicable \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} .




Since \mathfrak{g} is assumed to be finite-dimensional, \operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}} is a finite-dimensional endomorphism of \mathfrak{g}  [94]. Its exponential is therefore defined for every s\in\mathbb{F} by the convergent series




 \displaystyle\exp\left(-s\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}\right)(v_{k})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-s)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}^{\ell}(v_{k}). 



Substituting the formula (21) for non-terminating sequences of non-terminating directed walks W_{\ell}^{(j,k)} obtained above yields




 \displaystyle\exp\left(-s\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}\right)(v_{k})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{s^{\ell}}{\ell!}\left(\prod_{q=0}^{\ell-1}\omega(e_{q})\right)w_{\ell}. 



However, if a sequence of walks \{W_{\ell}^{(j,k)}\}_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}} terminates with a walk that ends at vertex w_{L} , this implies by the definition of the associated graph that [v_{j},w_{L}]=0 . Consequently \operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}^{\ell}(v_{k})=0 for all \ell>L . Hence,




 \displaystyle\exp\left(-s\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}\right)(v_{k})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\frac{(-s)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}^{\ell}(v_{k})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}\frac{s^{\ell}}{\ell!}\left(\prod_{q=0}^{\ell-1}\omega(e_{q})\right)w_{\ell}, 



as claimed.
∎




Corollary 89.



Under the assumptions of Lemma 88, fix
 v_{j},v_{k}\in V and consider the recursively defined walk W^{(j,k)}:=(w_{0},e_{0},w_{1},e_{1},\ldots) defined there. Then exactly one of the following alternatives occurs:

(i) 



There exists some L\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} such that no edge starting at w_{L} 
and labeled by v_{j} exists. In this case, the recursion terminates
at w_{L} .

(ii) 



The recursion does not terminate and defines an infinite
directed walk W_{\infty}^{(j,k)}:=(w_{0},e_{0},w_{1},e_{1},\ldots) that is eventually periodic.



If \mathfrak{g} is nilpotent, then only the first alternative can occur.




Proof.



For a fixed starting vertex w_{\ell} and a fixed labeling vertex v_{j} , the corresponding end vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{\ell}) is, by construction, uniquely determined whenever the bracket [v_{j},w_{\ell}] does not vanish. Thus, the continuation of the sequence of walks W_{\ell}^{(j,k)} is uniquely determined by the last vertex in the respective walk W_{\ell}^{(j,k)} and the labeling vertex v_{j} . Since V is finite, every sufficiently long directed walk W_{\ell}^{(j,k)} must visit at least one vertex more than once. Hence, there must exist indices p,q with p<q such that w_{p}=w_{q} . Since the continuation from a fixed vertex under the labeling vertex v_{j} is unique, one has e_{p}=e_{q} , w_{p+1}=w_{q+1} , and the same argument applies recursively to every subsequent step. The walk is therefore periodic from w_{p} onward.




Suppose now that \mathfrak{g} is nilpotent. By Theorem 66, the associated graph G(V,E) contains no closed directed walk. If the recursion did not terminate, the preceding argument would yield indices p<q with w_{p}=w_{q} . The finite segment




 \displaystyle(w_{p},e_{p},w_{p+1},e_{p+1},\ldots,e_{q-1},w_{q}=w_{p}) 



would then be a closed directed walk in G(V,E) , contradicting Theorem 66. Therefore, the recursion terminates after finitely many steps.
∎





The preceding lemma and corollary are exact finite-dimensional statements, which show that the graphical description of dynamical Lie algebras does not merely provide a qualitative analogy for understanding similarity transformations: the weighted walk constructed above reproduces the relevant nested commutators and their coefficients exactly. Moreover, the graphical interpretation can also aid in bounding the error of a truncated adjoint expansion. Let




 \displaystyle S_{T}(s):=\sum_{\ell=0}^{T}\frac{s^{\ell}}{\ell!}\left(\prod_{q=0}^{\ell-1}\omega(e_{q})\right)w_{\ell}, 



for any truncation order T\in\mathcal{L} for which the vertices w_{\ell} and edges e_{q} are defined, and let \|\cdot\|:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0} be an arbitrary norm on \mathfrak{g} with induced operator norm \|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}} on \operatorname{End}(\mathfrak{g}) . Then




 \displaystyle\left\|\exp\left(-s\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}\right)(v_{k})-S_{T}(s)\right\| 
 \displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=T+1}^{\infty}\frac{|s|^{\ell}}{\ell!}\left\|\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}^{\ell}(v_{k})\right\|\leq\|v_{k}\|\sum_{\ell=T+1}^{\infty}\frac{\left(|s|\left\|\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\right)^{\ell}}{\ell!} 




 \displaystyle\leq\|v_{k}\|e^{|s|\left\|\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}}\frac{\left(|s|\left\|\operatorname{ad}_{v_{j}}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\right)^{T+1}}{(T+1)!}, 



by the definition of the operator norm [184] and Suzuki’s theorem [105]. Thus, in the finite-dimensional setting, the weighted-walk expansion may be truncated with a controlled remainder. Therefore, this graphical interpretation goes beyond the development of an intuition for such calculations by providing an intuitive framework for understanding the structure of these approximations.




By contrast, the extension to infinite-dimensional Lie algebras and infinite associated graphs should be understood as an interpretative observation rather than as a consequence of the results above. In an infinite-dimensional operator setting, additional assumptions concerning, for instance, the existence of the relevant operator exponentials and the convergence of the adjoint series are required. Nevertheless, the graphical picture already highlights these subtleties: in this setting, a non-repeating and non-terminating walk illustrates the challenges that may arise when analyzing the convergence behavior of the corresponding series expansion.






Example: Obtaining similarity relations using walks in the associated graphs


Example 90.

We now demonstrate the results above in the case of the Wigner-Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} . We use again the basis \mathcal{B}=\{i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a},i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger},i\} , and for completeness, display the corresponding edge weights as secondary labels. Thus, \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e) continues to denote the labeling vertex of an edge e , whereas \omega(e) denotes its weight. Accordingly, in the notation used below, an edge displayed as (x,(y,\lambda),z) represents the edge e=(x,y,z) with \omega(e)=\lambda . This results in the graph depicted in Figure 23.


Following the approach described above, we define the walk W=(v_{0},e_{0},v_{1},e_{1},v_{2},e_{2},\ldots) , defined by:




 \displaystyle v_{2\ell}:=\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger},\quad e_{2\ell}:=(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger},(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a},1),i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger})),\quad v_{2\ell+1}:=i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),\quad e_{2\ell+1}:=(i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}),(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a},-1),\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}), 


for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . This walk is associated with the similarity transformation \exp(-i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger})\exp(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}) . An illustration of the corresponding walk can be found in Figure 23. For the edge weights, we note that \omega(e_{2\ell})=1=-\omega(e_{2\ell+1}) for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , and consequently




 \displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\omega(e_{j})=(-1)^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}. 

(22)

This allows us to compute the following similarity transformation, where, for simplicity, we set s=1 .




 \displaystyle\exp(-i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger})\exp(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}) 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\omega(e_{j})\right)v_{n}. 


Splitting the series into terms of even and odd orders yields together with the application of (22):




 \displaystyle\exp(-i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger})\exp(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}) 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2n)!}(-1)^{\lfloor\frac{2n}{2}\rfloor}v_{2n}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(2n+1)!}(-1)^{\lfloor\frac{2n+1}{2}\rfloor}v_{2n+1} 




 \displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n)!}v_{0}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n+1)!}v_{1}. 


Substituting v_{0}=\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger} and v_{1}=i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}) , we obtain:




 \displaystyle\exp(-i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger})\exp(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}) 
 \displaystyle=\cos(1)\left(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}\right)+i\sin(1)\left(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}\right)=e^{i}\hat{a}-e^{-i}\hat{a}^{\dagger}. 


This matches the expected result, as it is in agreement with the literature [99]. This example illustrates a walk that loops the same sequence indefinitely, as predicted by Corollary 89, which can be treated by splitting the series into the terms that have a common vertex label.


To contrast the previous example, we consider the similarity transformation \exp(-i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}))(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})\exp(i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger})) . Here, the corresponding walk W^{\prime} starts at the vertex v_{0}=i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a} . Following the edge labeled with i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}) , one reaches the vertex labeled by \hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger} . Continuing along the next edge, also labeled by i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}) , one arrives at the vertex labeled with i , where the walk terminates because no further edge originates from this vertex, especially no edge labeled by i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}) . This yields by Lemma 88 for s=1 :




 \displaystyle\exp(-i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}))(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})\exp(i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger})) 
 \displaystyle=i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\omega(e_{0})\left(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\omega(e_{0})\omega(e_{1})i, 


where \omega(e_{0})=1 and \omega(e_{1})=2 . Substituting these values yields:




 \displaystyle\exp(-i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger}))(i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a})\exp(i(\hat{a}+\hat{a}^{\dagger})) 
 \displaystyle=i\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\left(\hat{a}-\hat{a}^{\dagger}\right)+i. 


The corresponding walk is also depicted in Figure 23.


Figure 23: Depiction of the graph associated with the Wigner-Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{wh}_{2} from Figure 22, now including the edge weights as secondary labels. The two walks W (shown in green) and W^{\prime} (shown in blue) from Example 90 are also illustrated.







VI.6 Further remarks




Finally, we want to devote this last section to a broader reflection and prospective developments stemming from our study. Beyond the specific results established in the preceding sections, the framework of associating labeled directed graphs with Lie algebras opens several avenues for further exploration. These include both theoretical questions and potential applications, particularly in the classification of Lie algebras and their structural invariants. We now present a selection of open problems and conjectures that naturally arise from our findings.




VI.6.1 Open questions and conjectures




We begin by briefly discussing several open questions and potential directions for future research. These are presented in a logical sequence that mirrors the progression of the main text.




We started by defining and investigating the notion of graph-admissibility with particular emphasis on the subclass of minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras. Among the general results established, which include the existence Theorems 16 and 23, we showed that every finite-dimensional nilpotent Lie algebra is graph-admissible. This naturally raises the following questions:


Q: Is every nilpotent Lie algebra minimal-graph-admissible? If not, what are the structural differences between those that are minimal-graph-admissible and those that are not?


To approach this question, let us consider the subclass of two-step nilpotent Lie algebras, i.e., Lie algebras \mathfrak{g} that satisfy [\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]\neq\{0\} and [\mathfrak{g},[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]]=\{0\} . Engel’s Theorem [109] implies that \operatorname{ad}_{x}^{2}=0 for all x\in\mathfrak{g} . Generally, one has even \operatorname{ad}_{x}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{y}=\operatorname{ad}_{y}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{x}=0 for all x,y\in\mathfrak{g} . This is because \operatorname{ad}_{x}(z)\in[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]\subseteq\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g}) for all x,z\in\mathfrak{g} , and consequently (\operatorname{ad}_{x}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{y})(z)=0 for all x,y,z\in\mathfrak{g} . Henceforth, the adjoint actions of any pair of basis elements commute.




Motivated by the ability to construct a similarity transformation that brings a set of commuting normal matrices into their diagonalized form, one can conclude the following:
if it were possible to construct a simultaneous similarity transformation that brings all adjoint operations into their respective Jordan normal forms, it would follow that every two-step nilpotent Lie algebra is minimal-graph-admissible. In fact, for certain pairs of commuting nilpotent endomorphisms, the existence of such a similarity transformation has already been established [107]. However, in the case of three-step nilpotent Lie algebras, it is generally no longer true that all adjoint actions commute. This observation motivates the following conjecture:



Conjecture 91.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional k -step nilpotent Lie algebra. Then, the following holds: (a) If k\leq 2 , then \mathfrak{g} is minimal-graph-admissible; (b) If k>2 , then \mathfrak{g} is not necessarily minimal-graph-admissible. In fact, there exist k -step nilpotent Lie algebras that fail to be minimal-graph-admissible.





The focus on nilpotent Lie algebras further connects this work to ongoing efforts aimed at investigating such algebras through alternative graph-based approaches, as mentioned in the introduction (see for example, [145, 62, 93, 52]). A key difference between our framework and that in [145] lies in the interpretation of the Lie bracket. In [145], the bracket [x_{1},x_{2}]=x_{3} is represented by an edge originating from x_{1} , pointing towards x_{2} and being labeled by x_{3} . This encoding captures the sign of the bracket through the edge’s direction, since by the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket [x_{2},x_{1}]=-x_{3} . However, this approach enforces an even more restrictive condition on the basis elements, compared to the relations (6) or (7). Specifically, the Lie bracket of two basis elements can only yield one of three outcomes: either a single basis element, its additive inverse, or zero. This limitation excludes certain Lie algebras, for instance real Lie algebras that inherently involve irrational coefficients. One notable example is the Lie algebra \mathfrak{N}(q) , first introduced in [165]. This real Lie algebra is spanned by the six basis elements \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},y_{1},y_{2}\} , which satisfy the following nontrivial Lie bracket relations:




 \displaystyle[x_{1},x_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=y_{1},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{1},x_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=y_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{2},x_{4}] 
 \displaystyle=y_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{3},x_{4}] 
 \displaystyle=qy_{1}, 



where q\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q} . All other brackets either vanish or follow from the antisymmetric property of the Lie bracket. Using the current framework, the associated graph for \mathfrak{N}(q) , according to our prescription, is given by:






These two approaches, the one presented in this work and the one discussed in [145], share a notable feature: a single Lie algebra can, in general, be represented by multiple distinct graphs, which is demonstrated by Example 2.3 in [145]. To further highlight the similarities and differences between the different graph-based approaches, we compare the present framework with the nilpotent constructions discussed in [145, 52] and the related solvable construction from [93] in Table 1 of Appendix A. In particular, the table compares the direction of construction, the classes of Lie algebras covered, the interpretation of the vertices and edges, the quantitative relations between the graph and the Lie algebra, and the corresponding uniqueness properties.




Another interesting research direction emerges when considering Theorem 58, which provides sufficient and necessary criteria for solvability by analyzing closed directed walks within the associated graph. The only additional requirement imposed is that the subgraph induced by such a walk has to be self-contained. It might therefore be worthwhile to improve the efficiency of this criterion, which is especially relevant for computer-based implementations, by further restricting the set of admissible walks. For instance, one could aim to establish conditions on the maximum length of such walks or determine how frequently a vertex must be revisited. These refinements would significantly reduce the number of walks that need to be considered, making the criterion computationally more tractable. Inspiration for such an investigation can certainly be drawn from the field of computer science, where graph-based methods are widely adopted [67]. One idea could be to incorporate Dijkstra’s algorithm to determine the shortest path between two vertices [71]. Such techniques might help identify minimal or optimal walks relevant to solvability.




Currently, the number of possible walks can be infinite, since a closed directed walk can be repeated arbitrarily many times, which poses practical challenges. Restricting this to a finite set would greatly simplify verification, as infinite loops do not contribute meaningfully to the analysis. This motivates the following question:


Q: Is Theorem 58 still valid if one replaces “closed directed walk” with “closed directed trail”? If not, what are the structural differences in the associated Lie algebra?





As emphasized earlier, the current framework does not guarantee uniqueness of the graphs associated with a given Lie algebra, and vice versa. This observation opens several intriguing questions. For instance, what is the relationship between Lie algebras that share an associated graph, particularly when considering minimal graphs? Conversely, what is the relationship between different graphs associated with the same Lie algebra? Furthermore, one could explore connections between Lie algebras of different dimensions that share the same graph, i.e., in cases where at least one graph is not minimal. Similarly, it would be interesting to investigate the interplay between minimal and redundant graphs associated with the same Lie algebra, and to examine how such relationships extend to other Lie algebras which admit these redundant graphs as minimal ones. These, as well as related questions, point toward a broader structural theory that classifies Lie algebras not only by their intrinsic properties but also by the equivalence classes of graphs they admit. Such an approach might offer valuable insight into the investigation of Lie algebras.




A related and equally promising direction is to ask whether this graph-based approach can be used to explore additional structural properties beyond those discussed in the preceding sections. In particular, one might consider the following question:


Q: Does a relation exist between the nullity of a Lie algebra and the associated graphs?


Recall that the nullity of a Lie algebra is defined as the minimal number of elements necessary to generate a given Lie algebra under the Lie closure [99]. Exploring this connection could uncover specific connectivity conditions for the associated graphs, among other results. Such findings might include existence theorems for particular graph configurations and upper bounds on the nullity, thereby linking this framework to other research on the nullity of Lie algebras [127, 128, 164, 7].




Another potential direction relates directly to the question of whether the proposed framework can be useful for classifying Lie algebras, a topic we address in the following part.





VI.6.2 Contributing to the Lie algebra classification effort




When working with algebraic structures, a natural question to raise is whether a classification of such structures can be achieved. A prominent example of such an endeavor is the classification of all finite simple groups [15]. For Lie algebras, however, a full classification is not yet known. Nevertheless, the classification of every simple and therefore every semisimple Lie algebra over an algebraically closed field is well established [50, 18, 19, 20, 21]. Building on these foundational results, significant progress has been made [30, 134, 140], and current approaches are informed by the Levi-Mal’tsev theorem, which states that any complex Lie algebra can be decomposed into the semidirect sum of a semisimple component and its radical [130]. Despite this, major challenges remain, particularly in classifying all solvable Lie algebras and understanding the structure of the semidirect sum [154]. Contemporary research primarily focuses on low-dimensional solvable Lie algebras [157, 66, 64, 10, 54].




The framework introduced in this work can be connected to these efforts by attempting to classify all graph-admissible Lie algebras together with the set of graphs that represent them. This is especially interesting for minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras and their associated minimal graphs, as these present a faithful representation and are, so far, better understood. Such a classification effort has the potential to complement and aid the broader goal of classifying all Lie algebras. Consequently, developing a systematic understanding of graph-admissible Lie algebras could yield valuable insights. One promising avenue would be to classify all labeled directed graphs that can serve as minimal graphs for minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras. This approach could reveal structural invariants and provide a combinatorial perspective on the classification problem.




In a first step, one could attempt to determine the number of inequivalent and valid graphs with n vertices that can be associated with a minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra of dimension n as a minimal graph. Let us denote this number by S(n) . Calculating S(n) can be refined by introducing the number S(n,m) , which counts such graphs when the number of edges is fixed to m . Clearly, S(n)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}S(n,m) . Moreover, by Propositions 32 and 34, one has:




 \displaystyle S(n)=\sum_{m=0}^{n(n-1)/2}S(n,2m), 



since the number of edges must be even, due to the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket, and is bounded from above by n(n-1) . Thus, S(n,2k+1)=0 for all k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Additionally, one has S(n,0)=1 for all n\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} , as there exists only one abelian Lie algebra of dimension n . It is straightforward to verify that S(1)=1=S(1,0) , while S(2)=2 with S(2,0)=1 , and S(2,2)=1 .
