Furthermore, as shown in Theorem 35 and Corollaries 38 and 40, one has: S(3,0)=1 , S(3,2)=2 , S(3,4)=4 , and S(3,6)=3 , while S(3,m)=0 for all other m\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}\setminus\{0,2,4,6\} . Consequently, the number of inequivalent graphs that can be associated with a minimal-graph-admissible three-dimensional Lie algebra as a minimal graph is: S(3)=10 .




Conversely, one can also ask:


Q: What is the number of graphs that can be associated with a particular Lie algebra, when restricted to minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras and minimal graphs?


For example, each abelian Lie algebra can only be associated with one minimal graph, reflecting its trivial bracket structure. In contrast, as we have seen in Example 28, the special linear Lie algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) can be associated with two distinct minimal graphs.





VI.6.3 Generating Lie algebras for a provided graph




Another potential research direction involves reversing the perspective adopted in this work. Instead of starting with a Lie algebra and associating a labeled directed graph to it, one could ask:


Q: Can we generate new and interesting Lie algebras by first constructing a valid graph and then associating a Lie algebra with it?


Pursuing this approach would require further development of tools to identify valid graphs, particularly valid redundant graphs, building on the initial effort in Section III. Such a methodology would not only relate the current framework to similar approaches in the literature, where Lie algebras are also constructed from graphs [62, 160, 65], but could also yield valuable insight into the role of graph symmetries. Recall that in Section V.3 we observed that a subgraph depicted in Figure 19 possesses a \mathbb{Z}_{3} -symmetry, yet we were unable to trace this symmetry to its origin in the Lie algebra. It is conceivable that such symmetries do not correspond to any meaningful invariant of the Lie algebra, resulting in what might be termed a apparent symmetry. However, if these symmetries do reflect structural properties, their study could be highly informative. In particular, redundant graphs may offer greater potential for visualizing such symmetries. This line of inquiry also relates to the following question:


Q: If \mathfrak{g} is a graph-admissible but not minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra, what is the smallest overcomplete basis that satisfies (7)?


Addressing this question would require investigating how and which elements can be added to an overcomplete basis to maintain validity. Furthermore, one could explore the connection between proper graphs and choice-dependent graphs, as well as the existence of proper but not proper-minimal graphs. For instance, in Theorem 35 each of the obtained proper graphs with three vertices can also be associated with a Lie algebra as a minimal graph, raising deeper questions about the interplay between minimality and redundancy.







VII Conclusion




The primary aspiration of this work was to develop a visualization framework that facilitates intuitive understanding of the structural properties of Lie algebras. It is in this spirit that we created a tool that allows us to immediately identify fundamental algebraic properties, such as solvability, nilpotency, and the presence of certain ideals, by providing a graphical representation of the algebras. A strong motivation for our work lies in the programme recently initiated to study Hamiltonian Lie algebras that are key to quantum dynamics [42], together with the ongoing efforts to determine finite-dimensional Lie subalgebras of the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{n} generated by elements of significance to the dynamics of physical processes [99]. In this context, a visualization tool is expected to be helpful for understanding key aspects of the Lie algebras of interest.




We started by restricting our attention to finite-dimensional Lie algebras that admit a suitable (possibly overcomplete) basis whose elements obey a specific sparsity constraint under the adjoint action. Concretely, the Lie bracket of any two basis elements must be proportional to another basis element, or vanish. Lie algebras fulfilling this requirement were termed “graph-admissible”, whereas those that did not were classified as “non-graph-admissible”. This condition is motivated by the idea of associating a labeled directed graph with a given Lie algebra and encoding the Lie bracket structure within the vertex-edge configuration. Graph-admissible Lie algebras admit a further subclassification: those Lie algebras that can be represented by a graph in which the number of nodes coincides with the dimension of the Lie algebra and those that cannot. In the former case, the graphs provide a “faithful” representation and the corresponding Lie algebras were called “minimal-graph-admissible”. Conversely, those that cannot are referred to as “redundant-graph-admissible but not minimal-graph-admissible”, since their associated graphs exhibit redundancies: in particular, the vertex set forms only a spanning set rather than a basis.
We demonstrated that the classification into non-graph-admissible, minimal-graph-admissible and redundant-graph-admissible (but not minimal-graph-admissible) is well founded by providing explicit examples of Lie algebras in each category. This was achieved by analyzing the family of solvable three-dimensional Lie algebras L_{\alpha}^{3} (cf. [63]). Furthermore, we established that a broad class of mathematically and physically significant Lie algebras, including all complex semisimple Lie algebras, all nilpotent Lie algebras, and all finite-dimensional Lie subalgebras of the complex single-mode Weyl algebra A_{1} , are graph-admissible.




The graphs that can be associated with graph-admissible Lie algebras must satisfy specific general structural properties reflecting the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket, as well as the constraints imposed by the Jacobi identity. In this work we provided relevant preliminary results that restrict the number of admissible graphs. However, we were unable to determine the exact number of graphs that, for a given number of n vertices, can be associated with an n -dimensional Lie algebra, except for the case n\leq 3 . The conditions of interest were mostly derived for minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras associated with minimal graphs only, but not redundant graphs associated with redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebras. Moreover, they only provide a weak criterion for proper graphs, i.e., graphs that can be associated with a Lie algebra, dictated by the Jacobi identity. Further work is required to refine and generalize these criteria.




We then provided a methodology to identify key structural properties of a given Lie algebra solely via its associated graph. Specifically, we formulated graph-theoretic criteria for identifying central elements, solvability, nilpotency, the existence of ideals, as well as simplicity, semisimplicity, and reductiveness. The criteria for solvability and nilpotency were inspired by classical algebraic constructions: we mirrored the formation of the derived series and lower central series with corresponding sequences of graphs, and provided accessible pruning algorithms to compute them. These constructions enabled us to establish strong necessary and sufficient conditions for solvability and nilpotency based exclusively on the initial graph, without requiring iterative graph reductions. These results present a significant step toward bridging algebraic and combinatorial perspectives as they allow structural classifications using purely graphical features. In order to illustrate the practical perspectives of these methods, we applied them to three important Lie algebras that are also key to physics: the Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} , the Lorentz algebra \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}} , and a finite-dimensional subalgebra of the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{n} . These examples illustrate the effectiveness of our framework in capturing deep structural properties through visualizations alone.




We concluded our analysis by examining the strengths of the proposed approach and outlining potential generalizations of the framework. One particularly promising direction lies in exploring the relationship between graph-admissibility and magma-gradings of Lie algebras, which could lead to deeper structural insights, new classification techniques, and a broadening of the applicability of the current framework. In addition, we discussed several avenues for future research, presenting open questions and conjectures and highlighting their connections to broader mathematical efforts. Finally, we emphasized the practical significance of these results by considering the role of solvability in determining the time evolution of certain physical systems. In this context, our approach can assist in better understanding concepts such as similarity transformations, since it allows one to map computations of sequences of nested commutators within algebras of interest to walks on the associated graphs. This perspective helps clarify some of the difficulties that can arise when extending such factorization methods to infinite-dimensional settings, offering a conceptual bridge between abstract algebraic theory and practical applications in physics.

Appendix A Table comparing different frameworks for associating graphs with Lie algebras




As discussed in the introduction and, in particular, in Section VI, several different frameworks have been developed for associating graphs with Lie algebras. For completeness, Table 1 compares the present framework with the nilpotent constructions from [52, 145] and the related solvable construction from [93]. In particular, we summarize the classes of Lie algebras and graphs considered, the encoding of the Lie bracket, the quantitative relations between the graph and the Lie algebra, the corresponding uniqueness properties, and the direction of each construction.


Table 1: Comparison of the present framework with closely related graph-based constructions of Lie algebras. The table contrasts different aspects such as the direction of construction, the encoded bracket data, the classes of Lie algebras covered, quantitative graph data, and uniqueness properties.



Comparison feature
Nilpotent construction [52, 145]
Solvable construction [93]
Present framework: minimal setting
Present framework: redundant setting

Class of Lie algebras
Specific two-step nilpotent Lie algebras \mathfrak{n} .
Specific three-step solvable Lie algebras \mathfrak{s} 
Finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras \mathfrak{g} , including nilpotent, solvable, and non-solvable examples
Finite-dimensional redundant-graph-admissible Lie algebras \mathfrak{h} , including minimal-graph-admissible, nilpotent, solvable, and non-solvable examples

Class of graphs
Finite labeled directed simple graphs
Finite simple undirected graphs G(V,E) 
Finite proper labeled directed graphs


Role of vertices
Subset of basis of \mathfrak{n}/\mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{n} 
Subset of basis of \mathcal{D}\mathfrak{s}/\mathcal{D}^{2}\mathfrak{s} 
Basis of \mathfrak{g} 
Overcomplete spanning set of \mathfrak{h} consisting of pairwise non-proportional elements

Encoding of Lie brackets
Starting vertex: first input of the Lie bracket





Edge label: Resulting central basis element
Edge data: Resulting basis element of \mathcal{D}^{2}\mathfrak{s} via the identification of the Lie bracket between two connected vertices [v_{j},v_{k}] with antisymmetric product v_{j}\wedge v_{k} 
Edge label: second input of the Lie bracket



Target vertex: second input of the Lie bracket

Target vertex: result of the Lie bracket up to a non-zero scalar coefficient



Edge direction: order of the inputs and hence the sign prefactor of the result
—
Edge direction: order of the inputs


Number of vertices
 |V|=\dim(\mathfrak{n})-\dim(\mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{n}) .
 |V|\leq\dim(\mathfrak{s})-\dim(\mathcal{D}^{2}\mathfrak{s}) 
 |V|=\dim(\mathfrak{g}) 
 |V|\geq\dim(\mathfrak{h}) with |V|<\infty 

Number of graph edges
 0\leq|E|\leq|V|(|V|-1)/2 

 0\leq|E|\leq|V|(|V|-1) 


Number of edge labels N_{\mathrm{L}} 
 N_{\mathrm{L}}=\dim(\mathcal{C}^{1}\mathfrak{n}) 
 0\leq N_{\mathrm{L}}\leq\dim(\mathcal{D}^{2}\mathfrak{s}) 
 0\leq N_{\mathrm{L}}\leq|V| 


Uniqueness
If the graph is connected and every edge has a distinct label, reversing any subset of edge directions produces an isomorphic Lie algebra; cf. Theorem 3.9 in [145]
Two Lie algebras constructed by this method are isomorphic if and only if their defining graphs are isomorphic; cf. Theorem 3.4 in [93]
A single Lie algebra may admit several non-isomorphic associated proper graphs, while the same unweighted proper graph may be associated with several non-isomorphic Lie algebras; cf. Example 28


Direction of construction
Starts from a labeled directed simple graph and constructs an associated two-step nilpotent Lie algebra
Starts from a finite simple undirected graph, uses its vertices and edges, supplemented by sets of three-cliques v_{j}\odot v_{k}\odot v_{\ell} , and constructs an associated solvable Lie algebra
Starts from a finite-dimensional Lie algebra together with a suitable basis and constructs an associated labeled directed graph
Starts from a finite-dimensional Lie algebra together with a suitable finite overcomplete spanning set and constructs an associated labeled directed graph







Appendix B Supplementary proofs




This appendix collects detailed proofs of several graph-admissibility results presented in the main text. Since these proofs are largely technical and provide little additional conceptual insight, their inclusion in the main text would distract from the central results. Throughout this appendix, we use the definitions and conventions introduced in the main text without restating them.



Theorem.



There exist finite-dimensional Lie algebras that are minimal-graph-admissible and ones that are not minimal-graph-admissible.




Proof.



The fact that there exist finite-dimensional Lie algebras that are not minimal-graph-admissible follows directly from the following Lemma 92. The existence of minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras can easily be shown by providing a simple example. The simplest possible such example is the one-dimensional abelian Lie algebra \mathbb{F} , which is admissible with any basis.
∎




Lemma 92.



The family of solvable real Lie algebras L_{\alpha}^{3} spanned by the three basis elements x_{1},x_{2},x_{3} and determined by the Lie bracket relations




 \displaystyle[x_{1},x_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{1},x_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=-x_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{2},x_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=-\alpha x_{1}-x_{2}, 

(23)


for \alpha\in\mathbb{R} is not minimal-graph-admissible if \alpha<-1/4 .




Proof.



Consider the family of solvable Lie algebras L_{\alpha}^{3}=\spn\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\} ,
where \alpha is a real parameter [63]. We choose now \alpha<-1/4 and suppose that L_{\alpha}^{3} is minimal-graph-admissible. In other words, L_{\alpha}^{3} admits a basis \{\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}\} that satisfies the relations (6). Each \tilde{x}_{j} can be expressed as a linear combination of the original basis:




 \displaystyle\tilde{x}_{j} 
 \displaystyle=c_{1}^{(j)}x_{1}+c_{2}^{(j)}x_{2}+c_{3}^{(j)}x_{3}\qquad\text{for}\qquad j\in\{1,2,3\}, 



where the c_{k}^{(j)} are appropriate real coefficients. Next, we compute the Lie bracket [\tilde{x}_{p},\tilde{x}_{q}] using the bilinearity of the bracket and the original structure constants:




 \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{p},\tilde{x}_{q}] 
 \displaystyle=\left(-c_{1}^{(p)}c_{3}^{(q)}+c_{3}^{(p)}c_{1}^{(q)}\right)x_{2}+\left(c_{2}^{(p)}c_{3}^{(q)}-c_{3}^{(p)}c_{2}^{(q)}\right)(-\alpha x_{1}-x_{2}) 




 \displaystyle=-\alpha\left(c_{2}^{(p)}c_{3}^{(q)}-c_{3}^{(p)}c_{2}^{(q)}\right)x_{1}-\left(c_{1}^{(p)}c_{3}^{(q)}-c_{3}^{(p)}c_{1}^{(q)}+c_{2}^{(p)}c_{3}^{(q)}-c_{3}^{(p)}c_{2}^{(q)}\right)x_{2}. 



Since L_{\alpha}^{3} is non-abelian and \{\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}\} is, by assumption, a basis satisfying the relations (6), one must have [\tilde{x}_{p},\tilde{x}_{q}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{pq}\tilde{x}_{\tilde{\delta}(p,q)}\neq 0 for at least one pair (p,q)\in\{1,2,3\}^{\times 2} with p\neq q . Hence, one must have c_{3}^{(\ell)}=0 for at least one \ell\in\{1,2,3\} and to ensure linear independence also c_{3}^{(q)}\neq 0 for some q\in\{1,2,3\} . Without loss of generality, let us assume that c_{3}^{(1)}=0 and c_{3}^{(2)}\neq 0 .




We can now consider the Lie bracket [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] . Here, one has:




 \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=-c_{3}^{(2)}\left(\alpha c_{2}^{(1)}x_{1}+\left(c_{1}^{(1)}+c_{2}^{(1)}\right)x_{2}\right). 



Suppose this Lie bracket vanishes. This would imply that c_{2}^{(1)}=0=c_{1}^{(1)}+c_{2}^{(1)} and consequently c_{1}^{(1)}=0 . However, this in turn would imply \tilde{x}_{1}=0 , which is a contradiction to the requirement that \{\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}\} spans the three-dimensional Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} . Therefore, the commutator [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] must be proportional to either \tilde{x}_{1} or \tilde{x}_{3} , since [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{2} with \tilde{\alpha}_{12}\neq 0 is clearly prohibited by the condition c_{3}^{(2)}\neq 0 . We consider the remaining two cases:

(i) 



 [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{1} with \tilde{\alpha}_{12}\neq 0 . Here, we need to solve the following equations:




 \displaystyle c_{1}^{(1)} 
 \displaystyle=-\frac{c_{3}^{(2)}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{12}}\alpha\,c_{2}^{(1)},\; 
 \displaystyle\;c_{2}^{(1)} 
 \displaystyle=-\frac{c_{3}^{(2)}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{12}}\left(c_{1}^{(1)}+c_{2}^{(1)}\right). 



Solving for c_{2}^{(1)} , we find:




 \displaystyle c_{2}^{(1)}\left(1+\frac{c_{3}^{(2)}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{12}}\left(1-\alpha\frac{c_{3}^{(2)}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{12}}\right)\right)=0. 



If c_{2}^{(1)}=0 , then \tilde{x}_{1}=0 , which is not allowed. Thus, the bracket [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] can only be proportional to \tilde{x}_{1} , if the following quadratic equation has a real solution:




 \displaystyle 0 
 \displaystyle=\left(\frac{c_{3}^{(2)}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{12}}\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{\alpha}\frac{c_{3}^{(2)}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{12}}-\frac{1}{\alpha}=\left(\frac{c_{3}^{(2)}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{12}}-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\right)^{2}-\frac{1+4\alpha}{4\alpha^{2}} 



However, for \alpha<-1/4 , the discriminant is strictly negative, so there is no real solution [59]. One can therefore not have [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{1} with \tilde{\alpha}_{12}\neq 0 .

(ii) 



 [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{3} with \tilde{\alpha}_{12}\neq 0 . This is only possible if




 \displaystyle c_{1}^{(3)} 
 \displaystyle=-\frac{c_{3}^{(2)}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{12}}\alpha c_{2}^{(1)},\; 
 \displaystyle\;c_{2}^{(3)} 
 \displaystyle=-\frac{c_{3}^{(2)}}{\tilde{\alpha}_{12}}\left(c_{1}^{(1)}+c_{2}^{(1)}\right),\; 
 \displaystyle\;c_{3}^{(3)} 
 \displaystyle=0. 





We must therefore have case (ii) and consequently [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}]=0 . Suppose now [\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}]=0 . In this case, the structure constants would force L_{\alpha}^{3} to be isomorphic to the real Lie algebra L_{0}^{4} , which is not possible [63]. Thus, one needs to consider the three remaining possibilities for the bracket [\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}] :

(a) 



 [\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{23}\tilde{x}_{1} with \tilde{\alpha}_{23}\neq 0 . This implies




 \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{1}] 
 \displaystyle=-\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{3},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\tilde{x}_{2},-\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=-\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{\alpha}_{23}\tilde{x}_{1},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\tilde{x}_{1},-\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=0. 



One can therefore construct a Lie-algebra isomorphism mapping: \phi(\tilde{x}_{1})=e_{1} , \phi(\tilde{x}_{2})=e_{3} , and \phi(\tilde{x}_{3})=-e_{2}/\tilde{\alpha}_{12} . Thus L_{\alpha}^{3} would be isomorphic to L_{-\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{\alpha}_{23}}^{4} , which is not possible, since L_{\alpha}^{3} and L_{\beta}^{4} are never isomorphic for any \alpha,\beta , see [63].

(b) 



 [\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{23}\tilde{x}_{2} with \tilde{\alpha}_{23}\neq 0 . This is not possible, since c_{3}^{(2)}\neq 0 , but [\tilde{x}_{p},\tilde{x}_{q}]\in\spn\{x_{1},x_{2}\} for all p,q\in\{1,2,3\} .

(c) 



 [\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{23}\tilde{x}_{3} with \tilde{\alpha}_{23}\neq 0 . This implies:




 \displaystyle\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{\alpha}_{23}}\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{\alpha}_{23}\tilde{x}_{1}\right] 
 \displaystyle=-\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{3},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{\alpha}_{23}}\tilde{x}_{2},-\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{3}\right] 
 \displaystyle=-\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{3},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\left[-\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{3},\tilde{\alpha}_{23}\tilde{x}_{1}\right] 
 \displaystyle=0. 



Thus, one would be able to find an isomorphism from L_{\alpha}^{3} to L_{0}^{3} with the identification \tilde{x}_{2}/\tilde{\alpha}_{23}\leftrightarrow e_{3} , \tilde{\alpha}_{23}\tilde{x}_{1}\leftrightarrow e_{1} , and -\tilde{\alpha}_{12}\tilde{x}_{3}\leftrightarrow e_{2} . However, it is known that L_{\alpha}^{3}\cong L_{\beta}^{3} if and only if \alpha=\beta , see [63]. Thus, we find again a contradiction, since \alpha<-1/4 .



The results obtained above prove that L_{\alpha}^{3} does not admit a basis that satisfies the relations (6) for \alpha<-1/4 .
∎




Corollary.



There exist Lie algebras that are graph-admissible but not minimal-graph-admissible.




Proof.



This statement is a direct consequence of the analysis in the proof of Lemma 92. Recall that for certain values of the parameter, specifically for \alpha<-1/4 , the real Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} does not admit a minimal-graph-admissible basis. However, in some cases, it is still possible to find an overcomplete basis for such an algebra that satisfies the weaker condition of redundant-graph-admissibility given by the relations (7). For example, consider \alpha=-1 . In this case, the Lie algebra L_{-1}^{3} is redundant-graph-admissible with the overcomplete basis \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{1}-x_{2}\} defined by the basis \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\} given in the proof of Lemma 92. This basis allows all nontrivial Lie brackets of these basis elements to be written as proportional to one of these elements, which can be verified by a straightforward calculation. By the proof of Lemma 92, it is furthermore clear that no minimal basis exists with this property. Thus, the existence of such an overcomplete basis demonstrates that L_{-1}^{3} is graph-admissible but not minimal-graph-admissible.
∎




Theorem.



There exist finite-dimensional Lie algebras that are non-graph-admissible.




Proof.



This claim follows directly from the following Lemma 93, which provides a concrete example of finite-dimensional Lie algebras that fail to satisfy the conditions for graph-admissibility.
∎




Lemma 93.



The real Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} (see [63]) is graph-admissible if and only if \alpha>-1/4 or if \arctan(\sqrt{|1+4\alpha|}/(1+2\sqrt{|\alpha|}))/\pi\in\mathbb{Q}^{*} .




Proof.



The Lie algebra L_{\alpha}^{3} is defined as the real three-dimensional vector space spanned by the elements x_{1},x_{2},x_{3} , with the Lie bracket given by:




 \displaystyle[x_{1},x_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{1},x_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=-x_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{2},x_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=-\alpha x_{1}-x_{2}, 

(24)


where \alpha\in\mathbb{R} is a real parameter [63].
Suppose now there exists a finite set \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m} that spans L_{\alpha}^{3} and satisfies the graph-admissibility conditions given by Definition 15 and the relations in (7). Each \tilde{x}_{j}\in\mathcal{B} can be expressed via the original basis elements:




 \displaystyle\tilde{x}_{j}=c_{1}^{(j)}x_{1}+c_{2}^{(j)}x_{2}+c_{3}^{(j)}x_{3}\quad\text{for all }j\in\mathcal{M}:=\{1,\ldots,m\}\supseteq\{1,2,3\}, 



where the c_{k}^{(j)}\in\mathbb{R} are real coefficients. Since \mathcal{B} spans L_{\alpha}^{3} there must exist at least one element \tilde{x}_{j}\in\mathcal{B} such that c_{3}^{(j)}\neq 0 ,
otherwise the algebra would be two-dimensional and abelian since each new element would be expressed as a linear combination of x_{1} and x_{2} , and [x_{1},x_{2}]=0 . Without loss of generality, we assume c_{3}^{(2)}=1 . The Lie bracket between two elements \tilde{x}_{p},\tilde{x}_{q}\in\mathcal{B} can be computed using bilinearity and the structure constants determined by (24):




 \displaystyle[\tilde{x}_{p},\tilde{x}_{q}] 
 \displaystyle=-\alpha\left(c_{2}^{(p)}c_{3}^{(q)}-c_{3}^{(p)}c_{2}^{(q)}\right)x_{1}-\left(c_{1}^{(p)}c_{3}^{(q)}-c_{3}^{(p)}c_{1}^{(q)}+c_{2}^{(p)}c_{3}^{(q)}-c_{3}^{(p)}c_{2}^{(q)}\right)x_{2}, 



Since L_{\alpha}^{3} is non-abelian and the graph-admissibility condition requires that [\tilde{x}_{p},\tilde{x}_{q}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{pq}\tilde{x}_{\delta(p,q)} with \tilde{\alpha}_{pq}\neq 0 for at least one pair (p,q)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} , it follows that there must exist at least one \tilde{x}_{k}\in\mathcal{B} such that c_{3}^{(k)}=0 . Assume, without loss of generality, that c_{3}^{(1)}=0 . For convenience, let us write \tilde{x}_{1}=Ax_{1}+Bx_{2} , where A,B\in\mathbb{R} . The Lie bracket between \tilde{x}_{1} and \tilde{x}_{2} then becomes: [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]=-\alpha Bx_{1}-(A+B)x_{2} . Here, one observes that \tilde{x}_{2} can never be proportional to [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] , since c_{3}^{(2)}=1\neq 0 and the bracket lies entirely in the span of x_{1} and x_{2} . It is, furthermore, easy to show that \tilde{x}_{1} can be proportional (with a non-zero proportionality constant) to [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] if and only if \alpha\geq-1/4 . Let us prove this claim: To determine whether \tilde{x}_{1}\propto[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] , one must solve the system




 \displaystyle\kappa A 
 \displaystyle=-\alpha B,\; 
 \displaystyle\;\kappa B 
 \displaystyle=-(A+B), 

(25)


where \kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{*} is the proportionality constant. Note that at least one of the coefficients A or B must be non-zero; otherwise \tilde{x}_{1} would vanish, which contradicts the assumption that \tilde{x}_{1} is a non-zero element belonging to \mathcal{B} . Observe that if B=0 , the first equation of (25) implies A=B=0 if B=0 , which is prohibited. Hence, one has B\neq 0 . Simple manipulations of equations (25) yield 0=B(\kappa^{2}+\kappa-\alpha) and consequently \kappa^{2}+\kappa-\alpha=0 . This equation admits real solutions if and only if \alpha\geq-1/4 , since the discriminant of the quadratic polynomial is \Delta=1+4\alpha , which is non-negative if and only if \alpha\geq-1/4 [59]. Therefore, the proportionality conditions \tilde{x}_{1}\propto[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] can only be satisfied if \alpha\geq-1/4 . For \alpha<-1/4 , the system has no real solution, and the proportionality fails.




We consider, therefore, now the case \alpha>-1/4 . In this regime, the proportionality condition derived earlier can be formulated as an eigenvalue problem. Specifically, we can examine the linear transformation defined by the adjoint action -\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}(\tilde{x}_{1})=[\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] . Restricted to the space V:=\spn\{x_{1},x_{2}\} , we can represent the problem -\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}(\tilde{x}_{1})=\kappa\tilde{x}_{1} with \kappa\in\mathbb{R}^{*} using the matrix formalism:




 \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&-\alpha\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A\\
B\end{pmatrix} 
 \displaystyle=\kappa\begin{pmatrix}A\\
B\end{pmatrix}. 



The eigenvalues are given by \kappa_{\pm}=(-1\pm\sqrt{1+4\alpha})/2 . Using these eigenvalues, we define the new elements \tilde{x}_{1}:=\kappa_{-}x_{1}+x_{2} , \tilde{x}_{2}:=x_{3} , and \tilde{x}_{3}:=\kappa_{+}x_{1}+x_{2} , which are clearly linearly independent if \alpha>-1/4 , and hence form a valid basis of L_{\alpha}^{3} . Moreover, they satisfy the following Lie bracket relations: [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}]=\kappa_{+}\tilde{x}_{1} , [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{3}]=0 , and [\tilde{x}_{3},\tilde{x}_{2}]=\kappa_{-}\tilde{x}_{3} . This confirms that L_{\alpha}^{3} is minimal-graph-admissible and consequently also graph-admissible for \alpha>-1/4 .




Next, we turn to the case \alpha<-1/4 . As previously established, the Lie bracket [\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}] is, independent of the choice of c_{1}^{(1)} , c_{2}^{(1)} , c_{1}^{(2)} , and c_{2}^{(2)} , non-zero, lies in the subspace V:=\spn\{x_{1},x_{2}\} , and is neither proportional to \tilde{x}_{1} , nor to \tilde{x}_{2} . Therefore, it must be included in \mathcal{B} , up to a non-zero scalar multiple \kappa . Without loss of generality, we may assume this proportionality constant is \kappa=1 , since scalar multiples are irrelevant for satisfying the graph-admissibility condition (7).




To proceed, we introduce the iterated adjoint action: \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{1}):=[\tilde{x}_{2},[\tilde{x}_{2},[\ldots,[\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{1}]\ldots]]] , where the Lie bracket is nested \ell times. This allows us to make the following useful observations:




• 



The element \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{1}) is non-zero and lies entirely in V for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} .




This can be inductively shown: For the base case \ell=0 , one has by convention \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{0}(\tilde{x}_{1}):=A_{0}x_{1}+B_{0}x_{2} , where (A_{0},B_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\} , since \tilde{x}_{1}\neq 0 by assumption. Then \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{1}(\tilde{x}_{1})=A_{1}x_{1}+B_{1}x_{2} , where the coefficients are given by the recurrence relations: A_{1}=\alpha B_{0} and B_{1}=A_{0}+B_{0} . It is straightforward to verify that from (A_{0},B_{0})\neq 0 it follows that (A_{1},B_{1})\neq 0 . Hence, \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{1}(\tilde{x}_{1})\neq 0 . With the base case shown, we can move on to the induction step. Assume the induction hypothesis holds for some \ell\geq 1 , i.e., assume that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{1})=A_{\ell}x_{1}+B_{\ell}x_{2}\neq 0 holds for some \ell\geq 1 . Then, \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell+1}(\tilde{x}_{1})=A_{\ell+1}x_{1}+B_{\ell+1}x_{2} , with the recurrence A_{\ell+1}=\alpha B_{\ell} , and B_{\ell+1}=A_{\ell}+B_{\ell} . By the same argument as before, we have that (A_{\ell+1},B_{\ell+1})\neq 0 since (A_{\ell},B_{\ell})\neq 0 , which shows that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{1})\in V\setminus\{0\} for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} .

• 



The linear map \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}|_{V} is invertible for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . This follows from examining the matrix representation of \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V} .

• 



For all p,q\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , one has [\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p}(\tilde{x}_{1}),\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{q}(\tilde{x}_{1})]=0 . This is a direct consequence of the observation that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{1})\in V for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} and that the subspace is abelian, i.e., [V,V]=\{0\} , since [x_{1},x_{2}]=0 .






Since \mathcal{B} is assumed to be a finite (possibly overcomplete) basis of L_{\alpha}^{3} , all elements \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{1}) must belong to \mathcal{B} , up to an irrelevant non-zero scalar multiple, for all \ell smaller than some particular \ell_{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2} . This follows from the fact that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}(\tilde{x}_{1})\in\mathcal{B} , which implies that its Lie bracket with any other element in \mathcal{B} must either vanish or be proportional to another element in \mathcal{B} , particularly when considering the Lie bracket with \tilde{x}_{1} and \tilde{x}_{2} . From the previous observation that [\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p}(\tilde{x}_{1}),\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{q}(\tilde{x}_{1})]=0 for all p,q\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , and that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{2}(\tilde{x}_{1})=[\tilde{x}_{2},\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}(\tilde{x}_{1})]\in V\setminus\{0\} , it follows that either: (a) \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{2}(\tilde{x}_{1}) is proportional to \tilde{x}_{1} or \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}(\tilde{x}_{1}) ; or (b) \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{2}(\tilde{x}_{1}) belongs to \mathcal{B} . In case (b), the same logic must be applied recursively. However, since \mathcal{B} is finite, this process must eventually terminate, requiring the existence of the aforementioned minimal \ell_{*}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2} , such that the iterated adjoint becomes proportional to a previous one. Thus, there must exist some \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p}(\tilde{x}_{1}) , with p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2} , and some q\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} with q<p , such that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p}(\tilde{x}_{1})\propto\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{q}(\tilde{x}_{1}) . Since, by the observations listed above, \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p}(\tilde{x}_{1})\in V and \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell} is invertible on V for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , one has that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p-q}(\tilde{x}_{1})=[\tilde{x}_{2},\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p-q-1}(\tilde{x}_{1})]\propto\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{0}(\tilde{x}_{1})=\tilde{x}_{1} . One may therefore assume, without loss of generality, that the set \mathcal{B}^{\prime}:=\{\tilde{x}_{2},\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{0}(\tilde{x}_{1}),\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}(\tilde{x}_{1}),\ldots,\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p-q-1}(\tilde{x}_{1})\} is a non-empty subset of \mathcal{B} , where p-q is the smallest integer such that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p-q}(\tilde{x}_{1})\propto\tilde{x}_{1} , as these satisfy the relations (7). By the properties established above: (i) all brackets [\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{j}(\tilde{x}_{1}),\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{k}(\tilde{x}_{1})] vanish, and [\tilde{x}_{2},\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{1})]=\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell+1}(\tilde{x}_{1})\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime} for all \ell\in\{0,\ldots,p-q-2\} , while [\tilde{x}_{2},\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p-q-1}(\tilde{x}_{1})]\propto\tilde{x}_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime} ; (ii) 0\notin\mathcal{B}^{\prime} ; and (iii) For any x,y\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime} , x\propto y if and only if x=y . It is furthermore clear that \mathcal{B}^{\prime} spans L_{\alpha}^{3} , since \tilde{x}_{2} has non-vanishing support in L_{\alpha}^{3}\setminus V and \tilde{x}_{1} and \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}(\tilde{x}_{1}) are linearly independent elements within the two-dimensional subspace V .




One is now left with determining the smallest \ell such that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{1})\propto\tilde{x}_{1} . This is a simple eigenvalue problem, and we start, therefore, by computing the spectrum of the linear map \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V} . The eigenvalues of this map are: \operatorname{Specc}(\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V})=\{(1\pm i\sqrt{|1+4\alpha|})/2\} . Thus, the eigenvalues of \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}|_{V} are given by




 \displaystyle\operatorname{Specc}(\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}|_{V})=\left\{\sqrt{|\alpha|^{\ell}}\exp\left(\pm 2i\arctan\left(\frac{\sqrt{|1+4\alpha|}}{(1+2\sqrt{|\alpha|})}\right)\ell\right)\right\}. 



These eigenvalues are real for some \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{>0} if and only if \arctan(\sqrt{|1+4\alpha|}/(1+2\sqrt{|\alpha|}))/\pi\in\mathbb{Q}^{*} . Conversely, if this rationality condition holds, choose 0\neq v\in V , and let d\geq 2 be minimal such that \operatorname{ad}_{x_{3}}^{d}|_{V}(v)\propto v . Then \{x_{3},v,\operatorname{ad}_{x_{3}}|_{V}(v),\ldots,\operatorname{ad}_{x_{3}}^{d-1}|_{V}(v)\} is a finite overcomplete spanning set satisfying relation (7).




This leaves us with the final case \alpha=-1/4 . In this scenario, the linear map \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}} has only a one-dimensional eigenspace, spanned by \tilde{x}_{1}:=x_{1}-2x_{2} , corresponding to the doubly degenerate eigenvalue 1/2 . In order for \mathcal{B} to span L_{-1/4}^{3} , it must therefore also contain an element \tilde{x}_{3}=c_{1}^{(3)}x_{1}+c_{2}^{(3)}x_{2}+c_{3}^{(3)}x_{3} , which does not belong to \spn\{\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2}\} . We can now, without loss of generality, consider the following two cases:

• 



Case 1: \boldsymbol{c_{3}^{(3)}=1} . Here, we compute: [\tilde{x}_{3},\tilde{x}_{2}]=-(c_{2}^{(2)}-c_{2}^{(3)})x_{1}/4+(c_{1}^{(2)}+c_{2}^{(2)}-c_{1}^{(3)}-c_{2}^{(3)})x_{2} . It is straightforward to verify that [\tilde{x}_{3},\tilde{x}_{2}]\propto\tilde{x}_{1} if and only if the set \{\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}\} is linearly dependent and that [\tilde{x}_{3},\tilde{x}_{2}]=0 if and only if \tilde{x}_{3}\propto\tilde{x}_{2} . The latter case is prohibited by Definition 15, while the former case requires \mathcal{B} to contain a fourth element outside the span of \{\tilde{x}_{1},\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}\} . We may therefore assume that \tilde{x}_{3} is such that [\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}] neither vanishes nor is proportional to \tilde{x}_{1} . \mathcal{B} must contain an element proportional to \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}(\tilde{x}_{3}) . After rescaling and relabelling this element, we obtain an element of \mathcal{B} belonging to V\setminus\spn\{\tilde{x}_{1}\} , which is covered by the next case.

• 



Case 2: \boldsymbol{c_{3}^{(3)}=0} . Here, we have that \tilde{x}_{3}\in V\setminus\spn\{\tilde{x}_{1}\} , and we compute: [\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}]=-c_{2}^{(3)}x_{1}/4+(c_{1}^{(3)}+c_{2}^{(3)})x_{2} . This bracket is non-zero, since its vanishing would imply c_{2}^{(3)}=c_{1}^{(3)}=0 , contradicting \tilde{x}_{3}\neq 0 . Moreover, this bracket is proportional to \tilde{x}_{1} if and only if \tilde{x}_{3}\propto\tilde{x}_{1} , which is excluded by assumption. Thus, the graph-admissibility condition implies that \mathcal{B} contains an element proportional to \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}(\tilde{x}_{3})=[\tilde{x}_{2},\tilde{x}_{3}] . We now need to show that \mathcal{B} contains an element proportional to \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{3}) for every \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} and that these iterates are pairwise non-proportional.




Let us first show the latter. Suppose that there exist distinct p,q\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} such that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p}|_{V}(\tilde{x}_{3})=\lambda\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{q}|_{V}(\tilde{x}_{3}) for some \lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*} . Without loss of generality, we may assume p>q . Let us start by defining the map N:=\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V}-\frac{1}{2}\mathds{1}_{V} . A straightforward computation shows that N^{2}=0 and \ker(N)=\spn\{\tilde{x}_{1}\} . Next, we show by induction that




 \displaystyle\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}|_{V}=2^{-\ell}(\mathds{1}_{V}+2\ell N)=2^{-\ell}\left(2\ell\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V}-(\ell-1)\mathds{1}_{V}\right) 



holds for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . The base case \ell=0 is trivial. Let us therefore proceed to the induction step and assume that the induction hypothesis holds for some \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . Here, it is helpful to observe that N^{2}=0 implies that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{2}|_{V}=\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V}-\frac{1}{4}\mathds{1}_{V} . This allows us to compute:




 \displaystyle\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell+1}|_{V} 
 \displaystyle=2^{-\ell}\left(2\ell\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{2}|_{V}-(\ell-1)\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V}\right)=2^{-\ell}\left((\ell+1)\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V}-\frac{1}{2}\ell\mathds{1}_{V}\right) 




 \displaystyle=2^{-(\ell+1)}\left(2(\ell+1)\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V}-\ell\mathds{1}_{V}\right), 



which completes the induction step and therefore proves the claimed identity. Next, we observe that this implies




 \displaystyle N\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p}|_{V}(\tilde{x}_{3})=2^{-p}N\tilde{x}_{3}=2^{-q}\lambda N\tilde{x}_{3}. 



Since \tilde{x}_{3}\notin\ker(N) , we have \lambda=2^{q-p} . Inserting this into the assumption that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p}|_{V}(\tilde{x}_{3})=\lambda\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{q}|_{V}(\tilde{x}_{3}) , and using the fact that \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V} is invertible, we find that 2^{p-q}\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{p-q}|_{V}(\tilde{x}_{3})=\tilde{x}_{3} . Comparing this with the induction hypothesis applied to \tilde{x}_{3} for \ell=p-q shows that 2(p-q)N\tilde{x}_{3}=0 . Since \tilde{x}_{3}\notin\ker(N) , this implies p=q , contradicting p>q . Consequently, the elements \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{3}) are pairwise non-proportional.




It remains to show that \mathcal{B} contains a proportional representative of every such iterate. We prove this by induction over \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . For \ell=0 , set b_{0}:=\tilde{x}_{3}\in\mathcal{B} . Suppose that there exists b_{\ell}\in\mathcal{B} such that b_{\ell}\propto\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell}(\tilde{x}_{3}) . Since \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}|_{V} is invertible, one has [\tilde{x}_{2},b_{\ell}]\propto\operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell+1}(\tilde{x}_{3})\neq 0 . Graph-admissibility therefore implies that \mathcal{B} contains, for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , an element b_{\ell+1} proportional to \operatorname{ad}_{\tilde{x}_{2}}^{\ell+1}(\tilde{x}_{3}) . Hence, \mathcal{B} contains a proportional representative of every iterate. Since these iterates are pairwise non-proportional, the representatives b_{\ell} are pairwise distinct. Thus, \mathcal{B} would be infinite, contradicting the assumption that it is finite. This shows that L_{-1/4}^{3} is non-graph-admissible. This completes the proof, since




 \displaystyle\left.\frac{1}{\pi}\arctan\left(\frac{\sqrt{|1+4\alpha|}}{1+2\sqrt{|\alpha|}}\right)\right|_{\alpha=-1/4}=0\notin\mathbb{Q}^{*}. 





∎





Example: Graph-admissible Lie algebras from the family L_{\alpha}^{3} 


Example 94.

We examine the family of real Lie algebras L_{\alpha}^{3} for specific values of the parameter \alpha\in\mathbb{R} , to illustrate the distinction between minimal-graph-admissible and redundant-graph-admissible:

(i) 

Case \boldsymbol{\alpha=0} : Here, the bracket relations of the basis elements x_{1} , x_{2} , x_{3} are: [x_{1},x_{2}]=0 , [x_{1},x_{3}]=-x_{2} , and [x_{2},x_{3}]=-x_{2} . This basis satisfies the minimal-graph-admissible condition (6). The corresponding graph is depicted in Figure 24 (a).

(ii) 

Case \boldsymbol{\alpha=1} : Here, one has [x_{1},x_{2}]=0 , [x_{1},x_{3}]=-x_{2} , and [x_{2},x_{3}]=-(x_{1}+x_{2}) . This implies [x_{3},Ax_{1}+Bx_{2}]=Bx_{1}+(A+B)x_{2} , revealing the emergence of the Fibonacci sequence: We can choose A=F_{0}=0 and B=F_{1}=1 and find \operatorname{ad}_{x_{3}}^{k}(x_{2})=F_{k}x_{1}+F_{k+1}x_{2} for all k\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , where F_{k} is the Fibonacci Sequence defined by F_{n+1}=F_{n}+F_{n-1} and the initial values F_{0}=0 and F_{1}=1 [137]. We can now define the golden ratio \varphi as the largest root of the polynomial X^{2}-X-1 and \vartheta as the smaller root [167], and we have \varphi=(1+\sqrt{5})/2 and \vartheta=(1-\sqrt{5})/2 . A basis of L_{1}^{3} satisfying the relations (6) is consequently \{x_{3},x_{1}+\varphi x_{2},x_{1}+\vartheta x_{2}\} , since they are linearly independent and




 \displaystyle[x_{3},x_{1}+\varphi x_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=\varphi(x_{1}+\varphi x_{2}),\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{3},x_{1}+\vartheta x_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=\vartheta(x_{1}+\vartheta x_{2}),\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{1}+\varphi x_{2},x_{1}+\vartheta x_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=0. 


The graph associated to L_{1}^{3} is depicted in Figure 24 (b).

(iii) 

Case \boldsymbol{\alpha=-1} : The real Lie algebra L_{-1}^{3} is, by the proof of Theorem 16, not minimal-graph-admissible. It is, however, by Lemma 93 graph-admissible. This can be seen by considering the overcomplete basis \{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x^{\prime}:=x_{1}-x_{2}\} . This satisfies the relations (7), since:




 \displaystyle[x_{1},x_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{1},x_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=-x_{2},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{2},x_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=x^{\prime}, 



 \displaystyle[x_{1},x^{\prime}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{2},x^{\prime}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x_{3},x^{\prime}] 
 \displaystyle=x_{1}. 


The corresponding graph is depicted in Figure 24 (c).





Figure 24: Depiction of the graphs from Example 94. Graph (a) is associated with the Lie algebra L_{0}^{3} with v_{1}=x_{1} , v_{2}=x_{2} , and v_{3}=x_{3} . Graph (b) is associated with the Lie algebra L_{1}^{3} with v_{1}=x_{1}+\varphi x_{2} , v_{2}=x_{1}+\vartheta x_{2} , and v_{3}=x_{3} . Graph (c) is associated with the Lie algebra L_{-1}^{3} with v_{1}=x_{1} , v_{2}=x_{2} , v_{3}=x_{1}-x_{2} , and v_{4}=x_{3} .






Appendix C Further remarks on proper graphs 




In Section III we investigated the rules for distinguishing valid proper graphs, i.e., labeled directed graphs that can be associated with a given graph-admissible Lie algebra, from improper graphs, i.e., graphs that cannot. We demonstrated that certain graphs can only be associated with a given Lie algebra if edges of the form e=(v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}},v_{\mathrm{e}}) are translated into Lie brackets [v_{\mathrm{s}},v_{\mathrm{l}}]=\kappa v_{\mathrm{e}} with \kappa\in\mathbb{F}^{*} under the conditions that the coefficients \kappa satisfy specific constraints. This makes the corresponding graphs choice-dependent. Importantly, this dependence on the proportionality constants is affected by the characteristic of the underlying field \mathbb{F} , since the Jacobi identity




 \displaystyle 0=[x,[y,z]]+[y,[z,x]]+[z,[x,y]] 



holds also when the right-hand side coincides with pz , where p=\operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F}) and z is any element in \mathfrak{g} .
Consequently, any criterion that differentiates between choice-independent and choice-dependent graphs must account for this subtlety. To illustrate this point consider Example 95 below, which demonstrates how the validity of the Jacobi identity can hinge on the choice of coefficients and the field’s characteristic.




Example: The derived algebra of a solvable Lie algebra


Let \mathfrak{g} be a solvable Lie algebra over a field \mathbb{F} . If \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0 , then its derived algebra \mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g} is nilpotent [112]. However, when \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})\neq 0 , this property generally does not hold [112]. The following example illustrates a solvable yet graph-admissible Lie algebra whose first derived algebra fails to be nilpotent for \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})>0 . This example underscores the interplay between choice-dependent graphs and the selection of coefficients, demonstrating that these choices are inherently linked to the characteristic of the underlying field \mathbb{F} .


Example 95.

Let \mathbb{F} be a field of characteristic p , and let \mathfrak{g} denote the (p+2) -dimensional Lie algebra spanned by the basis elements \{x,y,e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{p} , which satisfy the following bracket relations, see [112] for reference:




 \displaystyle[x,y] 
 \displaystyle=x,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x,e_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=-e_{j+1}\text{ for }j\leq p-1,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[x,e_{p}] 
 \displaystyle=-e_{1},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[y,e_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=-(j-1)e_{j}, 

(26)

while all remaining brackets between basis elements vanish. Then, the Jacobi identity yields:




 \displaystyle[e_{j},[x,y]]+[x,[y,e_{j}]]+[y,[e_{j},x]] 
 \displaystyle=e_{j+1}+(j-1)e_{j+1}-je_{j+1}=0, 

 \displaystyle\text{for }j\leq p-1 

(27a)


 \displaystyle[e_{p},[x,y]]+[x,[y,e_{p}]]+[y,[e_{p},x]] 
 \displaystyle=e_{1}+(p-1)e_{1}=pe_{1}=0, 

 \displaystyle\text{for }j=p. 

(27b)

All other instances of the Jacobi identity clearly vanish identically. Thus, one can construct a graph for this Lie algebra using the basis \mathcal{B}=\{x,y,e_{j}\}_{j=1}^{p} . The corresponding graph is shown in Figure 25. However, if we change the field \mathbb{F} to another field \mathbb{F}^{\prime} with \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F}^{\prime})=q\neq p , the Jacobi identity computed in (27b) would be violated. Consequently, the graph G(V,E) cannot be associated with a Lie algebra spanned by elements in \mathcal{B} which satisfy the bracket relations (26). This failure directly affects the parameter choices for translating edges e\in E into valid Lie bracket relations.


Such a situation can never occur over a field of characteristic zero, since no nonzero multiple of the unit element ever vanishes. This observation emphasizes the choice-dependent nature of the graph representation, which is inherently tied to the characteristic of the underlying field.



Figure 25: Illustration of the graph constructed in Example 95 along with the first derived graph and its lower central series shown for the case p=5 .





This example motivates the following statement:



Corollary 96.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra over a field \mathbb{F} associated with the labeled directed graph G(V,E) . If G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraph induced by a closed directed walk, but the derived graph \mathcal{D}G(V,E) does contain a closed directed walk, then \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})\neq 0 .





From the preceding example, it is furthermore clear that a graph described by the conditions in Corollary 96 is generally choice-dependent.



Proof.



This claim is a direct consequence of Theorems 58 and 66, since Theorem 58 implies that \mathfrak{g} is solvable if G(V,E) contains no self-contained subgraph induced by a closed directed walk. Furthermore, Theorem 66 implies that the derived algebra \mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g} is not nilpotent, since the derived graph \mathcal{D}G(V,E) contains a closed directed walk. However, such a situation is prohibited if \mathfrak{g} is defined over a field \mathbb{F} of characteristic \operatorname{char}(\mathbb{F})=0 [112].
∎





C.1 Summary of the criteria for proper graphs




This subsection summarizes the current status of the criteria obtained in Section III for determining whether a labeled directed graph is proper. Table 2 collects the individual necessary and sufficient conditions, together with their respective scopes and limitations. Table 3 organizes these results according to the Lie-algebra axioms and distinguishes between minimal and redundant graphs, while also indicating the conditions that remain to be established.
