Table 3: Summary of the extent to which the Lie-algebra axioms can be verified from a labeled directed graph in the minimal and redundant settings using the results from Section III, making such graphs proper.



Lie-algebra axiom
Minimal graphs
Redundant graphs

Bilinearity



(Definition 26)
Presupposed by construction and therefore not subject to an additional graphical condition.


Antisymmetry



(addressed by Proposition 32;



partially addressed by Theorem 35, Corollary 38, and Proposition 44)
Proposition 32 provides the necessary local edge conditions for both minimal and redundant graphs: the starting and labeling vertices of an edge must be distinct, every edge must possess its antisymmetric counterpart, and its end vertex must be uniquely determined by its starting and labeling vertices. At the graphical level, these conditions are also sufficient when checking antisymmetry alone, without additionally imposing the Jacobi identity.


Jacobi identity



(partially addressed by Theorem 35, Corollary 38, Proposition 43, and Proposition 44)
Theorem 35 provides a necessary condition by restricting the possible self-contained three-vertex subgraphs of every proper-minimal graph.
No analogous condition is presented in this work. Such a condition could in principle be investigated by similar means, but it would generally be less restrictive because linear dependencies among the vertex elements permit additional cancellations.


Corollary 38 provides a complete necessary-and-sufficient classification for all three-vertex graphs into proper, improper, choice-independent, and choice-dependent graph types.
A corresponding necessary-and-sufficient classification of redundant three-vertex graphs has not been established.


Proposition 43 provides a necessary condition for arbitrary proper-minimal graphs by identifying the possible directed-trail configurations corresponding to cancellations in the Jacobi identity.
An analogous trail criterion has not been established because linear dependencies among the vertices permit additional cancellation patterns that are absent in the minimal setting, and would therefore lead to a less restrictive criterion.


Proposition 44 (i) provides a sufficient condition in the case that \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell)) vanishes for all relevant index triples. Indeed, the Jacobi identity is automatically satisfied when all nested brackets of three distinct basis elements appearing in it vanish.
The sufficient condition \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))=0 from Proposition 44 (i) remains applicable because it forces every nested bracket appearing in the Jacobi identity to vanish individually for all elements of the algebra.


Proposition 44 (ii) provides a necessary condition by restricting the possible non-zero output directions of nested brackets.
No directly analogous condition is obtained in this work because nested brackets terminating at distinct but linearly dependent vertex elements may cancel.

Missing conditions
—
A general necessary graph-based criterion for the Jacobi identity, similar to the one established by Proposition 43 for minimal graphs, is still missing.

Principal difficulty: Linear dependencies among the vertex elements permit additional cancellation patterns that are not encoded by the graph itself.




A general sufficient criterion for the Jacobi identity on graphs with more than three vertices is still missing.


Principal difficulty: An associated graph records only the proportionality directions of the non-zero brackets, whereas the Jacobi identity depends, for choice-dependent graphs, on their actual non-zero proportionality constants.
A general sufficient criterion for the Jacobi identity beyond the special condition in Proposition 44 (i) is still missing.


Principal difficulty: The graph records neither the non-zero proportionality constants nor the linear dependencies among the vertex elements relevant for verifying the Jacobi identity.




A general purely graph-theoretic necessary-and-sufficient criterion incorporating both antisymmetry and the Jacobi identity is still missing.









Appendix D Runtime discussion of the main-text algorithms




In this appendix, we want to derive rough runtime estimates for the main-text algorithms, namely Algorithms 1, 2, and 3. Note that the purpose of this analysis is not to determine optimal or asymptotically tight bounds. Instead, we derive simple and conservative worst-case upper bounds for direct implementations of the pseudocode provided in the main text above. In particular, no attempt is made to optimize the algorithms in concrete implementations.




Let us begin by recalling the asymptotic notation employed for this purpose, and let
 f,g:\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R} be two functions such that g(n)\neq 0 for sufficiently large n . Then, f(n)\in\mathcal{O}(g(n)) if and only if there exists a positive real constant C>0 and a non-negative integer n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} such that |f(n)|\leq C|g(n)| for all n\geq n_{0}  [58]. Similarly, we write f(n)\in\Omega(g(n)) if and only if there exists a positive real constant c>0 and a non-negative integer n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} such that |f(n)|\geq c|g(n)| for all n\geq n_{0}  [58]. Thus, \mathcal{O}(g(n)) and \Omega(g(n)) provide asymptotic upper and lower bounds for f , respectively. If both bounds hold, we write f(n)\in\Theta(g(n)) , or equivalently \Theta(g(n))=\Omega(g(n))\cap\mathcal{O}(g(n))  [58].




For the subsequent runtime discussion, we employ a unit-cost computational model in which elementary operations, such as checking whether a specific vertex belongs to a given vertex set, the removal of an edge or vertex, access to \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) for a given edge, or checking whether a coefficient \alpha_{jk} vanishes, require \mathcal{O}(1) time. Moreover, we assume that an admissible spanning set \mathcal{B}=\{x_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} , satisfying the corresponding admissibility condition from Definition 15, is supplied along with the matrix \alpha_{jk} and the map \delta , as required by the bracket structure [x_{j},x_{k}]=\alpha_{jk}x_{\delta(j,k)} . Thus, the cost of finding a suitable admissible spanning set or of verifying graph-admissibility is not included in the following estimates.




Recall that we employ the convention m:=|\mathcal{M}| , where by the requirements from Definition 15, one has |\mathcal{M}|=|\mathcal{B}| . Similarly, we define for convenience e:=|E| as the number of labeled directed edges in the associated graph. By the same logic employed to show Proposition 33, one has e\leq m(m-1) and hence \mathcal{O}(e)\subseteq\mathcal{O}(m^{2}) .




Before discussing Algorithms 2 and 3, we need to supplement the respective algorithms with a suitable stopping rule. As currently stated, the pseudocode contains no explicit stopping condition and may therefore continue even after reaching the trivial graph or a fixed point. Thus, at the end of every pruning round, the newly obtained graph must be compared with the graph from the preceding round and with the trivial graph. The respective algorithms shall now terminate if the new graph coincides with the preceding graph or is the trivial graph. For this purpose, it is sufficient to store the graphs from the current and preceding pruning rounds. The cost associated with copying and comparing the relevant graphs during the \ell -th pruning round is bounded by \mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V|+|\mathcal{D}^{\ell}E|) and \mathcal{O}(|\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V|+|E_{\mathrm{r}}|+|\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E|) for the Algorithms 2 and 3, respectively.




The pruning algorithms never add vertices to the sets \mathcal{D}^{\ell}V and \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V or edges to the ordinary edge sets \mathcal{D}^{\ell}E and \mathcal{C}^{\ell}E . In Algorithm 3, however, it can occur that an edge may be transferred from \mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}E to the supplementary edge set E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} . By construction, every removal or transfer of an edge occurs only as a consequence of the removal of at least one vertex. Thus, a graph can differ from its successively pruned graph only if at least one vertex is removed. Conversely, if no vertex is removed during a pruning round, the newly obtained graph coincides with the preceding graph, and a fixed point has been reached. Since the initial graph contains m vertices, there are at most m strictly changing rounds. Including the final round in which the trivial graph or a fixed point is detected, the algorithms therefore perform at most m+1 pruning rounds.




We first consider Algorithm 1 for constructing an associated labeled directed graph.



Proposition 97.



The runtime \mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 1},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta) of Algorithm 1 satisfies \mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 1},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta)\in\mathcal{O}(m^{2}) .




Proof.



Algorithm 1 first initializes the vertex set V (cf. line 1) together with an empty edge set E (cf. line 2), which requires at most \mathcal{O}(m) elementary operations. It then considers every tuple of distinct indices (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} with j<k (cf. line 3). There are m(m-1)/2\in\mathcal{O}(m^{2}) such tuples. For each tuple, the algorithm performs the constant-time test whether \alpha_{jk}\neq 0 (cf. line 4). If \alpha_{jk} does not vanish, exactly two directed edges are added to E (cf. lines 5-6). Under the assumed unit-cost model, each such insertion requires \mathcal{O}(1) time. If \alpha_{jk}=0 , the algorithm immediately proceeds with the next tuple. Therefore, the runtime satisfies \mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 1},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta)\in\mathcal{O}(m+m^{2}+e) . The earlier observation that e\in\mathcal{O}(m^{2}) implies consequently \mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 1},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta)\in\mathcal{O}(m^{2}) .
∎





We proceed with Algorithm 2 for constructing the graphs associated with the derived series.



Proposition 98.



The runtime \mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 2},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta) of Algorithm 2 satisfies \mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 2},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta)\in\mathcal{O}(m^{4}) .




Proof.



We begin by defining \mathcal{D}^{\ell}m:=|\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V| and \mathcal{D}^{\ell}e:=|\mathcal{D}^{\ell}E| , i.e., the number of vertices and edges contained in the graph obtained after the \ell -th pruning round. As discussed before, Algorithm 2 performs at most m+1 pruning rounds. Moreover, the sequences \mathcal{D}^{\ell}m and \mathcal{D}^{\ell}e are monotonically decreasing, as, at no stage of the algorithm, are vertices or edges added to the graph during the pruning procedure, only ever removed.




For the following estimate, we observe that each pruning round is explicitly implemented synchronously. That is, the vertices and edges to be removed in the \ell -th pruning round are determined from the graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}G(V,E)=G(\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}V,\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}E) , and the corresponding deletions are applied only after all vertices have been inspected. This ensures that the result of a pruning round does not depend on the order in which the vertices and edges are considered.




The algorithm first constructs the initial associated graph (cf. lines 1-6), as described by Algorithm 1. By Proposition 97, this initialization requires at most \mathcal{O}(m^{2}) elementary operations.




After that the pruning procedure begins, where, at each round, indexed by \ell , one considers every vertex v\in\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}V (cf. line 11). For each such vertex, the algorithm then determines whether there exists an edge a\in\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(a)=v (cf. line 12). This test is performed by scanning the complete edge set \mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}E and therefore requires at most \mathcal{O}(\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}e) elementary operations for each vertex. If no such edge exists, the corresponding vertex v is marked for removal from the set \mathcal{D}^{\ell}V (cf. line 13). Marking or removing the vertex requires \mathcal{O}(1) time. Moreover, all edges b\in\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}E for which at least one of \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(b)=v , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(b)=v , or \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(b)=v holds for a vertex v that is marked for removal must be removed from the edge set \mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}E (cf. line 14-16,18). For a fixed vertex, this requires another complete scan of the edge set and therefore contributes at most \mathcal{O}(\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}e) elementary operations. Finally, copying the resulting graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) and comparing it with the graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}G(V,E) from the pruning round requires at most \mathcal{O}(\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}m+\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}e) additional operations. Thus, combining these contributions yields a total runtime estimate of




 \displaystyle\mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 2},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta)\in\mathcal{O}\left(m^{2}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{m+1}\left(\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}m\left(\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}e+\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}e\right)+\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}m+\mathcal{D}^{\ell-1}e\right)\right). 



As discussed earlier, since \mathcal{D}^{\ell}m\leq m and \mathcal{D}^{\ell}e\leq e\in\mathcal{O}(m^{2}) , one obtains




 \displaystyle\mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 2},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta)\in\mathcal{O}\left(m^{2}+(m+1)\left(m\left(m^{2}+m^{2}\right)+m+m^{2}\right)\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(m^{4}\right), 



as claimed.
∎





Finally, let us consider Algorithm 3 for constructing graphs associated with the lower central series.



Proposition 99.



The runtime \mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 3},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta) of Algorithm 3 satisfies \mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 3},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta)\in\mathcal{O}(m^{4}) .




Proof.



Similar to before, we define \mathcal{C}^{\ell}m:=|\mathcal{C}^{\ell}V| , \mathcal{C}^{\ell}e=|\mathcal{C}^{\ell}E| , and \mathcal{C}^{\ell}r:=|E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)}| . Thus, \mathcal{C}^{\ell}m , \mathcal{C}^{\ell}e , and \mathcal{C}^{\ell}r denote the numbers of vertices from \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V , ordinary edges from \mathcal{C}^{\ell}E , and supplementary edges from E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} , respectively. The runtime analysis of Algorithm 3 is similar to the one of Algorithm 2, except that, here, the pruning steps are different.




The algorithm first constructs the initial associated graph in the same manner as described in Algorithm 1 (cf. lines 1-7). Hence, by Proposition 97, this initialization step requires at most \mathcal{O}(m^{2}) elementary operations. This leaves us with providing a runtime estimate of the subsequent pruning round. As discussed above, the algorithm performs at most m+1 such pruning rounds.




In the \ell -th pruning round, the algorithm first loops over all vertices v\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}V and determines whether there exists an edge a\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}E\cup E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell-1)} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(a)=v (cf. lines 14-15). This requires a complete scan of the two edge sets \mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}E and E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell-1)} in the worst case. Consequently, the test costs at most \mathcal{O}(\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}e+\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}r) . If the deletion condition is satisfied, the corresponding vertex is marked for removal from the set \mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}V (cf. line 16). This, together with the later removal (cf. line 17), contributes at most \mathcal{O}(1) elementary operations per vertex v\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}V . Hence, the complete loop over the vertices contributes at most \mathcal{O}(\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}m(\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}e+\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}r)) .




In the next step, the algorithm performs a loop over every supplementary edge a\in E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell-1)} and checks whether both vertices \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(a) and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(a) belong to \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V (cf. lines 21-22). A single membership test constitutes in the unit-cost model \mathcal{O}(1) elementary operations. Thus, the two membership tests contribute at most \mathcal{O}(2)=\mathcal{O}(1) elementary operations. If either condition fails, the investigated edge is removed from E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} (cf. line 23,31), adding per edge at most \mathcal{O}(1) elementary operations. Thus, this loop adds a cost of \mathcal{O}(\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}r) during the \ell -th pruning round.




Finally, the algorithm loops in each pruning round over all ordinary edges a\in\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}E (cf. line 24). For each such edge, it first checks the membership conditions involving the three vertices \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(a) , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(a) , and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(a) in the newly obtained vertex set \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V (cf. line 25). Each of these membership tests has a cost bounded by \mathcal{O}(1) in the employed unit-cost model. If not all three vertices belong to \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V , the edge is marked for removal from the ordinary edge set \mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}E (cf. line 26,30). The algorithm then checks whether both \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(a) and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(a) remain in the vertex set \mathcal{C}^{\ell}V (cf. line 27). If this is the case, the corresponding edge a is marked for transfer to the supplementary edge set E_{\mathrm{r}}^{(\ell)} (cf. line 28,31). For every ordinary edge, this procedure involves only a constant number of membership tests, at most five, together with at most one deletion and one insertion. It therefore contributes at most \mathcal{O}(\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}e) elementary operations during the \ell -th pruning round.




Finally, copying the resulting graph data and comparing the newly obtained graph data with the data from the previous pruning round requires at most \mathcal{O}(\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}m+\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}e+\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}r) additional elementary operations during the \ell -th pruning round. Combining all contributions, we obtain




 \displaystyle\mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 3},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta)\in\mathcal{O}\left(m^{2}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{m+1}\left(\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}m(\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}e+\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}r)+\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}r+\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}e+\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}m+\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}e+\mathcal{C}^{\ell-1}r\right)\right). 



From construction, it is clear that \mathcal{C}^{\ell}m\leq m . Moreover, the ordinary and supplementary edge sets are disjoint subsets of the original edge set. Hence, \mathcal{C}^{\ell}e,\mathcal{C}^{\ell}r,\mathcal{C}^{\ell}e+\mathcal{C}^{\ell}r\leq e\in\mathcal{O}(m^{2}) . It follows that the runtime of Algorithm 3 is bounded by




 \displaystyle\mathrm{Rt}(\text{Alg. 3},\mathcal{B},\boldsymbol{\alpha},\delta)\in\mathcal{O}\left(m^{2}+(m+1)\left(m\left(m^{2}\right)+m^{2}+m+m^{2}\right)\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(m^{4}\right), 



as claimed.
∎





We want to emphasize that these estimates are deliberately conservative worst-case upper bounds for a direct implementation involving repeated scans of the edge sets. Their purpose is only to demonstrate that the algorithms terminate after a polynomial number of elementary operations in the size of the supplied admissible spanning set; no claim of asymptotic optimality is made.





Appendix E Remaining algorithms




This section is dedicated to the remaining algorithms mentioned in the main text. These algorithms complement the constructions introduced earlier and provide explicit procedures for generating graphs associated with the derived series of Lie algebras. Their inclusion ensures completeness and facilitates practical implementation of the theoretical framework developed in the main text.




E.1 Generating labeled directed graphs associated with the derived series of a given Lie algebra




In Section IV.2 we introduced an approach for associating a labeled directed graph with the \ell -th derived algebra of a given Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} . The procedure starts with the construction of a graph G(V,E) using Algorithm 1 followed by iterative pruning rules that yield a graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) corresponding to \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . These rules remove vertices and edges that fail to satisfy specific structural conditions.




This construction is informed by the recursively defined index sets




 \displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)}:=\delta\left(\left\{(j,k)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\mid\;\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0\right\}\right)\quad\text{for all }\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0},\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(0)}:=\mathcal{M}. 



Consequently, one can directly employ these index sets to obtain a graph associated with any derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} , provided that \mathfrak{g} is graph-admissible and a suitable (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B} satisfying the relations (6) (or (7)) is known. A graph associated with \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} can then be constructed by applying Algorithm 1 to the basis \mathcal{B} when restricted to the elements with indices from \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} . For completeness, this procedure is formalized in Algorithm 5.


Algorithm 5 Algorithm for generating a labeled directed graph that represents the \ell -th derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for a given finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra



Data: A (possibly overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} of the graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , satisfying the Lie bracket relations (7) along with the determining matrix \boldsymbol{\alpha} , the index function \delta , and a non-negative integer \ell .


Result: A labeled directed graph associated with the derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} .



 1
 V \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize an empty vertex set */



 2
 E \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize an empty edge set */



 3
 \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}} \leftarrow \mathcal{M} /* Initialize the index set \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(0)} */



 4
 r \leftarrow 0 /* Initialize a counting index */



 5
 while r<\ell do /* Calculate the set \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)} */


   
 6
 \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}} \leftarrow \delta(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}},\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}})\setminus\{0\} /* Compute \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(r+1)} using the previous set \mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(r)} */


   
 7
 r \leftarrow r+1 




 8
 foreach j\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}} do


   
 9
 V \leftarrow V\cup\{\tilde{x}_{j}\} 




 10
 foreach j,k\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}} with j<k do /* Add all relevant edges to edge set E */


   
 11
 if [\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)} with \tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0 then


      
 12
 E \leftarrow E\cup\{(\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\}\cup\{(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\} 
/* Add edge to edge set E ; each edge is an ordered triple: (start vertex, edge-label, end vertex) */



 13
 return \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(V^{(\ell)},E^{(\ell)})\equiv G(V,E) /* Return labeled directed graph \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} */










It is straightforward to verify that the graphs produced by Algorithm 5 coincide with the graphs obtained via Algorithm 2, provided the former is applied iteratively for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} . This equivalence follows from the observation that




 \displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell+1)} 
 \displaystyle=\delta\left(\left\{(j,k)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\mid\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0\right\}\right)=\delta\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{D}}^{(\ell)}\right)\setminus\{0\}, 



for all \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , since \alpha_{jk}=0 if and only if \delta(j,k)=0 .





E.2 Improved algorithm for generating derived graphs from redundant initial graphs




The application of Algorithm 2, when working with redundant initial graphs, typically yields redundant graphs associated with the derived algebras. However, in certain cases Algorithm 2 may inadvertently remove redundant elements, resulting in a minimal graph. If the goal is to preserve the redundant nature of the representation, an improved procedure is required.




Algorithm 6 addresses this issue by modifying the pruning strategy of Algorithm 2. Specifically, edges are removed at a later stage, after reintroducing vertices that belong to \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} but were previously eliminated. This ensures that the resulting graph remains a “faithful” representation of the derived algebra when redundancy is maintained.
Similar to Algorithm 2, the improved algorithm does not discard edges associated with re-added vertices, as doing so would compromise the structural integrity of the representation. As discussed in Section IV.2 (see remarks following Algorithm 2), this approach guarantees that the resulting graph can be consistently associated with \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} . Nevertheless, it should be remarked that the pruned graphs obtained by Algorithm 6 are not necessarily redundant.


Algorithm 6 Algorithm for generating a directed graph associated with the \ell -th derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} for a given finite-dimensional graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} 



Data: An (overcomplete) basis \mathcal{B}=\{\tilde{x}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{M}} of the graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} , satisfying the Lie bracket relations (7)


Result: A sequence of labeled directed graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with the derived algebras \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} of the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} for all \ell\geq 0 , constructed with respect to the given basis \mathcal{B} .



 1
 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \emptyset 
/* Initialize the set containing all graphs \mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E) associated with each derived algebra \mathcal{D}^{\ell}\mathfrak{g} */



 2
 V \leftarrow \mathcal{B} /* Initialize the vertex set for G(V,E) with the basis \mathcal{B} */



 3
 E \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize the edge set for G(V,E) */



 4
 foreach (j,k)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M} with j<k do /* Add all relevant edges to edge set E */


   
 5
 if [\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k}]=\tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)} with \tilde{\alpha}_{jk}\neq 0 then


      
 6
 E \leftarrow E\cup\{(\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\}\cup\{(\tilde{x}_{k},\tilde{x}_{j},\tilde{x}_{\delta(j,k)})\} 
/* Add edge to edge set E ; each edge is an ordered triple: (start vertex, edge-label, end vertex) */



 7
 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \{G(V,E)\} /* Add initial graph associated with \mathfrak{g}=\mathcal{D}^{0}\mathfrak{g} to \mathcal{D} */



 8
 foreach \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1} do


   
 9
 V_{\mathrm{old}} \leftarrow V 


   
 10
 E_{\mathrm{old}} \leftarrow E 


   
 11
 V_{\mathrm{r}} \leftarrow \emptyset /* Initialize candidate vertices to be removed */


   
 12
 foreach vertex v\in V_{\mathrm{old}} do


      
 13
 if there exists no edge e\in E_{\mathrm{old}} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v then


         
 14
 V_{\mathrm{r}} \leftarrow V_{\mathrm{r}}\cup\{v\} /* Mark v as a candidate for removal */


   
 15
 V_{\mathrm{D}} \leftarrow V_{\mathrm{old}}\setminus V_{\mathrm{r}} /* Vertices occurring as endpoints of edges */


   
 16
 V \leftarrow V_{\mathrm{D}} 


   
 17
 foreach vertex v\in V_{\mathrm{r}} do


      
 18
 if v\in\spn\{V\} then


         
 19
 V \leftarrow V\cup\{v\} /* Retain redundant vertices belonging to the derived algebra */


   
 20
 foreach vertex v\in V_{\mathrm{old}}\setminus V do


      
 21
 foreach edge e\in E_{\mathrm{old}} do


         
 22
 if v\in e then /* edge is of the form (\cdot,\cdot,v) or (\cdot,v,\cdot) or (v,\cdot,\cdot) */


            
 23
 E \leftarrow E\setminus\{e\} /* Remove e from E */



   
 24
 V^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathcal{D}^{\ell}V \leftarrow V 


   
 25
 E^{(\ell)}\equiv\mathcal{D}^{\ell}E \leftarrow E 


   
 26
 \mathcal{D} \leftarrow \mathcal{D}\cup\{\mathcal{D}^{\ell}G(V,E)=G(V^{(\ell}),E^{(\ell)})\} 



 27
 return \mathcal{D} 












Appendix F Realizing the Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} within the Weyl algebra A_{m} 




It has been shown that the complex Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{1} admits a faithful realization within the complex Weyl algebra A_{1} , see [159]. Furthermore, it has been demonstrated that the real Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{1} can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{1} , see [99]. In this section, we aim to prove that the real Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{m} , and, consequently, that the complex Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} admits a faithful realization within the complex Weyl algebra A_{m} . Finally, we comment on the physical significance of the real Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} in the context of quantum mechanics.




Before constructing the realization, let us recall the most essential definitions. The bosonic creation and annihilation operators, denoted by \hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger} and \hat{a}_{j} , have the following canonical commutation relations77
 7
 
 
 
 Note that these commutation relations [A,B]:=AB-BA define a Lie bracket.:




 \displaystyle[\hat{a}_{j},\hat{a}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{a}_{j},\hat{a}_{k}^{\dagger}] 
 \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{\mathds{1}},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger},\hat{a}_{k}^{\dagger}] 
 \displaystyle=0, 



where \hat{\mathds{1}} is a central element, i.e., an element that commutes with every creation and annihilation operator. These operators span the Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{m}=\langle\{\hat{a}_{j},\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger},\hat{\mathds{1}}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , which is typically considered over either the real or complex numbers. The complex Weyl algebra A_{m} can be regarded as the universal enveloping algebra U(\mathfrak{h}_{m}) of the complex Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{m} , under the restriction that \hat{\mathds{1}} acts as the multiplicative identity. More precisely, if the abstract complex Heisenberg algebra is presented with central generator z , then the complex Weyl algebra is isomorphic to the quotient A_{m}\cong U(\mathfrak{h}_{m,\mathbb{C}})/\langle z-\mathds{1}\rangle  [86]. This algebra is equipped with the hermitian conjugation (\cdot)^{\dagger}:A_{m}\to A_{m} with concrete action (\cdot)^{\dagger}:p\mapsto p^{\dagger} , which is a conjugate-linear involutive anti-automorphism satisfying:




 \displaystyle(\hat{\mathds{1}})^{\dagger} 
 \displaystyle=\hat{\mathds{1}},\; 
 \displaystyle\;(i\hat{\mathds{1}})^{\dagger} 
 \displaystyle=-i\hat{\mathds{1}},\; 
 \displaystyle\;(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\dagger} 
 \displaystyle=\hat{a}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;(\hat{a}_{j})^{\dagger}=\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}, 



and reversing the order of multiplication, i.e., (\hat{p}_{1}\hat{p}_{2})^{\dagger}=\hat{p}_{2}^{\dagger}\hat{p}_{1}^{\dagger} .




The Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt theorem guarantees that the normal-ordered monomials




 \displaystyle\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\right)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}}\right)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}},\qquad\text{with}\qquad\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} 



form a basis of the Weyl algebra A_{m} , see [31]. However, since bosonic dynamics are governed by hermitian Hamiltonians \hat{H}(t) , where the time-evolution operator \hat{U}(t) satisfies the differential equation




 \displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}{t}}\hat{U}(t)=-i\hat{H}(t)\hat{U}(t),\qquad\text{with}\qquad\hat{U}(0)=\hat{\mathds{1}}, 



it is natural to restrict attention to the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{m} . This algebra consists of all skew-hermitian polynomials in A_{m} , i.e., polynomials \hat{p}\in A_{m} satisfying \hat{p}^{\dagger}=-\hat{p} , and forms a real Lie algebra under the commutator bracket. A basis of \hat{A}_{m} can be described by the following elements:




 \displaystyle g_{+}=i\left(\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}}\right)^{\dagger}+\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad g_{-}=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}}\right)^{\dagger}-\prod_{j=1}^{m}(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{\alpha_{j}}\hat{a}_{j}^{\beta_{j}}, 



where the integers \alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} satisfy the following conditions:




• 



 g_{+} term: If there exists an index k\in\{1,\ldots,m\} such that \alpha_{k}<\beta_{k} , then there exists an index j\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\} such that \alpha_{j}>\beta_{j} and \alpha_{\ell}\geq\beta_{\ell} for all \ell\in\{1,\ldots,j-1\} .

• 



 g_{-} term: \alpha_{j}\neq\beta_{j} for at least one index j\in\{1,\ldots,m\} , and if there exists an index k\in\{1,\ldots,m\} such that \alpha_{k}<\beta_{k} , then there exists an index j\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\} such that \alpha_{j}>\beta_{j} and \alpha_{\ell}\geq\beta_{\ell} for all \ell\in\{1,\ldots,j-1\} .



This concludes the repetition of the most essential definitions. More details can be found in the relevant literature [42].
Let us proceed with showing the following claim:



Proposition 100.



The real Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{m} .




Proof.



It has been shown that the following six elements of \hat{A}_{m} 




 \displaystyle\hat{h}_{j} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{2}-(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2}\right),\; 
 \displaystyle\;\hat{x}_{j} 
 \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\left(2i\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\right)+i\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{2}+(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2}\right)\right),\; 
 \displaystyle\;\hat{y}_{j} 
 \displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(2i\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\right)-i\left(\hat{a}_{j}^{2}+(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2}\right)\right), 



 \displaystyle\hat{p}_{j} 
 \displaystyle=\hat{a}_{j}-\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\hat{q}_{j} 
 \displaystyle=i\left(\hat{a}_{j}+\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\right),\; 
 \displaystyle\;\hat{z} 
 \displaystyle=-2i\hat{\mathds{1}}, 



satisfy the commutation relations:




 \displaystyle[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=2\hat{x}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=-2\hat{y}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=\hat{h}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=\hat{q}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{p}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=-\hat{p}_{j}, 



 \displaystyle[\hat{q}_{j},\hat{p}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=\hat{z},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{p}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=\hat{q}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{y}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=\hat{p}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=0, 



while \hat{z} commutes with every other element [99]. This extends naturally to the multi-mode case:




 \displaystyle[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=2\delta_{jk}\hat{x}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{y}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=-2\delta_{jk}\hat{y}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{h}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{q}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{q}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{h}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=-\delta_{jk}\hat{p}_{j}, 



 \displaystyle[\hat{q}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{z},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{q}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{x}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{q}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{y}_{j},\hat{q}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{p}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=0, 



with \hat{z} being a central element. We can now define the three elements:




 \displaystyle\hat{h} 
 \displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{h}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\hat{x} 
 \displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{x}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;\hat{y} 
 \displaystyle:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\hat{y}_{j}. 



Then the commutation relations become:




 \displaystyle[\hat{h},\hat{x}] 
 \displaystyle=2\hat{x},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{h},\hat{y}] 
 \displaystyle=-2\hat{y},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{x},\hat{y}] 
 \displaystyle=\hat{h},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{h},\hat{q}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=\hat{q}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{h},\hat{p}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=-\hat{p}_{j}, 



 \displaystyle[\hat{q}_{j},\hat{p}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=\delta_{jk}\hat{z},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{x},\hat{q}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=0,\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{x},\hat{p}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=\hat{q}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{y},\hat{q}_{j}] 
 \displaystyle=\hat{p}_{j},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[\hat{y},\hat{p}_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=0, 



where \hat{z} belongs to the center. The PBW basis shows that the elements \hat{h},\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{p}_{1},\hat{q}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{m},\hat{q}_{m},\hat{z} are linearly independent over \mathbb{R} : the first three have linearly independent quadratic components, the elements \hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j} have linearly independent linear components, and \hat{z} is a non-zero constant element. Consequently, the Lie homomorphism determined by the preceding commutation relations is injective. Thus, comparing these bracket relations to those found in [174], one concludes




 \langle\{\hat{h},\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m}, 



which shows that the Schrödinger algebra can be faithfully realized within the skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{m} .
∎




Corollary 101.



The complex Schrödinger algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} can be faithfully realized within the Weyl algebra A_{m} .




Proof.



The skew-hermitian Weyl algebra \hat{A}_{m} is a real form of A_{m} . Indeed, every \hat{p}\in A_{m} admits the unique decomposition \hat{p}=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{p}-\hat{p}^{\dagger})+i(-\frac{i}{2}(\hat{p}+\hat{p}^{\dagger})) , where both summands before multiplication by i belong to \hat{A}_{m} . Thus, A_{m}=\hat{A}_{m}\oplus i\hat{A}_{m} as real vector spaces. Complexifying the injective realization from Proposition 100 therefore yields an injective realization




 \displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m}\right)\otimes\mathbb{C}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} 



within A_{m} .
∎





Let us continue by discussing the physical relevance of this result. Bosonic systems, such as modes of the electromagnetic field [163], phononic excitations in crystals [16], or Bose-Einstein condensates in many body physics [61], are typically modeled by a collection of bosonic harmonic oscillators. When analyzing the time evolution of such systems, one often performs a basis change that transforms the Hamiltonian into one describing a set of uncoupled harmonic oscillators [41]. A corresponding time-dependent Hamiltonian \hat{H}(t) for an m -mode bosonic system takes the form:




 \displaystyle\hat{H}(t) 
 \displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\omega_{j}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j}+s_{j}(t)\hat{a}_{j}^{2}+s_{j}^{*}(t)(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2}\right), 

(28)


where \omega_{j}>0 are the oscillator frequencies, and s_{j}(t) complex-valued functions known as single-mode squeezing parameters [100, 80]. This Hamiltonian structure naturally involves quadratic combinations of creation and annihilation operators, i.e., precisely the same building blocks used in the realization of the Schrödinger algebra within the Weyl algebra presented in the proof of Proposition 100. The terms \omega_{j}\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{j} model a single free uncoupled harmonic oscillator, while the terms s_{j}(t)\hat{a}_{j}^{2}+s_{j}^{*}(t)(\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger})^{2} correspond to active operations known as single-mode squeezing operators [80]. These operations are fundamental in continuous-variable quantum information theory and quantum optics [2, 100]. The fact that this Hamiltonian models uncoupled oscillators is evident from the absence of cross-mode interaction terms, i.e., there are no products involving creation and annihilation operators from different modes.




The Hamiltonian (28), multiplied by i , can be expressed in terms of the operators \hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j} , up to an additive term proportional to i\hat{\mathds{1}} (equivalently \hat{z} ), which corresponds to a physically irrelevant global phase factor. This phase does not affect observable quantities and can therefore be omitted in the analysis. The resulting expression is:




 \displaystyle i\hat{H}(t) 
 \displaystyle=-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(2\mathrm{Im}(s_{j}(t))\hat{h}_{j}+\left(2\mathrm{Re}(s_{j}(t))+\omega_{j}\right)\hat{x}_{j}+\left(2\mathrm{Re}(s_{j}(t))-\omega_{j}\right)\hat{y}_{j}\right). 



For simplicity of exposition, we impose the assumptions s_{j}(t)=s_{k}(t)\equiv s(t) and \omega_{j}=\omega_{k}\equiv\omega for all t\in\mathbb{R} and j,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\} . These conditions correspond to a system of identical modes, which significantly reduces the complexity of the problem by introducing the symmetry among the oscillators. However, to broaden the applicability of the analysis, we incorporate displacement operators on each mode, as described in [80], which can all be controlled independently. These operations introduce different mode-dependent shifts and are parameterized by complex displacement coefficients \alpha_{j} for each mode. This displacement operator acts as follows: \hat{\mathds{1}}\mapsto\hat{\mathds{1}} , \hat{a}_{j}\mapsto\hat{a}_{j}+\alpha_{j}\hat{\mathds{1}} , and \hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}\mapsto\hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger}+\alpha_{j}^{*}\hat{\mathds{1}} . The resulting displaced Hamiltonian acquires additional linear terms in the operators \hat{p}_{j} and \hat{q}_{j} (resp. \hat{a}_{j} and \hat{a}_{j}^{\dagger} ) and consequently reads:




 \displaystyle i\hat{H}_{\mathrm{f}}(t) 
 \displaystyle=-2\mathrm{Im}(s(t))\hat{h}-\left(2\mathrm{Re}(s(t))+\omega\right)\hat{x}-\left(2\mathrm{Re}(s(t))-\omega\right)\hat{y}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\lambda_{j}(t)\hat{p}_{j}+\kappa_{j}(t)\hat{q}_{j}\right), 



modulo an irrelevant global phase factor proportional to \hat{z} . The real-valued functions \lambda_{j}(t),\kappa_{j}(t) depend on the displacement parameters \alpha_{j} , the squeezing parameter s(t) , as well as the frequency \omega . Their explicit form can be computed straightforwardly but is omitted here as it is not essential for the present discussion. Following the discussion in Sections V.3 and VI.5, the Lie algebra required to compute the time-evolution of this system via a factorization ansatz is generated by \mathcal{G}=\{\hat{h},\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m} , whose Lie closure satisfies, by the proof of Proposition 100, \langle\mathcal{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}=\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m} , underscoring the importance of the Schrödinger algebra in describing the dynamics of multi-mode bosonic systems.
Moreover, it is clear that the simplifying assumptions s_{j}(t)=s_{k}(t)\equiv s(t) and \omega_{j}=\omega_{k}\equiv\omega for every t\in\mathbb{R} and j,k\in\{1,\ldots,m\} dramatically reduce the dimensionality of the problem by collapsing the system into a symmetric configuration. Without these assumptions, one would need to consider the full Lie algebra \langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , which is substantially larger (i.e., \dim(\langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{})=5m+1 against \dim(\langle\{\hat{h},\hat{x},\hat{y},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{})=2m+4 ) and more complex to handle computationally. However, using the graph-theoretic approach developed in this work it becomes evident that this Lie algebra is isomorphic to




 \displaystyle\langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}\cong\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\right)\ltimes\mathfrak{h}_{m}. 

(29)


Let us explain this in more detail: First, one constructs the graph associated with \langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} using the basis \{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m} . This graph is minimal because the basis is not overcomplete. Next, one recognizes that, according to Lemma 70, Lemma 62 and Algorithm 3, the vertices labeled by the elements \{\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m} span a two-step nilpotent ideal, which is precisely the Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{m} . Furthermore, Corollary 42 implies that the triples \{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}\} span, for every j\in\{1,\ldots,m\} , a simple three-dimensional subalgebra. Employing then the Bianchi classification [30], we know that there exist only two such algebras in the real case, namely \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) and \mathfrak{su}(2) . However, Theorem 4.4 from [114] excludes the case \mathfrak{su}(2)\cong\langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , since these algebras can be realized within \hat{A}_{1} . Moreover, the commutator between two such algebras vanishes, i.e., [\langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{},\langle\{\hat{h}_{k},\hat{x}_{k},\hat{y}_{k}\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}]=\{0\} if j\neq k , which shows that the subalgebra generated by all such triples is a direct sum of m copies of \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) , i.e.,




 \langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}\cong\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}). 



Since the graph is minimal and each algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}}) is a simple and non-solvable Lie algebra, the Heisenberg algebra \mathfrak{h}_{m} forms the radical of the full Lie algebra \langle\{\hat{h}_{j},\hat{x}_{j},\hat{y}_{j},\hat{p}_{j},\hat{q}_{j},\hat{z}\}_{j=1}^{m}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} . This leads to the Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition stated in (29), as the radical does not form the center of the Lie algebra.





Appendix G The Poincaré algebra




The Poincaré group, also referred to as the inhomogeneous Lorentz group [182], comprises all coordinate transformations of Minkowski space that preserve the form of all physical laws [49]. This group is a ten-dimensional and non-abelian Lie group that is generated by the following hermitian operators:

• 



The Hamiltonian operator H ,

• 



The momentum operators P_{1},P_{2},P_{3} ,

• 



The angular momentum operators J_{1},J_{2},J_{3} ,

• 



The three boost operators K_{1},K_{2},K_{3} .



These ten elements span the complexified Poincaré algebra \mathfrak{p}(1,3) [85], which is also denoted by \mathfrak{iso}(1,3) [92]. The commutation relations, and therefore the Lie bracket relations, among these basis elements are




 \displaystyle[J_{j},J_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}J_{\ell},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[J_{j},K_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}K_{\ell},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[K_{j},K_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=-i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}J_{\ell}, 



 \displaystyle[J_{j},P_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}P_{\ell},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[K_{j},P_{k}] 
 \displaystyle=-iH\delta_{jk},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[K_{j},H] 
 \displaystyle=-iP_{j}, 



while all other commutators vanish [182]. The corresponding graph G(V,E) is depicted in Figure 26.


Figure 26: Depiction of the graph G(V,E) associated with the Poincaré algebra \mathfrak{p}(1,3) utilizing the basis \{H,P_{j},J_{j},K_{j}\}_{j=1}^{3} .




When considered over the complex numbers, the subalgebra spanned by the three boost and three angular momentum operators is the Lorentz algebra \mathfrak{so}(3;1)_{\mathbb{C}}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}) (cf. Section V.2). The application of Lemma 70 to the graph G(V,E) implies that the vertices labeled with the three momentum operators P_{1},P_{2},P_{3} and the energy operator H span together an ideal of the Poincaré algebra \mathfrak{p}(1,3) . Furthermore, since the subgraph containing only vertices and edges labeled by these four operators contains no edges, it follows from Lemma 47 that the Lie algebra \langle\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},H\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} is abelian. Moreover, because the graph G(V,E) is minimal and the algebra \mathfrak{so}(3;1) is semisimple, and therefore non-solvable, one has \langle\{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},H\}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}=\operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{p}(1,3)) . Thus, we can conclude that




 \displaystyle\mathfrak{p}(1,3)=(\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}))\ltimes\left(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{4}\mathfrak{a}\right)\cong(\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}))\ltimes\mathbb{C}^{4}, 



where \mathfrak{a} denotes the one-dimensional abelian Lie algebra isomorphic to the complex numbers, i.e., \mathfrak{a}\cong\mathbb{C} .




The Poincaré algebra plays a fundamental role in encoding the Lorentz invariance of inertial systems within the relativistic framework. However, in the non-relativistic limit (i.e., for velocities v that satisfy (v/c)^{2}\ll 1 , where c is the speed of light) this algebra reduces to the Galilei algebra, thus capturing the Galilei invariance of classical mechanical systems. To understand this demand, one needs to reintroduce the relevant physical constants into the bracket relations, rather than working in natural units, where the speed of light is normally set to c\equiv 1 [111]. The correspondingly modified relations read then:




 \displaystyle[J_{j}^{\prime},J_{k}^{\prime}] 
 \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}J_{\ell}^{\prime},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[J_{j}^{\prime},K_{k}^{\prime}] 
 \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}K_{\ell}^{\prime},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[K_{j}^{\prime},K_{k}^{\prime}] 
 \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{c^{2}}i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}J_{\ell}^{\prime}, 



 \displaystyle[J_{j}^{\prime},P_{k}^{\prime}] 
 \displaystyle=i\sum_{\ell=1}^{3}\varepsilon_{jk\ell}P_{\ell}^{\prime},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[K_{j}^{\prime},P_{k}^{\prime}] 
 \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{c^{2}}iH^{\prime}\delta_{jk},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[K_{j}^{\prime},H^{\prime}] 
 \displaystyle=-iP_{j}^{\prime}, 



as discussed in [104], where the real form of this algebra is considered. This real form can be obtained, by multiplying each operator with -i . As shown in [104], taking the classical limit c\to\infty yields the Galilei algebra, which obeys the same bracket relations as above, except that [K_{j}^{\prime},K_{k}^{\prime}]=0=[K_{j}^{\prime},P_{k}^{\prime}] . In this case, the operators P_{j}^{\prime} represent spatial translations, J_{j}^{\prime} represent rotations, K_{j}^{\prime} correspond to Galilean boosts, and H^{\prime} represents time translations. Note that this Galilei algebra is also known as the reduced classical Galilei algebra A\bar{G}_{1}(3) [147]. The corresponding graph can be found in Figure 27.


Figure 27: Depiction of the graph G(V,E) associated with the Galilei algebra utilizing the basis \{H^{\prime},P_{j}^{\prime},J_{j}^{\prime},K_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{3} in the non-relativistic limit c\to\infty .




This graph can again be analyzed using the graph-theoretic framework developed in this work. Corollary 42, applied for the real form, guarantees after complexification that this Galilei algebra contains a simple complex three-dimensional Lie subalgebra spanned by the three rotation operators \{J_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{3} , since \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{R}})\otimes\mathbb{C}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\cong\mathfrak{su}(2)\otimes\mathbb{C}  [99], i.e., \langle\{J_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{3}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}) . Furthermore, the radical of this Galilei algebra, which we denote by \mathfrak{ga}:=\langle\{H^{\prime},P_{j}^{\prime},J_{j}^{\prime},K_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{3}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , is given by \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{ga})=\langle\{H^{\prime},P_{j}^{\prime},K_{j}^{\prime}\}_{j=1}^{3}\rangle_{\mathrm{Lie}}{} , since the corresponding vertices span by Lemma 70 an ideal, which is by Theorem 66 nilpotent and therefore solvable. This ideal is furthermore the maximal solvable ideal of \mathfrak{ga} , since the graph is minimal and the three remaining vertices span a simple Lie algebra. Moreover, by computing the lower central series of the associated graphs, we find that the radical is a two-step nilpotent Lie algebra, where the first derived algebra is three-dimensional. The edge structure of the graph associated with the radical allows also a direct identification of \operatorname{rad}(\mathfrak{ga})=\mathfrak{tn}_{3,7_{A}} , where \mathfrak{tn}_{3,7_{A}} denotes the indecomposable two-step nilpotent Lie algebra labeled by 3,7_{A} in [168]. This identification follows because every edge terminating at a sinkhole either originates from the vertex labeled by H^{\prime} or is itself labeled by H^{\prime} . The Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition theorem allows therefore the conclusion that:




 \displaystyle\mathfrak{ga}\cong\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\ltimes\mathfrak{tn}_{3,7_{A}}. 







Appendix H Further remarks on symmetries




In the main text, particularly in Section V.3, we recognized that some proper labeled directed graphs admit certain symmetries. This appendix shall be dedicated to a more detailed examination of these symmetries and their interplay with the graphical representations of Lie algebras. For clarity and simplicity, we restrict our attention to minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebras and their rotational symmetries.



Definition 102.



Let G(V,E) be a minimal graph associated with an n -dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra. We say that G(V,E) admits a \mathbb{Z}_{n} -symmetry if the following holds: There exists an equidistant distribution of the vertices v\in V on a circle in the Euclidean plane, such that the graphical representation of the graph G(V,E) is invariant under rotation by integer multiples of 360^{\circ}/n , combined with appropriate relabeling of vertices and edges.





Our work leads us to believe that rotational symmetries of minimal graphs reflect underlying algebraic invariances. To illustrate this concept, we provide an example below.




Example: Rotational symmetries of the graph associated with \mathfrak{su}(2) 


Let us examine how this definition works in practice:


Example 103.

Consider the real Lie algebra \mathfrak{su}(2) discussed in Example 27. Here, we can choose the basis \{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\} satisfying the bracket relations:




 \displaystyle[e_{1},e_{2}] 
 \displaystyle=e_{3},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[e_{2},e_{3}] 
 \displaystyle=e_{1},\; 
 \displaystyle\;[e_{3},e_{1}] 
 \displaystyle=e_{2}. 


The corresponding graph consists of three vertices and six edges. A possible representation of this graph in the Euclidean plane, with the vertices equidistantly distributed on a circle, is depicted in Figure 28. To verify that this graph admits a \mathbb{Z}_{3} -symmetry, observe that rotating the Euclidean plane counterclockwise by 120^{\circ} and 240^{\circ} respectively, combined with appropriate permutations of the vertex and edge labels, leaves the illustration invariant. Specifically:

• 

A rotation by 120^{\circ} paired with the permutation \sigma:e_{1}\mapsto e_{3}\mapsto e_{2}\mapsto e_{1} preserves the graph representation.

• 

A rotation by 240^{\circ} paired with the permutation \sigma:e_{1}\mapsto e_{2}\mapsto e_{3}\mapsto e_{1} preserves the graph representation.




Figure 28: Illustration of the graph associated with \mathfrak{su}(2) , which is represented in the Euclidean plane with vertices placed equidistantly on a circle (indicated by the gray dash-dotted line). This configuration highlights the \mathbb{Z}_{3} -symmetry of the graph.






The following propositions formalize some immediate consequences for the symmetry concept introduced above. They highlight the relationship between rotational symmetries of minimal graphs and structural properties of the corresponding Lie algebras.




We begin by noting the following fundamental observations:



Proposition 104.



Let \mathfrak{g} be an n -dimensional abelian Lie algebra. Then every minimal graph G(V,E) associated with \mathfrak{g} admits a \mathbb{Z}_{n} -symmetry.




Proof.



This is immediate, since any minimal graph G(V,E) associated with an abelian Lie algebra contains by Lemma 47 no edges.
∎




Proposition 105.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a complex, non-abelian, n -dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with a minimal graph G(V,E) that admits a \mathbb{Z}_{n} -symmetry. Then \mathfrak{g} is perfect with nilpotent radical.




Proof.



Let now \mathfrak{g} be a non-abelian, n -dimensional, minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the minimal graph G(V,E) that admits a \mathbb{Z}_{n} -symmetry. According to Lemma 47 this implies that E\neq\emptyset and there exists at least one edge that targets a vertex v_{j} . This edge e cannot satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v_{j} , due to Proposition 32. Therefore, by the antisymmetry requirement formalized by Proposition 32, there exists at least one edge e^{\prime} such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) . By the symmetry requirement, this property must be true for all vertices v\in V . Consequently, there exists a closed directed walk W_{v} for every vertex v\in V such that W_{v} visits v at least once. A closer inspection leads us to conclude that these walks must induce self-contained subgraphs. We explain this conclusion in detail below:

(i) 



Claim: There exists a closed directed walk W_{v} for every vertex v\in V such that W_{v} visits v at least once. We have seen that for every vertex v\in V there exists at least one edge e_{v}\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{v})=v . Now assume that there exists a vertex w\in V such that every edge e\in E that satisfies \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=w also obeys \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=w . Since G(V,E) is assumed to admit a \mathbb{Z}_{n} -symmetry, we can therefore rotate the graph by 360^{\circ}/n and must, modulo a permutation of the labels, find the same graph. This implies that every vertex must be of the same form and consequently every edge e\in E satisfies \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . This contradicts Proposition 32, as any edge e^{\prime\prime}\in E must satisfy \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime\prime})\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime\prime}) , and since an edge e^{\prime\prime} belongs to E if and only if the edge e^{\prime\prime\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime\prime}),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime\prime}),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime\prime})) also belongs to E . This edge e^{\prime\prime\prime} satisfies \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime\prime\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime\prime})\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime\prime\prime}) , contrary to the assumption that every edge has identical starting and ending vertices. We can conclude that for every vertex v\in V , there exists an edge e\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . It is now straightforward to construct for every vertex v\in V a closed directed walk W_{v} that visits v at least once: Start at the vertex v and follow one of the edges that satisfies \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . The vertices of the graph G(V,E) are equidistantly distributed on a circle. Thus, one can count the number of vertices between v and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) in a counterclockwise direction. Let us denote this number by N\equiv N(v,\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) , where N(v,w)=0 means that v and w are next neighbors and define for convenience N(v,v):=-\infty for all v\in V . Note furthermore that N(v,w)=n-2-N(w,v) if v\neq w , as then N(v,w)+N(w,v)=n-2 . The rotation-invariance of the graph under rotations by integer multiples of 360^{\circ}/n reveals now that for the vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) there exists an edge e^{\prime}\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) and N(\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}))=N(v,\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) . Thus one can append the corresponding edge e^{\prime} and vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) to the walk W_{v}=(v,e,\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)) and continue this process. Here it is important to recognize that N(v,\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e))>-\infty such that this process must after at most n steps yield a closed directed walk that visits v at least once. This is exemplarily visualized in Figure 29. Note that by Definition 102 one cannot assume that N(v,\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e))=1 holds, as one is only guaranteed that one equidistant distribution of the vertices on a circle exists that satisfies the symmetry constraint, but not that all distributions satisfy the desired symmetry condition.


Figure 29: Illustration of a directed graph with four vertices. The graph is assumed to admit a \mathbb{Z}_{4} -symmetry. For clarity, one instance is shown with only one edge, while the remaining edges are omitted. The graph is assumed to contain an edge that connects two distinct vertices, as illustrated in the first instance. The symmetry condition requires that the graph remains invariant under successive rotations by 90^{\circ} . Thus, the complete graph must include the four edges that are obtained via the depicted rotations. Note that the labels are not displayed, as they are irrelevant for this argument.

(ii) 



Claim: There exists a closed directed walk W_{v} for every vertex v\in V such that W_{v} visits v at least once and induces a self-contained subgraph. We have in the previous argument already shown that, for every vertex v\in V , there exists an edge e\in E satisfying \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v and \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) . We may now write \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e):=v^{(0)} , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e):=v^{(q)} , and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e):=v^{(p)} , where v^{(r)} denotes the vertex obtained from v^{(0)} by r successive rotations by 360^{\circ}/n . Note that we assume that all superscripts are understood modulo n . Since the starting and end vertices are distinct, one has p\not\equiv 0\pmod{n} . The invariance of the graph under the corresponding rotations now implies that, for every r\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 0} , the graph contains an edge e_{r}:=(v^{(rp)},v^{(q+rp)},v^{((r+1)p)}) . Since the vertex set is finite, there exists a smallest positive integer m such that mp\equiv 0\pmod{n} . Consequently,




 \displaystyle W_{v}:=\left(v^{(0)},e_{0},v^{(p)},e_{1},v^{(2p)},\ldots,e_{m-1},v^{(mp)}=v^{(0)}\right) 



is a closed directed walk within G(V,E) that visits the initial vertex v=v^{(0)} .




We now distinguish two cases. If p=q , i.e., if \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e)=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e) , then the labeling vertex of every edge e_{r} coincides with its end vertex, since




 \displaystyle\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{r})=v^{(p+rp)}=v^{((r+1)p)}=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{r}). 



Hence, every labeling vertex of an edge occurring in W_{v} is already visited by W_{v} . Therefore, the graph induced by the closed directed walk W_{v} is self-contained, as desired.




Suppose now that p\neq q . In this case, the closed directed walk constructed above need not yet induce a self-contained subgraph, since some labeling vertices \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{r})=v^{(q+rp)} may not be visited by W_{v} . Assume that this occurs, and choose an edge e^{\prime} from the previously constructed walk W_{v} whose labeling vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime}) is not visited by W_{v} . Since the end vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) is visited by W_{v} , one necessarily has \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})\neq\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) . By Proposition 32, the antisymmetric counterpart e^{\prime\prime}=(\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime}),\varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime}),\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime})) also belongs to E .




Applying the preceding rotational construction to the edge e^{\prime\prime} , we obtain a closed directed walk W_{\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})} that visits the labeling vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime}) . Moreover, because the first edge used in this construction is e^{\prime\prime} , the walk W_{\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})} also visits its end vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) . The vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) already belongs to W_{v} . Since W_{\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})} is closed and visits \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) , we may cyclically reorder its sequence so that it starts and ends at \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) . We can therefore insert W_{\varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime})} into W_{v} at an occurrence of \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime}) . This produces a new closed directed walk W_{v}^{(1)} that visits every vertex visited by W_{v} , together with the previously missing labeling vertex \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e^{\prime}) . The new walk W_{v}^{(1)} may itself contain edges whose labeling vertices are not yet visited. Whenever this happens, we repeat the same construction: choose such an edge, use its antisymmetric counterpart to construct a closed directed walk that visits the missing labeling vertex and passes through the end vertex of the chosen edge, and insert this new closed walk into the previously constructed walk at their common end vertex. See for a visualization of this procedure Figure 30.




At every iteration, at least one vertex that was not previously visited is added to the resulting closed directed walk. Since V is finite, this procedure terminates after finitely many steps. Let \bar{W}_{v} denote the resulting closed directed walk. By construction, every labeling vertex of every edge occurring in \bar{W}_{v} is also visited by \bar{W}_{v} . Hence, the graph induced by \bar{W}_{v} is self-contained. Moreover, the original vertex v remains part of the walk throughout the construction. Thus, for every vertex v\in V , there exists a closed directed walk that visits v at least once and induces a self-contained subgraph.


Figure 30: Illustration of a graph with 12 vertices. The graph contains two closed directed walks: W_{1} , which visits the vertices c,d,f,a , and W_{2} , which visits the vertices c,e,b . However, each walk induces a graph that is not self-contained, because the edges targeting vertex c are labeled by vertices not belonging to the respective walk. By joining these two walks into the blue walk W , which visits the vertices c,e,b,c,d,f,a,c in this sequence, the resulting induced graph contains the same edges but also includes the vertices labeling these edges.



It follows immediately that \mathfrak{g} is perfect [43], i.e., \mathcal{D}\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g} , which can be verified using Algorithm 2 and Lemma 52. Thus, by the Levi-Mal’tsev decomposition theorem \mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{s}\ltimes\mathfrak{r} , where \mathfrak{s} is semisimple and \mathfrak{r} is solvable [130]. Furthermore, by [172], since \mathfrak{g} is perfect, \mathfrak{r} is nilpotent.
∎





Building upon the previous propositions, we now strengthen the results by showing that under certain dimensional constraints, the presence of rotational symmetry in the associated minimal graph implies simplicity of the Lie algebra.



Theorem 106.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a non-abelian complex n -dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with a minimal graph G(V,E) that admits a \mathbb{Z}_{n} symmetry, where n\leq 3 . Then \mathfrak{g} is simple.




Proof.



We denote the graph of \mathfrak{g} with G(V,E) and proceed by considering the cases, where \dim(\mathfrak{g})\in\{1,2,3\} :




• 



One-dimensional case. There does not exist any non-abelian one-dimensional Lie algebra.

• 



Two-dimensional case. The only complex non-abelian two-dimensional Lie algebra is \mathfrak{aff}(\mathbb{C}) . For the associated graph to admit a \mathbb{Z}_{2} symmetry, there would need to exist an edge e\in E that satisfies \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e)=v_{1} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e)=v_{2} , since this algebra is non-abelian and due to Proposition 32. By the symmetry requirement, there must also exist an edge e^{\prime}\in E satisfying \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e^{\prime})=v_{2} and \varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e^{\prime})=v_{1} . This configuration violates the antisymmetry of the Lie bracket. Therefore, no graph associated with \mathfrak{aff}(\mathbb{C}) can admit a \mathbb{Z}_{2} symmetry.

• 



Three-dimensional case. In Figure 3, we classified all minimal graphs with three vertices. A close inspection of these graphs reveals that the only graph admitting a \mathbb{Z}_{3} -symmetry is of Type IX, which is always associated with a simple Lie algebra, as the corresponding Lie algebra is by Theorem 58 non-solvable, and the only non-solvable three-dimensional Lie algebras are by [30] simple.



∎





We present here some final remarks. One might conjecture that there exists no non-abelian five-dimensional Lie algebra that can be associated with a minimal graph that admits a \mathbb{Z}_{5} -symmetry. Furthermore, one might conjecture that the only non-abelian six-dimensional Lie algebra that can be associated with a graph that admits a \mathbb{Z}_{6} -symmetry might, in the complex case, be the algebra \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}}) . While these conjectures are consistent with the classification of semisimple Lie algebras, according to which there exists no semisimple Lie algebra of dimension five, and the only semisimple Lie algebra of dimension six is \mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})\oplus\mathfrak{sl}({2},{\mathbb{C}})  [103], they do not follow from this classification alone, since perfect non-semisimple Lie algebras with nilpotent radicals already exist in dimensions five and six [43].




Moreover, Definition 102 can clearly be generalized to arbitrary symmetry groups. For instance, the graph depicted in Figure 28 is also invariant under three reflections along the bisectors of the triangle formed by the three vertices, combined with appropriate permutations of the vertex and edge labels. Hence, the corresponding graph G(V,E) admits a D_{3} -symmetry, where D_{3} is the dihedral group generated by the aforementioned rotations and reflections [169].




H.1 On the Killing form




In this context, it could be helpful to investigate the role of the Killing form [124]. This is a symmetric bilinear form that arises naturally in the structure theory of Lie algebras and Lie groups [126]. It is commonly defined as B(x,y):=\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{ad}_{x}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{y}) for every pair of elements x,y\in\mathfrak{g} [126]. One important property is that B has an invariance property in the sense that B(\operatorname{ad}_{x}(y),z)=-B(y,\operatorname{ad}_{x}(z)) or equivalently B([x,y],z)=B(x,[y,z]) for all x,y,z\in\mathfrak{g} [126]. The Killing form is of particular interest for Lie algebras over fields of characteristic zero, as in this case, Cartan’s criterion states that B is nondegenerate if and only if the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is semisimple [70].




Consider now a basis \mathcal{B} of a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} satisfying the relation (6), i.e., \operatorname{ad}_{x_{k}}(x_{\ell})=[x_{k},x_{\ell}]=\alpha_{k\ell}x_{\delta(k,\ell)} . Applying now \operatorname{ad}_{x_{j}} to this element yields:




 \operatorname{ad}_{x_{j}}(\operatorname{ad}_{x_{k}}(x_{\ell}))=[x_{j},[x_{k},x_{\ell}]]=\alpha_{k\ell}\alpha_{j,\delta(k,\ell)}x_{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))}. 



The entries of the Killing matrix are given by B_{jk}=\operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{ad}_{x_{j}}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{x_{k}}) . Since the trace of a linear map is the sum of the coefficients of x_{\ell} ’s in the image of the x_{\ell} ’s, only terms with \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))=\ell contribute. Hence:




 B_{jk}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{N}}\alpha_{k\ell}\alpha_{j,\delta(k,\ell)}\delta_{\ell,\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))}, 



where \delta_{\ell,\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))} denotes the Kronecker delta that evaluates to one if \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))=\ell , and zero otherwise. As we stated before, the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} is semisimple if and only if the Killing form is non-degenerate [70]. This motivates the following observation for the entries B_{jk} of the Killing matrix:



Lemma 107.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the minimal graph G(V,E) . Fix j,k\in\mathcal{N} and let v_{j},v_{k}\in V be the corresponding vertices. Then B_{jk}\neq 0 only if there exist v_{\ell},v_{\ell^{\prime}}\in V and two edges e_{1},e_{2}\in E such that \varpi_{\mathrm{s}}(e_{1})=v_{\ell}=\varpi_{\mathrm{e}}(e_{2}) , \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{1})=v_{k} and \varpi_{\mathrm{l}}(e_{2})=v_{j} .





Employing Proposition 32, this trace condition can be illustrated graphically by the subgraph substructure in Figure 31. This structure witnesses a nonzero trace contribution: the trace contribution is nonzero if and only if there exists a vertex v_{\ell} that returns to itself by traversing first an edge labeled by v_{k} and then an edge labeled by v_{j} , i.e., along the two-step pattern visualized in Figure 31.


Figure 31: Illustration of the subgraph that appears in every graph associated with every semisimple Lie algebra for a given \ell\in\mathcal{N} , provided the associated graph is minimal. Note that multiple vertices may coincide, as for example k=j may occur.



Proof.



Let the Lie algebra \mathfrak{g} and the associated graph G(V,E) be as stated in the claim above. By the computation above, the B_{jk} components are determined by the coefficients of the elements \sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{N}}[x_{j},[x_{k},x_{\ell}]]=\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{N}}\alpha_{k\ell}\alpha_{j,\delta(k,\ell)}x_{\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))} . Thus, by the definition of the trace, if B_{jk}\neq 0 , then there exists an index \ell\in\mathcal{N} such that \delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))=\ell , i.e., a vertex v_{\ell} such that [x_{k},x_{\ell}]\propto x_{\ell^{\prime}} and [x_{j},x_{\ell^{\prime}}]\propto x_{\ell} . Under the graph construction rules, these relations are exactly the existence of edges e_{1}=(v_{\ell},v_{k},v_{\ell^{\prime}}) and e_{2}=(v_{\ell^{\prime}},v_{j},v_{\ell}) , i.e., the condition stated in the lemma. Note that the prefactor \alpha_{k\ell}\alpha_{j,\delta(k,\ell)} is by the definition of the matrix \boldsymbol{\alpha} and the index-function \delta , non-zero in this case, as \ell\in\mathcal{N} and \ell\neq 0 . Conversely, if no such \ell exists, then every Kronecker-delta factor vanishes and B_{jk}=0 .
∎





We can now connect this result to Cartan’s criterion for semisimplicity. Indeed, if for some j\in\mathcal{N} one had B_{jk}=0 for all k\in\mathcal{N} , then the j -th row of the Killing matrix would be zero, hence the Killing form would be degenerate. Therefore, if \mathfrak{g} is semisimple, for every j\in\mathcal{N} there must exist k,\ell\in\mathcal{N} such that [x_{j},[x_{k},x_{\ell}]]\propto x_{\ell} . This allows us to provide the following necessary subgraph condition for semisimplicity in minimal graphs:



Proposition 108.



Let \mathfrak{g} be a finite-dimensional minimal-graph-admissible Lie algebra associated with the minimal graph G(V,E) . If \mathfrak{g} is semisimple, then, for every vertex v_{j}\in V , there exist vertices v_{k},v_{\ell} such that the Figure 31 structure occurs in G(V,E) .




Proof.



By Cartan’s criterion, \mathfrak{g} is semisimple if and only if the Killing form B is nondegenerate. This implies that the Killing matrix is nondegenerate and thus contains no zero row. If one now fixes an index j\in\mathcal{N} , one cannot have that B_{jk}=0 for all k\in\mathcal{N} . Hence, there must exist an index k\in\mathcal{N} such that B_{jk}\neq 0 . By Lemma 107, the Figure 31 subgraph structure must occur for (v_{j},v_{k}) , completing the proof.
∎





We can deduce the following practical rule following Lemma 107: Fix j,k\in\mathcal{N} and define the set




 \mathcal{K}_{jk}:=\{\ell\in\mathcal{N}\;\mid\,\delta(j,\delta(k,\ell))=\ell\}. 



Then \mathcal{K}_{jk}=\emptyset (and hence B_{jk}=0 ) if there is no Figure 31 subgraph structure compatible with the vertices v_{j} and v_{k} .




This subgraph structure provides a necessary local constraint on minimal graphs of semisimple Lie algebras and allows one to determine a possible support of the Killing matrix (i.e., the set of indices (j,k)\in\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N} where B_{jk}\neq 0 is possible) efficiently. However, nondegeneracy is a global property: even if no row is identically zero, the determinant may vanish due to cancellations among different contributions.




To illustrate the comment on the support of the Killing matrix, consider
the graph depicted in Figure 28. This corresponds to a strictly diagonal Killing matrix, as for j\neq k , the graph lacks any x_{\ell} that returns to itself under \operatorname{ad}_{x_{j}}\circ\operatorname{ad}_{x_{k}} , hence B_{jk}=0 by Lemma 107. If, on the other hand, j=k , the subgraph structure from Figure 31 exists within the graph, showing that the Killing form has possibly nonzero diagonal elements.
